arXiv:2509.15390v1 [math.SG] 18 Sep 2025
Packing stability and the subleading asymptotics of symplectic Weyl laws
Oliver Edtmair
Abstract.
We prove that symplectic ball packing stability holds for every compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold with smooth boundary. This follows from a stronger result: the full volume of any such manifold can be filled by a single symplectic ellipsoid. As an application, we obtain estimates—with sharp exponents—for the error terms in the symplectic Weyl laws for embedded contact homology capacities, periodic Floer homology spectral invariants, and link spectral invariants. We also construct an example of a star-shaped domain in ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}, arbitrarily C1C^{1} close to the unit ball and with boundary of regularity just below C2C^{2} and smooth away from a single point, for which packing stability fails. Our proofs reveal a close connection between symplectic packing stability in the presence of smooth boundary and the algebraic structure of Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups, particularly Banyaga’s simplicity results.

1. Introduction
1.1. Ball packing stability
Consider a connected symplectic manifold (M2​n,ω)(M^{2n},\omega) of finite volume, which may be open or have boundary. For k≥1k\geq 1, the kk-th ball packing number of MM, denoted by pk​(M)p_{k}(M), quantifies how much of the volume of MM can be covered by kk disjoint, symplectically embedded balls of equal size. More precisely, the numbers pk​(M)p_{k}(M) are defined by
pk​(M)≔supak⋅vol⁡(B⁡(a))vol⁡(M)∈[0,1],p_{k}(M)\coloneqq\sup_{a}\frac{k\cdot\operatorname{vol}(B(a))}{\operatorname{vol}(M)}\in[0,1],
where the supremum is taken over all a>0a>0 such that the disjoint union of kk copies of the closed 2​n2n-dimensional ball B​(a)=B2​n​(a)B(a)=B^{2n}(a) of symplectic width aa admits a symplectic embedding into the interior of MM. The ball packing numbers serve as a fundamental quantitative measure of symplectic rigidity. Since Gromov’s seminal work [Gro85], which revealed the first non-trivial obstructions to symplectic embeddings, these invariants have attracted considerable attention. We refer to [Sch, Hut11b, CHLS07] for surveys.
In [MP94, Remark 1.5.G], McDuff and Polterovich observed that limk→∞pk​(M)=1\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}p_{k}(M)=1 for every symplectic manifold of finite volume. This implies that symplectic ball packings become asymptotically flexible as the number of balls increases. In [Bir97, Bir99], Biran proved a much stronger and deeper flexibility result for closed, connected symplectic 44-manifolds (M4,ω)(M^{4},\omega) which are rational, meaning that the symplectic form ω\omega represents a rational cohomology class [ω]∈H2​(M,ℚ)[\omega]\in H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Q}). He showed that there exists a finite threshold k0k_{0}, depending on MM, such that pk​(M)=1p_{k}(M)=1 for all k≥k0k\geq k_{0}. This phenomenon is referred to as symplectic ball packing stability.
A natural problem is to determine which connected symplectic manifolds of finite volume, beyond closed, rational symplectic 44-manifolds, exhibit packing stability. This question is explicitly raised in the survey article [CHLS07, §3.2, Problem 4] by Cieliebak, Latschev, Hofer, and Schlenk, as well as in [Sch, Conjecture 13.2] by Schlenk. Several results have been established in this direction. It was proved by Buse and Hind [BH11, BH13] that packing stability holds for closed, rational symplectic manifolds of arbitrary dimension. In dimension four, Buse, Hind, and Opshtein [BHO16] eliminated the rationality assumption, showing that packing stability holds for all closed, connected symplectic 44-manifolds. In higher dimensions, however, the general case remains open. Moreover, packing stability has been verified for certain open manifolds with highly symmetric boundary, including balls and ellipsoids [BH13], 44-dimensional polydisks and pseudoballs [BHO16], and 44-dimensional rational convex toric domains [CHMP25].
In contrast to the positive results described above, Cristofaro-Gardiner and Hind [CHa] recently identified the first examples of open symplectic manifolds of finite volume for which packing stability fails. They constructed open domains U⊂ℝ4U\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}, diffeomorphic to the open 44-ball, such that pk​(U)<1p_{k}(U)<1 for all k≥1k\geq 1. The failure of packing stability in these examples is connected to the domains’ “wild” boundary behaviour; see Subsection 1.3 below for further discussion. This leaves a significant gap between these counterexamples and the known cases of packing stability, i.e. closed manifolds and special toric domains. It is an intriguing problem to more precisely identify and understand the transition between packing stability and its failure.
An important question is whether packing stability holds for compact symplectic manifolds with smooth boundary; see [CHa, Question 7.1]. The known cases involving toric boundary provide limited insight into what one might expect in the general setting. For example, the symplectic 44-ball B4​(1)B^{4}(1) is symplectomorphic to the complement of a line in ℂ​P2\mathbb{C}P^{2}, equipped with a suitably scaled Fubini–Study symplectic form. More generally, any 44-dimensional convex toric domain can be approximated by toric domains which are symplectomorphic to the complements of nodal divisors in closed symplectic 44-manifolds; see [Cri19]. In other words, the known instances of packing stability in the presence of boundary essentially reduce to the case of nodal divisor complements and thus do not substantially extend beyond the closed case. In particular, no examples are known of packing stability for symplectic manifolds whose boundary carries a characteristic foliation with chaotic behaviour. In fact, general symplectic embedding results for manifolds with dynamically rich boundaries are rare; see [Edt24] for an exception.
A major motivation for studying ball packings in symplectic manifolds with general smooth boundary arises from a close connection to the asymptotic behaviour of certain sequences of symplectic spectral invariants; see Subsection 1.4 below. These invariants encode important dynamical information, making manifolds with boundary—where the dynamics occurs—central to our investigation. Our first main result establishes that packing stability holds for all compact, connected symplectic 44-manifolds with smooth boundary.
Theorem A (Ball packing stability).
Let (M4,ω)(M^{4},\omega) be a compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold, possibly with smooth boundary. Then symplectic ball packing stability holds for MM. This means that there exists a positive integer k0k_{0} such that pk​(M)=1p_{k}(M)=1 for all k≥k0k\geq k_{0}.
We note that in Theorem A we only assume that the boundary is smooth and in particular do not impose any contact type assumptions.
1.2. Ellipsoid embedding stability
Recall that the symplectic ellipsoid E⁡(a1,…,an)E(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}) with widths ai>0a_{i}>0 is defined by
E⁡(a1,…,an)≔{z∈ℂn|∑jπ​|zj|2aj≤1},E(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\kern 5.0pt\Big|\kern 5.0pt\sum_{j}\frac{\pi|z_{j}|^{2}}{a_{j}}\leq 1\right\},
where ℝ2​n≅ℂn\mathbb{R}^{2n}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n} is equipped with the standard symplectic form ω0=∑id​xi∧d​yi\omega_{0}=\sum_{i}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}. Let (M2​n,ω)(M^{2n},\omega) be a connected symplectic manifold of finite volume. For every real number a≥1a\geq 1, the ellipsoid embedding number paE​(M)p_{a}^{E}(M) quantifies how much of the volume of MM can be filled by a single symplectic ellipsoid obtained from E⁡(1,…,1,a)E(1,\dots,1,a) by scaling. It is defined by
paE​(M)≔supλvol⁡(E⁡(λ,…,λ,λ​a))vol⁡(M)∈[0,1],p_{a}^{E}(M)\coloneqq\sup_{\lambda}\frac{\operatorname{vol}(E(\lambda,\dots,\lambda,\lambda a))}{\operatorname{vol}(M)}\in[0,1],
where the supremum is taken over all positive numbers λ>0\lambda>0 such that the ellipsoid E⁡(λ,…,λ,λ​a)E(\lambda,\dots,\lambda,\lambda a) admits a symplectic embedding into the interior of MM; see [Sch05b, §1.3.2]. Since the ellipsoid E⁡(a,…,a)E(a,\dots,a) is simply the ball B⁡(a)B(a), we have p1E​(M)=p1​(M)p_{1}^{E}(M)=p_{1}(M). It is well known that, for every positive integer k≥1k\geq 1, the ellipsoid E⁡(1,…,1,k)E(1,\dots,1,k) can be fully packed by kk balls of equal size. This implies that pkE​(M)≤pk​(M)p_{k}^{E}(M)\leq p_{k}(M) for all k≥1k\geq 1.
Schlenk proved in [Sch05b, §1.3.2, Theorem 3] that, for every connected symplectic manifold MM of finite volume, we have lima→∞paE​(M)=1\lim_{a\rightarrow\infty}p_{a}^{E}(M)=1. In other words, symplectic embeddings of ellipsoids into MM become asymptotically flexible as the ellipsoids become increasingly skinny. We say that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for MM if there exists a finite threshold a0a_{0} such that paE​(M)=1p_{a}^{E}(M)=1 for all a≥a0a\geq a_{0}.
Since ball packing stability holds for every ellipsoid, the condition paE​(M)=1p_{a}^{E}(M)=1 for even a single value of aa implies ball packing stability. In particular, ellipsoid embedding stability implies ball packing stability. Moreover, it was shown in [BH13, Theorem 1.1] that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for every ellipsoid, so knowing that paE​(M)=1p_{a}^{E}(M)=1 for one value of aa also implies ellipsoid embedding stability.
It follows from [Bir01] and [Ops13]—see also [BH13, Theorem 1.3]—that every closed, connected, rational symplectic manifold MM can be fully filled by one single ellipsoid; that is paE​(M)=1p_{a}^{E}(M)=1 for some a≥1a\geq 1. This means that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for such manifolds. While ball packing stability is known to hold for all closed symplectic 44-manifolds [BHO16], the corresponding statement for ellipsoid embedding stability is open; see [BHO16, Conjecture 1] and [Sch, Conjecture 13.8].
We prove that our ball packing stability result (Theorem A) can, in fact, be strengthened to ellipsoid embedding stability. Although this result is new even in the closed case, the primary case of interest concerns compact symplectic manifolds whose boundary exhibits rich dynamical behaviour.
Theorem B (Ellipsoid embedding stability).
Let (M4,ω)(M^{4},\omega) be a compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold, possibly with smooth boundary. Then symplectic ellipsoid embedding stability holds for MM. This means that there exists a number a0a_{0} such that paE​(M)=1p_{a}^{E}(M)=1 for all a≥a0a\geq a_{0}.
Remark 1.1.
Let (M4,ω)(M^{4},\omega) be a compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold with smooth boundary. Let a0a_{0} denote the threshold from Theorem B. Fix a>a0a>a_{0} and consider the symplectic ellipsoid E=E⁡(r,r​a)E=E(r,ra) where the parameter r>0r>0 is chosen so that vol⁡(M)=vol⁡(E)\operatorname{vol}(M)=\operatorname{vol}(E). This means that the closed symplectic ellipsoid λ​E\lambda E symplectically embeds into the interior of MM for all λ∈(0,1)\lambda\in(0,1). This naturally raises the question of whether the interior of EE itself admits a symplectic embedding into the interior of MM. In light of [PV15, Theorem 4.4], this would follow if one could arrange the symplectic embeddings of λ​E\lambda E into the interior of MM to depend smoothly on λ\lambda as λ\lambda approaches 11. It is conceivable that, with additional care, such a smooth dependence can be achieved using the symplectic embedding constructions developed in this paper. However, we will not pursue this direction further here and leave it for future work.
In the literature, the ellipsoid embedding numbers paE​(M)p_{a}^{E}(M) of a symplectic 44-manifold (M,ω)(M,\omega) are often encoded in terms of the ellipsoid embedding function cM:[0,∞)→ℝc_{M}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}; see, for example, [CHMP25]. This function is defined by
cM​(a)≔inf{r∣E⁡(1,a)​↪𝑠​(M,r​ω)},c_{M}(a)\coloneqq\inf\left\{r\mid E(1,a)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}(M,r\omega)\right\},
where X​↪𝑠​YX\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}Y denotes the existence of a symplectic embedding from XX into YY. The function cM​(a)c_{M}(a) encodes the same information as the ellipsoid embedding numbers paE​(M)p_{a}^{E}(M).
The ellipsoid embedding function cMc_{M} is notoriously difficult to compute. In a landmark paper, McDuff and Schlenk [MS12] succeeded in computing the ellipsoid embedding function cB⁡(1)c_{B(1)} of the 44-dimensional ball—arguably the simplest nontrivial target manifold. Their result is an intricate yet explicit function featuring the so-called infinite Fibonacci staircase. A further key feature of cB⁡(1)c_{B(1)} is that for sufficiently large aa, the function stabilizes to the volume curve:
cB⁡(1)​(a)=a2​vol⁡(B⁡(1))=a.c_{B(1)}(a)=\sqrt{\frac{a}{2\operatorname{vol}(B(1))}}=\sqrt{a}.
This stabilization is equivalent to the assertion that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for B⁡(1)B(1).
For a general compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold MM with smooth boundary, fully computing the function cMc_{M} appears entirely out of reach. Nevertheless, Theorem B shows that cMc_{M} always stabilizes to the volume curve.
1.3. Limits of packing stability
As mentioned above, Cristofaro-Gardiner and Hind [CHa] constructed the first examples of symplectic manifolds of finite volume that do not exhibit ball packing stability. For a discussion of the failure of packing stability in the context of packings by certain generalized convex toric domains, rather than by balls, we refer the reader to [CMM].
The examples in [CHa] are unbounded concave toric domains of finite volume. More precisely, let f:[0,∞)→(0,1]f:[0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,1] be a convex function such that ∫0∞f⁡(x)​𝑑x<∞\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)dx<\infty. Define Ω⊂ℝ2\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} to be the region in the first quadrant lying below the graph of ff, and set
(1.1)
Xf≔{(z1,z2)∈ℂ2∣π⁡(|z1|2,|z2|2)∈Ω}⊂ℂ2.X_{f}\coloneqq\left\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid\pi(|z_{1}|^{2},|z_{2}|^{2})\in\Omega\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}.
This domain has finite volume, and it is shown in [CHa] that packing stability fails for XfX_{f}.
Moreover, for suitable choices of ff, [CHa] proves that one can symplectically fold the infinite “tail” of XfX_{f} into a bounded region of ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}, thereby producing an open, bounded domain UU that is diffeomorphic to the open ball and symplectomorphic to the interior of XfX_{f}. Packing stability must then also fail for UU. However, the folding construction yields “wild” boundary behaviour: in particular ∂U\partial U is not a topological submanifold of ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}. To more precisely locate the transition between packing stability and its failure, it is natural to seek counterexamples with more regular boundary. This is the content of the following result.
Theorem C (C2−εC^{2-\varepsilon} failure of packing stability).
Arbitrarily C1C^{1} close to the unit ball in ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}, there exists a star-shaped domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with the following properties:
(1)
The boundary of XX is in the regularity class ⋂α∈(0,1)C1,α\bigcap_{\alpha\in(0,1)}C^{1,\alpha}, but not in C1,1C^{1,1}. Here C1,αC^{1,\alpha} denotes the Hölder space of differentiable functions with C0,αC^{0,\alpha} Hölder continuous derivative.
(2)
The boundary of XX is smooth on the complement of a single point.
(3)
Ball packing stability and ellipsoid embedding stability fail for XX. In fact, we have the following more general assertion: Let W⊂ℝ4W\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} be an arbitrary compact domain with smooth boundary, possibly disconnected. Consider the number
pW​(X)≔suprvol⁡(r​W)vol⁡(X)∈[0,1],p^{W}(X)\coloneqq\sup_{r}\frac{\operatorname{vol}(rW)}{\operatorname{vol}(X)}\in[0,1],
where the supremum is taken over all r>0r>0 such that r​WrW admits a symplectic embedding into XX. Then pW​(X)p^{W}(X) is strictly less than 11.
We do not currently know whether counterexamples to packing stability exist for symplectic manifolds with boundary regularity of class C2C^{2} or higher. We expect that our methods for proving packing stability in the smooth case can be extended to manifolds whose boundary has finite differentiability. However, we do not expect that our present techniques can reach the C2C^{2} threshold; see Subsection 1.6 for further discussion.
It is a classical problem, dating back to work of Eliashberg and Hofer [EH92] (see also [CFHW96, Cie97]), to understand when an open symplectic manifold can be given a boundary, and to what extent the interior of a symplectic manifold with boundary encodes information about the boundary. Questions of this nature have also been studied in [CHa]. For example, it is shown there that, under suitable decay conditions on the function ff, the interior of the domain XfX_{f} defined in equation (1.1) is not symplectomorphic to the interior of any compact symplectic manifold with smooth boundary.
Note that if the interior of a symplectic 44-manifold MM is symplectomorphic to the interior of a compact symplectic manifold with smooth boundary, then packing stability must hold for each component of MM by Theorem A. Thus Theorem C has the following consequence:
Corollary (C2−εC^{2-\varepsilon} domains with non-smoothable interior).
There exists a star-shaped domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} which is C1C^{1} close to the unit ball, whose boundary is in the regularity class ⋂α∈(0,1)C1,α\bigcap_{\alpha\in(0,1)}C^{1,\alpha} and smooth on the complement of a single point, and whose interior is not symplectomorphic to the interior of a compact symplectic manifold with smooth boundary.
1.4. Subleading asymptotics of symplectic Weyl laws
Given a symplectic 44-manifold (M4,ω)(M^{4},\omega), possibly open or with boundary, Hutchings [Hut11a] defines a sequence of numbers
0=c0​(M)<c1​(M)≤c2​(M)≤⋯≤∞0=c_{0}(M)<c_{1}(M)\leq c_{2}(M)\leq\cdots\leq\infty
called embedded contact homology (ECH) capacities; see [Hut14] for a survey. For every compact domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with piecewise smooth boundary, he proves the following remarkable asymptotic formula:
(1.2)
ck​(X)=2​vol⁡(X)​k+o⁡(k1/2)(k→∞).c_{k}(X)=2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X)k}+o(k^{1/2})\qquad(k\rightarrow\infty).
This identity shows that ECH capacities asymptotically recover the symplectic volume of the domain. By analogy with the classical Weyl law for the spectrum of the Laplace operator, formula (1.2) is often referred to as the ECH Weyl law. We refer to [CHR15] for a version of the Weyl law for ECH spectral invariants associated with a contact form on a 33-manifold. See also [Iri15] for a major application of the ECH Weyl law concerning the smooth closing lemma for 33-dimensional Reeb flows.
It is a natural and compelling problem to study the error terms
ek​(X)≔ck​(X)−2​vol⁡(X)​ke_{k}(X)\coloneqq c_{k}(X)-2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X)k}
in the ECH Weyl law.
In the case of the classical Weyl law for the Laplace spectrum, the corresponding error terms have been extensively investigated over the past century; see, for example, [Cou20, Lev52, DG75, Ivr80, CG23], among many others. In the symplectic setting, Sun [Sun19] proved that ek​(X)=O⁡(k125/252)e_{k}(X)=O(k^{125/252}) for every star-shaped domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with smooth boundary. This was improved by Cristofaro-Gardiner and Savale [CS20], who established the bound ek​(X)=O⁡(k2/5)e_{k}(X)=O(k^{2/5}). Later Hutchings [Hut22a, Theorem 1.1] showed that in fact ek​(X)=O⁡(k1/4)e_{k}(X)=O(k^{1/4}) for every compact domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with smooth boundary.
On the other hand, in all known examples where the capacities ck​(X)c_{k}(X) have been explicitly computed, one finds that ek​(X)=O​(1)e_{k}(X)=O(1); see, for instance, [Wor23]. These examples comprise different classes of toric domains and are somewhat limited in scope, as the Reeb dynamics on the boundary of a toric domain is exceptionally simple and fails to reflect the complex, chaotic dynamics that can occur on the boundary of a general domain.
Nevertheless, Hutchings [Hut22a, Conjecture 1.5] conjectured that, for a generic star-shaped domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with smooth boundary, the error terms satisfy
(1.3)
limk→∞ek​(X)=−12​Ru⁡(X),\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}e_{k}(X)=-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Ru}(X),
where Ru⁡(X)\operatorname{Ru}(X) denotes the Ruelle invariant [Rue85] associated to the natural Reeb flow on the boundary of XX; see [Hut22a, §1.2] for more details. This conjecture can be viewed as a symplectic analogue of the well-known Weyl conjecture on the error terms in the classical Weyl law; see, for instance, [LMP23, Conjecture 3.3.7].
The following theorem is an application of our symplectic ball packing stability result, Theorem A. It establishes that the error terms in the ECH Weyl law are bounded for all compact domains in ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} with smooth boundary. This significantly improves upon the best previously known bound of O⁡(k1/4)O(k^{1/4}), and can also be viewed as a step towards Hutchings’ conjecture (1.3).
Theorem D (Subleading asymptotics ECH).
Let X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} be a compact domain with smooth boundary. Then the ECH capacities satisfy
ck​(X)=2​vol⁡(X)​k+O⁡(1)(k→∞).c_{k}(X)=2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X)k}+O(1)\qquad(k\rightarrow\infty).
In other words, ek​(X)=O​(1)e_{k}(X)=O(1).
The exponent 00 in the bound O⁡(1)O(1) on the error terms ek​(X)e_{k}(X) is sharp. There are examples of smooth domains XX, for example the ball B⁡(a)B(a), such that ek​(X)e_{k}(X) does not converge to zero; see, for instance, [Hut22a, Example 1.2].
Remark 1.2.
Recently, Hutchings [Hut22b] defined an alternative sequence of capacities ckalt​(X)c_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X) which retain most of the formal properties of the ECH capacities ck​(X)c_{k}(X), thereby recovering many key applications. Unlike the ECH capacities, however, their construction avoids the sophisticated machinery of embedded contact homology and is instead considerably more elementary. The proof of Theorem D carries over essentially unchanged to this setting and implies that the error terms in the Weyl law for these alternative capacities are also bounded. More precisely, for every compact domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with smooth boundary, one has
ckalt​(X)=2​vol⁡(X)​k+O⁡(1)(k→∞).c_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X)=2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X)k}+O(1)\qquad(k\rightarrow\infty).
A variant of embedded contact homology, known as periodic Floer homology (PFH), was introduced in [Hut02, HS05]. This theory allows one to extract numerical invariants—called PFH spectral invariants—from area-preserving surface diffeomorphisms. It was recently established independently by Cristofaro-Gardiner, Prasad, and Zhang [CPZ], and by Hutchings and the author [EH], that these invariants satisfy a Weyl-type asymptotic formula, analogous to the ECH Weyl law. Important applications of the PFH Weyl law include smooth closing lemmas for area preserving surface maps established in [CPZ, EH] and the resolution of the long-standing simplicity conjecture in [CHS24a].
As in the ECH case, understanding the asymptotic behavior of the associated error terms is an interesting problem; see, for instance, [Aim, Problem 2.2]. Our ball packing stability result (Theorem A) implies that the error terms in the PFH Weyl law are bounded. More precisely, we obtain the following result. For the relevant notation and terminology, we refer to [EH, Theorem 8.1].
Theorem E (Subleading asymptotics PFH).
Consider a closed surface Σ\Sigma equipped with an area form ω\omega of total area AA. Fix a rational area preserving diffeomorphism ϕ∈Diff⁡(Σ,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{Diff}(\Sigma,\omega) and let YϕY_{\phi} denote its mapping torus. Let γi⊂Yϕ\gamma_{i}\subset Y_{\phi} be a sequence of monotone reference cycles whose degrees did_{i} diverge to +∞+\infty and let σi∈H​P​(ϕ,γi)\sigma_{i}\in HP(\phi,\gamma_{i}) be a sequence of non-zero PFH classes. Then, for every pair of Hamiltonians H±∈C∞​(Yϕ)H_{\pm}\in C^{\infty}(Y_{\phi}), we have
cσi​(ϕ,γi,H+)−cσi​(ϕ,γi,H−)+∫γi(H+−H−)​𝑑t=di​A−1​∫Yϕ(H+−H−)​𝑑t∧ωϕ+O⁡(1)(k→∞).c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{+})-c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{-})+\int_{\gamma_{i}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt=d_{i}A^{-1}\int_{Y_{\phi}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt\wedge\omega_{\phi}+O(1)\qquad(k\rightarrow\infty).
Remark 1.3.
In Theorem E, we set the subgroups Gi⊂ker⁡(ωϕ)G_{i}\subset\operatorname{ker}(\omega_{\phi}) appearing in [EH, Theorem 8.1] all equal to ker⁡(ωϕ)\operatorname{ker}(\omega_{\phi}). As explained in [EH, Remark 8.2] and [EH, Remark 7.3], this choice, combined with the assumption that ϕ\phi is rational and the reference cycles γi\gamma_{i} are monotone, ensures that the UU-cycle assumption in [EH, Theorem 8.1] is automatically satisfied. This was proved in [CPPZ]. We also emphasize that Theorem E is not vacuous: there exists a sequence of monotone reference cycles γi\gamma_{i} with diverging degrees, along with a sequence of non-zero classes σi∈H​P​(ϕ,γi)\sigma_{i}\in HP(\phi,\gamma_{i}); see again [EH, Remark 8.2].
Remark 1.4.
In [Edta], we introduced an alternative to PFH spectral invariants using more elementary methods; see also Remark 1.2.
These elementary PFH spectral invariants likewise satisfy a Weyl law, and again our methods show that the associated error terms are O⁡(1)O(1). More precisely, in the notation of [Edta, Theorem 1.6], we have
cdi​(H+,H−)=di​A−1​∫Yϕ(H+−H−)​𝑑t∧ωϕ+O⁡(1)(i→∞)c_{d_{i}}(H_{+},H_{-})=d_{i}A^{-1}\int_{Y_{\phi}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt\wedge\omega_{\phi}+O(1)\qquad(i\rightarrow\infty)
for every diverging sequence of positive integers did_{i} such that cdic_{d_{i}} is finite for each ii.
In [CHMSS22], Cristofaro-Gardiner, Humilière, Mak, Seyfaddini, and Smith introduced another sequence of spectral invariants, called link spectral invariants, which also satisfy a Weyl law. As in the previous cases, it is natural to investigate their subleading asymptotics; see [Aim, Problem 2.1]. Let (Σ,ω)(\Sigma,\omega) be a closed surface equipped with an area form of total area AA. A Lagrangian link L¯⊂Σ\underline{L}\subset\Sigma is a disjoint union of smoothly embedded circles in Σ\Sigma. For links L¯\underline{L} satisfying a certain monotonicity condition, the associated link spectral invariant is a function
cL¯:C∞​([0,1]×Σ)→ℝ.c_{\underline{L}}:C^{\infty}([0,1]\times\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.
It is shown in [CHMSS22] that for an equidistributed sequence of dd-component links L¯d\underline{L}_{d}, one has
cL¯d​(H)=A−1​∫[0,1]×ΣH​𝑑t∧ω+o⁡(1)(d→∞).c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)=A^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}Hdt\wedge\omega+o(1)\qquad(d\rightarrow\infty).
In subsequent work [CHMSS], the same authors established the sharp bound O⁡(d−1)O(d^{-1}) on the subleading asymptotics of this Weyl law in the case where Σ\Sigma is the 2-sphere. For higher genus surfaces, Mak and Trifa [MT] obtained a weaker bound of O(d−1/2)O(d^{-1/2}).
Using Theorem E and results of Chen [Chea, Cheb] relating PFH spectral invariants to link spectral invariants, we are able to obtain the sharp bound O⁡(d−1)O(d^{-1}) for arbitrary genus. We emphasize, however, that the class of links considered in Chen’s work differs slightly from that in [CHMSS22]. Our result applies to the former class.
We say that a link L¯⊂Σ\underline{L}\subset\Sigma is admissible if the complement Σ∖L¯\Sigma\setminus\underline{L} consists of open disks and a single planar surface. We say that L¯\underline{L} is monotone if each connected component of Σ∖L¯\Sigma\setminus\underline{L} has the same area.
Theorem F (Subleading asymptotics link spectral invariants).
Let (Σ,ω)(\Sigma,\omega) be a closed connected surface of total area AA and consider a sequence L¯d\underline{L}_{d} of admissible, monotone dd-component links. Then, for every H∈C∞​([0,1]×Σ)H\in C^{\infty}([0,1]\times\Sigma), we have
cL¯d​(H)=A−1​∫[0,1]×ΣH​𝑑t∧ω+O⁡(d−1)(d→∞).c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)=A^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}Hdt\wedge\omega+O(d^{-1})\qquad(d\rightarrow\infty).
1.5. Overview of the proofs and outline of the paper
We now highlight some key ideas underlying the proofs of our main results. We begin with Theorems A and B, which concern ball packing and ellipsoid embedding stability, respectively. Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) be a compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold with smooth boundary. As noted above, since both ball packing and ellipsoid embedding stability are known to hold when the target is a symplectic ellipsoid, it suffices to fill the volume of MM—up to arbitrarily small error—by a single symplectic ellipsoid of fixed aspect ratio. In the case where (M,ω)(M,\omega) is closed and rational, Opshtein [Ops13, Proposition 1.3] observed that such an ellipsoid filling can be deduced from the existence of a Donaldson divisor [Don96]. This is closely related to the well-known Biran decomposition of a symplectic manifold [Bir01]. In contrast, our approach does not appeal to Donaldson’s theorem. Instead, we rely crucially on deep results of Banyaga concerning the simplicity and perfectness of the group of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms [Ban78, Ban97]. We refer to [BS] for another recent flexibility result related to Banyaga’s theorems.
To illustrate how the algebraic structure of Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups enters into our symplectic embedding constructions, we consider the following simple toy example. Let σ\sigma be an area form on the 22-sphere S2S^{2} of total area 11. Let 𝕋≔ℝ/ℤ\mathbb{T}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z} denote the circle, and define the open 44-manifold W≔ℝ×𝕋×S2W\coloneqq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times S^{2}, equipped with the symplectic form Ω≔d​s∧d​t+σ\Omega\coloneqq ds\wedge dt+\sigma, where (s,t)(s,t) are coordinates on the cylinder ℝ×𝕋\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}. Let H:𝕋×S2→ℝH:\mathbb{T}\times S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be a 11-periodic, mean-normalized Hamiltonian. We naturally view its graph as a codimension-11 hypersurface graph⁡(H)⊂(W,Ω)\operatorname{graph}(H)\subset(W,\Omega). The characteristic foliation on graph⁡(H)\operatorname{graph}(H) induced by Ω\Omega lifts the Hamiltonian flow φHt\varphi_{H}^{t} on (S2,σ)(S^{2},\sigma) generated by HH. In particular, graph⁡(H)\operatorname{graph}(H) can be interpreted as the mapping torus of φH1\varphi_{H}^{1}. Let C<0C<0 be a constant strictly less than the minimum of HH, and define MM to be the compact region in WW bounded from below by {C}×𝕋×S2\{C\}\times\mathbb{T}\times S^{2} and from above by graph⁡(H)\operatorname{graph}(H). We view (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) as a toy model for a compact symplectic 44-manifold with smooth boundary. Since HH is arbitrary, the dynamics on the boundary component graph⁡(H)\operatorname{graph}(H) can be complicated. In what follows, we sketch how to decompose MM, for sufficiently negative values of CC, into much simpler pieces—namely finitely many balls and polydisks.
In suitable polar coordinates (z,θ)∈(0,1)×𝕋(z,\theta)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{T} on S2S^{2}, the area form σ\sigma can be written as σ=d​z∧d​θ\sigma=dz\wedge d\theta. Define the autonomous Hamiltonian
F:S2→ℝF⁡(z,θ)≔12​z−14.F:S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad F(z,\theta)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}z-\frac{1}{4}.
This Hamiltonian is mean-normalized and its time-11 flow φF1\varphi_{F}^{1} is a half-rotation of S2S^{2}. Consider the normal closure of the subgroup {id,φF1}⊂Ham⁡(S2,σ)\left\{\operatorname{id},\varphi_{F}^{1}\right\}\subset\operatorname{Ham}(S^{2},\sigma) generated by φF1\varphi_{F}^{1}. Since Ham⁡(S2,σ)\operatorname{Ham}(S^{2},\sigma) is a simple group by Banyaga’s theorem, this normal closure must equal the entire group Ham⁡(S2)\operatorname{Ham}(S^{2}). This implies that every Hamiltonian diffeomorphism of (S2,σ)(S^{2},\sigma) can be expressed as a finite composition of conjugates of the half-rotation φF1\varphi_{F}^{1}. In particular, we can find finitely many Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms ψ1,…,ψn∈Ham⁡(S2,σ)\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\in\operatorname{Ham}(S^{2},\sigma) such that
(1.4)
φH1=(ψn−1∘φF1∘ψn)∘⋯∘(ψ1−1∘φF1∘ψ1)=φψn∗​F1∘⋯∘φψ1∗​F1.\varphi_{H}^{1}=(\psi_{n}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{F}^{1}\circ\psi_{n})\circ\cdots\circ(\psi_{1}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{F}^{1}\circ\psi_{1})=\varphi_{\psi_{n}^{*}F}^{1}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{\psi_{1}^{*}F}^{1}.
Moreover, this identity continues to hold on the level of the universal cover of Ham⁡(S2,σ)\operatorname{Ham}(S^{2},\sigma), possibly after adding two additional factors of φF1\varphi_{F}^{1}.
We now use the factorization (1.4) of φH1\varphi_{H}^{1} into half-rotations φψi∗​F1\varphi_{\psi_{i}^{*}F}^{1} to construct a decomposition of MM into balls and polydisks. To this end, we define a special Hamiltonian G:𝕋×S2→ℝG:\mathbb{T}\times S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} with the same time-11 map as HH, that is φG1=φH1\varphi_{G}^{1}=\varphi_{H}^{1}. This Hamiltonian is constructed such that the path (φGt)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{G}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} is the concatenation of the nn paths (φψi∗​Ft)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{\psi_{i}^{*}F}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]}. We divide the circle 𝕋\mathbb{T} into intervals Ii≔[i−1n,in]I_{i}\coloneqq[\frac{i-1}{n},\frac{i}{n}] for 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n. Let ρn:𝕋→[0,∞)\rho_{n}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow[0,\infty) be a non-negative function such that ρn|Ii\rho_{n}|_{I_{i}} is compactly supported in the interior of IiI_{i}, and ∫Iiρn​𝑑t=1\int_{I_{i}}\rho_{n}dt=1. We set
G⁡(t,p)≔ρn​(t)​ψi∗​F​(p)for p∈S2 and t∈Ii.G(t,p)\coloneqq\rho_{n}(t)\psi_{i}^{*}F(p)\qquad\text{for $p\in S^{2}$ and $t\in I_{i}$}.
Here the role of ρn\rho_{n} is to reparametrize the paths (φψi∗​Ft)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{\psi_{i}^{*}F}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} in such a way that GG is smooth.
Now assume that the constant C<0C<0 is smaller than the minimum of GG. We then consider the compact region NN in WW bounded below by {C}×𝕋×S2\left\{C\right\}\times\mathbb{T}\times S^{2} and bounded above by graph⁡(G)\operatorname{graph}(G). It is an elementary but important observation that (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) and (N,Ω)(N,\Omega) are symplectomorphic, provided HH can be connected to GG through a smooth family HλH^{\lambda} of mean-normalized Hamiltonians, strictly greater than CC, and satisfying φHλ1=φH1=φG1\varphi_{H^{\lambda}}^{1}=\varphi_{H}^{1}=\varphi_{G}^{1} for all λ\lambda; see Lemma 4.4. Since the factorization (1.4) is assumed to hold on the level of the universal cover, such a connecting family of Hamiltonians exists whenever CC is sufficiently negative. We have therefore reduced the task of decomposing (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) into balls and polydisks to the analogous task for (N,Ω)(N,\Omega).
We now cut (N,Ω)(N,\Omega) into nn pieces of the form N∩(ℝ×Ii×S2)N\cap(\mathbb{R}\times I_{i}\times S^{2}). It is straightforward to verify that each of these nn pieces is symplectomorphic to the domain K⊂ℝ×[0,1]×S2K\subset\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\times S^{2} bounded below by {Cn}×[0,1]×S2\left\{\frac{C}{n}\right\}\times[0,1]\times S^{2} and above by the graph of FF. Here, we equip ℝ×[0,1]×S2\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\times S^{2} with the symplectic form d​s∧d​t+σds\wedge dt+\sigma and regard FF as a Hamiltonian F:[0,1]×S2→ℝF:[0,1]\times S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}. Hence it suffices to decompose KK into balls and polydisks. This can be done explicitly, since FF is a simple height function on S2S^{2}.
We remove the poles of S2S^{2} and cut open the resulting annulus (0,1)×𝕋(0,1)\times\mathbb{T} along the line segment {θ=0}\left\{\theta=0\right\}. This reveals that the open domain
U≔{(s,t,z,θ)∈ℝ×(0,1)3∣Cn<s<12​z−14}⊂(ℝ4,d​s∧d​t+d​z∧θ)U\coloneqq\left\{(s,t,z,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)^{3}\mid\frac{C}{n}<s<\frac{1}{2}z-\frac{1}{4}\right\}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{4},ds\wedge dt+dz\wedge\theta)
can be naturally regarded as an open subset of KK of full volume. The domain UU can be viewed as the Lagrangian product of the right trapezoid
{(s,z)∈ℝ×(0,1)∣Cn<s<12​z−14}\left\{(s,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)\mid\frac{C}{n}<s<\frac{1}{2}z-\frac{1}{4}\right\}
and the square (0,1)2(0,1)^{2}. We can cut the right trapezoid into a rectangle and a triangle. This yields a decomposition of UU into two pieces: a Lagrangian product of a rectangle and a square, which is symplectomorphic to a polydisk, and a Lagrangian product of a right triangle and a square, which is symplectomorphic to an ellipsoid. In our case, this ellipsoid is E⁡(1,12)E(1,\frac{1}{2}), which can in turn be decomposed into two disjoint copies of the ball B⁡(12)B(\frac{1}{2}). This concludes our construction of a decomposition of (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) into balls and polydisks. We carry out a variation of this construction in more detail in Section 4.
A significant amount of work is required to extend this simple toy example to obtain a filling of a general compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold by a single ellipsoid. One immediate challenge is to upgrade a decomposition into finitely many balls and polydisks to an ellipsoid filling. Very roughly speaking, our strategy is as follows. Since ellipsoid embedding stability is already known to hold for balls and polydisks, we can fill each of the balls and polydisks in our decomposition with a skinny ellipsoid. We assume that the smaller of the two widths of each skinny ellipsoid is the same across all the ellipsoids. We then connect these skinny ellipsoids through tunnels at the interfaces between the balls and polydisks in the decomposition, ultimately forming a single ellipsoid that fills the entire volume. A detailed implementation of this strategy is provided in Sections 5 and 6. It is interesting to point out that these arguments, combined with the proof of ball packing stability for general, possibly irrational, closed symplectic 44-manifolds given in [BHO16], are already sufficient to prove ellipsoid embedding stability for general closed symplectic 44-manifolds. This special case of Theorem B resolves [BHO16, Conjecture 1].
Another challenge is that a general symplectic 44-manifold MM is not necessarily symplectomorphic to the subgraph of some Hamiltonian on a surface. Our strategy is to decompose MM into pieces that are symplectomorphic to subgraphs. To achieve this, it becomes necessary to replace the 22-sphere S2S^{2} with the annulus 𝔸\mathbb{A} since MM could be exact and, in particular, not contain any closed symplectic surfaces. Working on the annulus requires versions of Banyaga’s results for Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms on a surface with boundary, where the diffeomorphisms are not required to restrict to the identity on the boundary. We establish such generalizations in a separate paper [Edtb]. They are reviewed in Section 2. The decomposition of a general manifold MM into subgraphs of Hamiltonians on the annulus 𝔸\mathbb{A} is somewhat technical in nature and is carried out in Sections 7, 8, and 9.
A more subtle issue with our toy example above is the role of the negative constant CC. Recall that we only explained how to decompose the domain (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) inside WW into balls and polydisks for sufficiently negative CC. The problem is that the threshold of how negative CC has to be depends on nn, the number of half-rotations in the factorization of φH1\varphi_{H}^{1}. Specifically, the oscillation of the Hamiltonian GG is of the same order of magnitude as nn, meaning that |C||C| must also be of the order of nn. However, the number nn of half-rotations in the factorization of φH1\varphi_{H}^{1} can be arbitrarily large. In fact, this number is bounded from below by the autonomous norm of φH1\varphi_{H}^{1}, which is defined as the least number of autonomous Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms required in a factorization of φH1\varphi_{H}^{1}. The autonomous norm on Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups is known to be unbounded in many cases; see, for instance, [GG04, §6.3], [BK13], [BKS18]. This is problematic because we would like to decompose (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) into balls and polydisks for a fixed value of CC, not just for sufficiently negative values. In order to resolve this, we establish certain quantitative refinements of Banyaga’s results in [Edtb]. These refinements are of independent interest and are reviewed in Section 2. As a consequence, we show in Section 3 that the number of rotations needed to factor a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism is bounded in some C∞C^{\infty} open neighbourhood of the identity. In Section 4, we use this quantitative factorization result to decompose subgraphs of Hamiltonians on 𝔸\mathbb{A} that are C∞C^{\infty} small perturbations of affine Hamiltonians into balls and polydisks.
In Section 10, we prove Theorems D, E, and F, establishing bounds on the error terms in the Weyl laws for ECH capacities, PFH spectral invariants, and link spectral invariants. The proof of the ECH Weyl law for domains in ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} given in [Hut11a] relies on packing domains with balls up to arbitrarily small error, where the number of balls depends on the error. The more efficient ball packing provided by Theorem A allows us to upgrade the estimates in [Hut11a] and obtain O⁡(1)O(1) bounds on the error terms. Similarly, in the case of PFH, the computations in [EH], which also rely on ball packings, can be improved to yield bounded error terms. Finally, the bounds on the error terms in the Weyl law for link spectral invariants are derived from the PFH bounds via Chen’s works [Chea, Cheb], which relate the two sets of spectral invariants.
Finally, in Section 11, we construct a star-shaped domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with regularity slightly less than C2C^{2} for which packing stability fails. To prove the failure of packing stability, we estimate the error terms in the ECH Weyl law (strictly speaking, the Weyl law for alternative ECH capacities, see Remark 1.2) and show that they diverge to negative infinity. It is noteworthy that our domain XX is not toric, so we cannot appeal to known computational techniques for ECH capacities of toric domains. Instead, our estimates rely on translating the problem of estimating the ECH capacities of XX into estimating the link spectral invariants of a certain Hamiltonian, which, in our situation, is much easier. This approach to estimating ECH capacities may be of independent interest; see [Aim, Problem 2.4 & 2.5].
1.6. Discussion and open problems
One of the main insights of this work is the connection between symplectic packing stability for manifolds with smooth boundary and the algebraic structure of Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups, particularly their simplicity and perfectness properties. This reflects a broader pattern: several deep questions about Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups—especially those related to Hofer geometry—have counterparts in the theory of symplectic embeddings. Illustrative examples include recent parallel developments concerning the large-scale geometry of the Hofer metric on Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups and the symplectic Banach–Mazur metric on spaces of symplectic domains; see, e.g., [PS23, CHS24b, CHb] and the references therein. Another instance is provided by closely related results on the local structure of geodesics with respect to the Hofer distance [BP94, LM95] and the symplectic Banach–Mazur distance [ABE].
In this light, the failure of packing stability for domains with rough boundary (Theorem C) may be viewed as an analogue of recent non-simplicity theorems for various groups of Hamiltonian homeomorphisms, beginning with the inital breakthrough [CHS24a] resolving the long-standing simplicity conjecture. It is proved in [CHS24b] that the group of area preserving homeomorphisms of the 22-sphere, denoted Ham¯​(S2)\overline{\operatorname{Ham}}(S^{2}), is not simple—contrasting the simplicity of the smooth group Ham⁡(S2)\operatorname{Ham}(S^{2}). This non-simplicity result does not directly correspond to failure of packing stability for domains with rough boundary, since the proof relies, loosly speaking, on detecting area preserving homeomorphisms with “infinite” Hofer energy and Calabi invariant, which would correspond to symplectic domains of infinite volume, for which the ball packing numbers are not even defined. However, Ham¯​(S2)\overline{\operatorname{Ham}}(S^{2}) contains a natural subgroup Hameo⁡(S2)\operatorname{Hameo}(S^{2}) of Hamiltonian hameomorphisms, introduced by Oh and Müller [OM07], whose elements have finite Hofer energy. It was shown in [CHMSS] that Hameo⁡(S2)\operatorname{Hameo}(S^{2}) is not a simple group either, and this result serves as a close analogue of Theorem C.
Given a compact, connected symplectic 44-manifold (M,ω)(M,\omega) with smooth boundary, a natural problem is to estimate the threshold k0=k0​(M)k_{0}=k_{0}(M) in Theorem A at which the ball packing numbers pk​(M)p_{k}(M) stabilize to 11. This appears to be a difficult question in general, as the threshold is highly sensitive to the geometry of the boundary of MM. For example, if Xn⊂ℝ4X_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} is a sequence of smooth domains converging to a rough domain X∗⊂ℝ4X_{*}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} as in Theorem C, for which packing stability fails, then the stability thresholds k0​(Xn)k_{0}(X_{n}) tend to infinity. Interestingly, for the toy model domains (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) inside WW considered in Subsection 1.5, the stability threshold k0​(M)k_{0}(M) can be estimated in terms of the minimal number of rotations needed to factor φH1\varphi_{H}^{1}. Closely related quantities—such as the autonomous norm of a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism—have been studied by various authors; see, for instance, [GG04, BK13, BKS18]. It would be interesting to further investigate this relationship.
Another natural problem is to determine explicit bounds on the error terms in the ECH Weyl law. As before, this is challenging due to the strong dependence of the error terms on the boundary of the domain. Notably, the problem of bounding these error terms is closely tied to that of estimating ball packing stability thresholds. Indeed, as seen in our proof of Theorem D in Section 10, one can obtain explicit bounds on the error terms ek​(X)e_{k}(X) for a smooth domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} in terms of the stability thresholds k0​(X)k_{0}(X) and k0​(B∖X)k_{0}(B\setminus X), where B⊂ℝ4B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} is a sufficiently large ball containing XX.
The Ruelle invariant Ru⁡(X)\operatorname{Ru}(X), which appears in Hutchings’ conjecture (1.3) on the error terms in the ECH Weyl law, can be defined for star-shaped domains X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with C2C^{2} boundary and it is continuous with respect to the C2C^{2} topology on the space of star-shaped domains [CE22, Proposition 2.13 c]. In light of Hutchings’ conjecture, one would therefore expect that the error terms ek​(X)e_{k}(X) are O⁡(1)O(1) for compact domains with C2C^{2} boundary. However, we do not yet know how to prove this. We show (Proposition 11.2) that the domains in Theorem C, which have regularity just below C2C^{2} and for which packing stability fails, have unbounded error terms ekalte_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}} in the Weyl law for the alternative ECH capacities (see Remark 1.2). The same is expected to hold for the error terms eke_{k} in the usual ECH Weyl law, but we strictly speaking do not prove this since we rely on results from [EH] which are only stated for the alternative ECH capacities. This leads to the following natural question, which could serve as a test for Hutchings’ conjecture.
Question 1.5.
Is C2C^{2} the regularity threshold at which the error terms in the ECH Weyl law start to be O⁡(1)O(1)?
If we knew packing stability for domains with C2C^{2} boundary, this would imply O⁡(1)O(1) subleading asymptotics for such domains. As already noted above, we expect that our methods yield packing stability for domains in some finite differentiability class, but we do not expect that our present techniques can reach C2C^{2} regularity. The reason for this is that our packing construction uses (quantitative versions of) Banyaga’s simplicity results, which ultimately rely on a KAM result due to Herman [Her70, Annexe, Theorem 2.2]. This result says that a C∞C^{\infty} small Hamiltonian perturbation of a Diophantine rotation of the 22-dimensional torus T2T^{2} is still conjugated to the original rotation. For sufficiently Diophantine rotation vectors, this continues to hold for C3+εC^{3+\varepsilon} perturbations, but fails for C3−εC^{3-\varepsilon} perturbations, see e.g. [Her83, CW13]. Since C4+εC^{4+\varepsilon} Hamiltonians generate C3+εC^{3+\varepsilon} Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms, it seems plausible that our methods can show packing stability with C4+εC^{4+\varepsilon} boundary, but they likely break down for lower regularity.
Question 1.6.
What is the regularity threshold at which packing stability starts to hold? Does it agree with the regularity threshold for O⁡(1)O(1) subleading asymptotics in the ECH Weyl law?
The star-shaped domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} of regularity just below C2C^{2} in our counterexample to packing stability (Theorem C) is not convex. In fact, there does not even exist a symplectomorphism of ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} mapping XX to a convex domain. The reason is that the sectional curvature of the boundary of XX (away from the single non-smooth point) is unbounded from below. A more interesting question is whether the interior of XX is symplectomorphic to the interior of a convex domain. It is reasonable to conjecture that this is not the case. Indeed, by [CE22] the Ruelle invariant of a smooth convex domain can be bounded from above and below by constants only depending on the volume and the action of a systole of the domain. Since volume and action of a systole are continuous on the space of convex domains with respect to the Hausdorff topology, Hutchings’ conjecture (1.3) suggests that the error terms in the ECH Weyl law of any (not necessarily smooth) convex domain should be bounded. Since the error terms of the domain XX in Theorem C diverge to negative infinity, one expects the interior of XX to not be symplectomorphic to the interior of a convex domain.
In view of this discussion, ECH capacities conjecturally do not yield obstructions to symplectic packing stability for general convex domains and we are led to the following question:
Question 1.7.
Does packing stability hold for the interiors of arbitrary, not necessarily smooth, convex domains?
Let (Y3,ξ)(Y^{3},\xi) be a closed connected contact 33-manifold. Let Γ∈H1​(Y,ℤ)\Gamma\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}) be a homology class such that c1​(ξ)+2​PD⁡(Γ)c_{1}(\xi)+2\operatorname{PD}(\Gamma) is a torsion element of H2​(Y,ℤ)H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}) and let II be an absolute ℤ\mathbb{Z}-grading of the embedded contact homology E​C​H​(Y,ξ,Γ)ECH(Y,\xi,\Gamma). Then the ECH Weyl law in [CHR15, Theorem 1.3] states that, for every sequence of non-zero homogeneous ECH classes σk∈E​C​H​(Y,ξ,Γ)\sigma_{k}\in ECH(Y,\xi,\Gamma) with grading I⁡(σk)I(\sigma_{k}) diverging to +∞+\infty and for every contact form λ\lambda defining ξ\xi, the ECH spectral invariants cσk​(Y,λ)c_{\sigma_{k}}(Y,\lambda) satisfy the asymptotic formula
(1.5)
cσk​(Y,λ)=vol⁡(Y,λ)​I​(σk)+o⁡(I​(σk)1/2)(k→∞).c_{\sigma_{k}}(Y,\lambda)=\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(Y,\lambda)I(\sigma_{k})}+o(I(\sigma_{k})^{1/2})\qquad(k\rightarrow\infty).
In view of Theorem D, the following question is natural:
Question 1.8.
Are the error terms in the ECH Weyl law (1.5) bounded as well?
In this direction, our packing stability result Theorem A together with the arguments in [CHR15, §3] imply the inequality
cσk​(Y,λ)≥vol⁡(Y,λ)​I​(σk)+O⁡(1)(k→∞).c_{\sigma_{k}}(Y,\lambda)\geq\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(Y,\lambda)I(\sigma_{k})}+O(1)\qquad(k\rightarrow\infty).
Moreover, if the error terms are O⁡(1)O(1) for one single contact form λ0\lambda_{0} defining ξ\xi, then they are O⁡(1)O(1) for every contact form λ\lambda defining ξ\xi.
Finally, it is natural to explore packing stability in higher dimensions. Since Banyaga’s results apply in arbitrary dimensions, it seems promising to attempt to extend the 44-dimensional symplectic embedding techniques developed in this paper to higher dimensions. However, the question of whether there exist higher-dimensional domains with rough boundaries for which packing stability fails remains wide open. Given the connections between symplectic packing and the algebraic structure of Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups established in this paper, this question can be viewed as a variant of the higher-dimensional version of the simplicity conjecture, which asks whether the groups of compactly supported Hamiltonian homeomorphisms Ham¯​(B2​n​(1))\overline{\operatorname{Ham}}(B^{2n}(1)) and hameomorphisms Hameo⁡(B2​n​(1))\operatorname{Hameo}(B^{2n}(1)) of the open ball B2​n​(1)B^{2n}(1) in dimension 2​n≥42n\geq 4 are simple or not; see, for instance, [MSS]. We expect these questions to be of the same level of difficulty.
Acknowledgements
The author thanks Dan Cristofaro-Gardiner, Richard Hind, and Michael Hutchings for helpful discussions and Felix Schlenk for his interest and comments on a draft of this paper. The author is supported by Dr. Max Rössler, the Walter Haefner Foundation, and the ETH Zürich Foundation.
2. Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms of surfaces with boundary
Given a symplectic surface with boundary, we introduce Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms which are not required to restrict to the identity on the boundary. This is a slightly non-standard notion, but has appeared before in the literature in similar forms; see, for instance, [ABHS18, §2]. Moreover, we state Theorem 2.1 proved in [Edtb], which is a quantitative refinement of Banyaga’s perfectness and simplicity results for Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups [Ban78, Ban97].
Let (Σ,ω)(\Sigma,\omega) be a 22-manifold equipped with an area form. We allow Σ\Sigma to have boundary and do not require it to be compact. Let Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) denote the group of all compactly supported diffeomorphisms of Σ\Sigma which preserve the area form ω\omega. Diffeomorphisms φ∈Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\varphi\in\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) are not required to restrict to the identity on the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. Consider an isotopy φt\varphi^{t} in Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega). Let XtX_{t} be the time dependent vector field generating φt\varphi^{t}. Since φt\varphi^{t} is a compactly supported area preserving isotopy, the vector field XtX_{t} is compactly supported, tangent to the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma, and satisfies ℒXt​ω=0\mathcal{L}_{X_{t}}\omega=0. Conversely, any such vector field XtX_{t} generates a compactly supported isotopy in Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega). Vector fields XtX_{t} satisfying these properties are in bijective correspondence with families of compactly supported closed 11-forms αt\alpha_{t} on Σ\Sigma which vanish on vectors tangent to the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. This correspondence is characterized by the identity αt=ιXt​ω\alpha_{t}=\iota_{X_{t}}\omega.
Consider a time dependent Hamiltonian H:[0,1]×Σ→ℝH:[0,1]\times\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}. The induced family of closed 11-forms d​HtdH_{t} is compactly supported and vanishes on vectors tangent to the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma if and only if, for every fixed time tt, the function HtH_{t} is locally constant on the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma and outside some compact subset of Σ\Sigma. If this is the case, we call the Hamiltonian HH ∂\partial-admissible. If HH is ∂\partial-admissible, then the vector field XHtX_{H_{t}} characterized by d​Ht=ιXHt​ωdH_{t}=\iota_{X_{H_{t}}}\omega induces an isotopy φHt\varphi_{H}^{t} in Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega).
If HH and GG are ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonians, we define new Hamiltonians H​#​GH\#G and H¯\overline{H} by
(2.1)
(H#G)t≔Ht+Gt∘(φGt)−1andH¯t≔−Ht∘φHt.(H\#G)_{t}\coloneqq H_{t}+G_{t}\circ(\varphi_{G}^{t})^{-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{H}_{t}\coloneqq-H_{t}\circ\varphi_{H}^{t}.
The Hamiltonians H​#​GH\#G and H¯\overline{H} are also ∂\partial-admissible. They generate the isotopies φHt∘φGt\varphi_{H}^{t}\circ\varphi_{G}^{t} and (φHt)−1(\varphi_{H}^{t})^{-1}, respectively.
We call an isotopy φt\varphi^{t} in Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) a compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy if it is of the form φt=φHt\varphi^{t}=\varphi_{H}^{t} for a ∂\partial-admissible HH which in addition is compactly supported and vanishes on the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. We call a diffeomorphism φ∈Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\varphi\in\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) a compactly supported Hamiltonian diffeomorphism if there exists a compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy (φt)t∈[0,1](\varphi^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} starting at φ0=id\varphi^{0}=\operatorname{id} and ending at φ1=φ\varphi^{1}=\varphi. The compactly supported Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms form a subgroup
Hamc⁡(Σ,ω)⊂Diffc⁡(Σ,ω).\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega)\subset\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega).
We emphasize that since HH is only required to vanish on the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma but not in a neighbourhood of it, Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms need not restrict to the identity on the boundary. We caution that while every ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian HH induces an isotopy in Diffc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Diff}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega), this isotopy is not necessarily a Hamiltonian isotopy.
Suppose now that Σ\Sigma is connected and not closed. Let Ham~c​(Σ,ω)\widetilde{\operatorname{Ham}}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) denote the universal cover of Hamc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega). The Calabi homomorphism
Cal:Ham~c​(Σ,ω)→ℝ\operatorname{Cal}:\widetilde{\operatorname{Ham}}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as follows: Given an element φ~∈Ham~c​(Σ,ω)\widetilde{\varphi}\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Ham}}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega), choose a Hamiltonian isotopy (φt)t∈[0,1](\varphi^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} representing φ~\widetilde{\varphi}. Let HH be the unique Hamiltonian generating φt\varphi^{t} which is compactly supported and vanishes on the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. Then Cal⁡(φ~)\operatorname{Cal}(\widetilde{\varphi}) is defined by
Cal⁡(φ~)≔∫[0,1]×ΣH​𝑑t∧ω.\operatorname{Cal}(\widetilde{\varphi})\coloneqq\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}Hdt\wedge\omega.
This integral turns out to be independent of the choice of representative φt\varphi^{t} of φ~\widetilde{\varphi}. We define
Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)⊂Hamc⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega)\subset\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega)
to be the subgroup consisting of all Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms φ∈Hamc⁡(Σ,ω)\varphi\in\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) which possess a lift φ~∈Ham~c​(Σ,ω)\widetilde{\varphi}\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Ham}}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) which is contained in the kernel of the Calabi homomorphism Cal\operatorname{Cal}.
For closed surfaces Σ\Sigma, it will be convenient to set Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)≔Ham⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega)\coloneqq\operatorname{Ham}(\Sigma,\omega). In the case that Σ\Sigma is disconnected, we define Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega) to be the subgroup consisting of all Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms φ∈Hamc⁡(Σ,ω)\varphi\in\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(\Sigma,\omega) such that, for every connected component Σ′\Sigma^{\prime} of Σ\Sigma, the restriction of φ\varphi to Σ′\Sigma^{\prime} belongs to Hamc0⁡(Σ′,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\omega).
It was proved by Banyaga in [Ban78] (see also [Ban97, §4.3]) that if (Σ,ω)(\Sigma,\omega) does not have boundary, then the group Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega) is perfect, i.e. it agrees with its commutator subgroup. If in addition Σ\Sigma is connected, then Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega) is also simple, i.e. it does not have any non-trivial normal subgroups. If Σ\Sigma has non-empty boundary, then the group Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega) is never simple because the diffeomorphisms restricting to the identity on the boundary form a non-trival normal subgroup. However, we prove in [Edtb] that Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega) is still perfect. In the context of general diffeomorphism groups, an analogous result is obtained in [Ryb98]. However, the methods there do not adapt well to the conservative setting and our proofs in [Edtb] rely on different tools.
For our purposes, the following refinement of the perfectness of Hamc0⁡(Σ,ω)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\Sigma,\omega) proved in [Edtb] will be essential. In the non-conservative setting, similar results were obtained in [HRT13], but it is unclear whether the proofs there can be adapted to the conservative settings and our arguments in [Edtb] are rather different.
Theorem 2.1 (Quantitative perfectness [Edtb]).
There exists a positive integer m>0m>0 such that the following is true. Let (Σ,ω)(\Sigma,\omega) be a symplectic surface, possibly with boundary and not necessarily compact, and let U⋐V⋐ΣU\Subset V\Subset\Sigma be relatively compact open subsets. Consider the map
Φ:Hamc0​(V)2​m→Hamc0⁡(V)(u1,v1,…,um,vm)↦∏i=1m[ui,vi].\Phi:\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(V)^{2m}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(V)\quad(u_{1},v_{1},\dots,u_{m},v_{m})\mapsto\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}[u_{i},v_{i}].
Let us equip both Hamc0⁡(V)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(V) and Hamc0⁡(U)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(U) with the topology of uniform C∞C^{\infty} convergence on the compact set V¯\overline{V}. Then there exist a C∞C^{\infty} open neighbourhood 𝒰⊂Hamc0⁡(U)\mathcal{U}\subset\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(U) of the identity and a continuous map
Ψ:𝒰→Hamc0​(V)2​m\Psi:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(V)^{2m}
which is a right inverse of Φ\Phi, i.e. which satisfies Φ∘Ψ=id𝒰\Phi\circ\Psi=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{U}}. Moreover, we can choose 𝒰\mathcal{U} and Ψ\Psi such that Ψ⁡(id)\Psi(\operatorname{id}) is arbitrarily C∞C^{\infty} close to the tuple (id,…,id)(\operatorname{id},\dots,\operatorname{id}).
Remark 2.2.
The right inverse Ψ\Psi in Theorem 2.1 can in fact be chosen to be smooth in a suitable sense, see [Edtb]. However, we will not need this here.
3. Quantitative factorization into rotations
The main result of this section is Theorem 3.1, which allows us to write Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms of the annuls which are C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity and have vanishing Calabi invariant as a composition of finitely many smooth conjugates of rotations in a quantitatively controlled way. A key ingredient is the quantitative perfectness result for Hamiltonian diffeomorphism groups Theorem 2.1. Some of our arguments are inspired by the proof that perfectness implies simplicity for certain groups of homeomorphisms; see [Eps70] and [Ban97, Theorem 2.1.7].
Let 𝕋≔ℝ/ℤ\mathbb{T}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z} denote the circle and consider the annulus 𝔸≔[0,1]×𝕋\mathbb{A}\coloneqq[0,1]\times\mathbb{T} equipped with coordinates (x,y)(x,y) and the area form ω≔d​x∧d​y\omega\coloneqq dx\wedge dy. For every real number α∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}, we define the autonomous Hamiltonian
Hα:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝHα​(t,x,y)≔α​x.H_{\alpha}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad H_{\alpha}(t,x,y)\coloneqq\alpha x.
The Hamiltonian HαH_{\alpha} is ∂\partial-admissible and the Hamiltonian vector field of HαH_{\alpha} is given by XHα=−α∂yX_{H_{\alpha}}=-\alpha\partial_{y}. The time-11 map φHα1\varphi_{H_{\alpha}}^{1} of the induced flow is the rotation
Rα:𝔸→𝔸Rα​(x,y)≔(x,y−α).R_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}\qquad R_{\alpha}(x,y)\coloneqq(x,y-\alpha).
Theorem 3.1.
There exists an integer ℓ>0\ell>0 divisible by 44 such that the following statement is true for every α∈ℝ∖12​ℤ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z} and for every C∞C^{\infty} neighbourhood 𝒰⊂Ham0⁡(𝔸)\mathcal{U}\subset\operatorname{Ham}^{0}(\mathbb{A}) of the identity:
For every Hamiltonian diffeomorphism φ∈Ham0⁡(𝔸)\varphi\in\operatorname{Ham}^{0}(\mathbb{A}) which is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity, there exist Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms ψ1,…,ψℓ∈𝒰\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\ell}\in\mathcal{U} depending continuously on φ\varphi with respect to the C∞C^{\infty} topology and satisfying the following property: For 1≤i≤ℓ1\leq i\leq\ell, define εi≔+1\varepsilon_{i}\coloneqq+1 if ii is congruent to 00 or 11 modulo 44 and εi≔−1\varepsilon_{i}\coloneqq-1 otherwise. Moreover, define Hi≔εi​ψi∗​HαH_{i}\coloneqq\varepsilon_{i}\psi_{i}^{*}H_{\alpha}. Then
φ=∏i=1ℓφHi1.\varphi=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\varphi_{H_{i}}^{1}.
Let us abbreviate the two components of ∂𝔸\partial\mathbb{A} by
∂+𝔸≔{1}×𝕋and∂−𝔸≔{0}×𝕋.\partial_{+}\mathbb{A}\coloneqq\left\{1\right\}\times\mathbb{T}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{-}\mathbb{A}\coloneqq\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{T}.
Corollary 3.2.
Let ℓ>0\ell>0 be an integer as in Theorem 3.1. Then for every α∈ℝ∖12​ℤ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}, every C∞C^{\infty} neighbourhood 𝒰⊂Ham0⁡(𝔸)\mathcal{U}\subset\operatorname{Ham}^{0}(\mathbb{A}) of the identity, and every C∞C^{\infty} neighbourhood 𝒱⊂C∞​([0,1]×𝔸)\mathcal{V}\subset C^{\infty}([0,1]\times\mathbb{A}) of zero, the following statement holds:
For every ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian H:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝH:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} which is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to 00 and satisfies
(3.1)
∫[0,1]×𝔸H​𝑑t∧ω=0and∫[0,1]H⁡(t,∂±𝔸)​𝑑t=0,\int\limits_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}Hdt\wedge\omega=0\quad\text{and}\quad\int\limits_{[0,1]}H(t,\partial_{\pm}\mathbb{A})dt=0,
there exists a 11-parameter family of ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonians (Hλ)λ∈[0,1](H^{\lambda})_{\lambda\in[0,1]} starting at H0=HH^{0}=H and satisfying the following properties:
(1)
The time-11-map φHλ1\varphi_{H^{\lambda}}^{1} is independent of λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1].
(2)
For all λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1], we have
∫[0,1]×𝔸Hλ​𝑑t∧ω=0and∫[0,1]Hλ​(t,∂±𝔸)​𝑑t=0.\int\limits_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}H^{\lambda}dt\wedge\omega=0\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{[0,1]}H^{\lambda}(t,\partial_{\pm}\mathbb{A})dt=0.
(3)
For all λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1], we have Hλ∈𝒱H^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{V}.
(4)
There exist Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms ψi∈𝒰\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{U} such that
H1=#i=1ℓ​εi​ψi∗​Hα,H^{1}=\#_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}\psi_{i}^{*}H_{\alpha},
where the signs εi\varepsilon_{i} are defined as in Theorem 3.1.
Proof.
Note that equation (3.1) implies that φ≔φH1∈Ham0⁡(𝔸)\varphi\coloneqq\varphi_{H}^{1}\in\operatorname{Ham}^{0}(\mathbb{A}). Indeed, define a ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian KK by
K:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝK⁡(t,x,y)≔−x​H​(t,∂+𝔸)−(1−x)​H​(t,∂−𝔸).K:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad K(t,x,y)\coloneqq-xH(t,\partial_{+}\mathbb{A})-(1-x)H(t,\partial_{-}\mathbb{A}).
Then K​#​HK\#H clearly vanishes on ∂𝔸\partial\mathbb{A}. Moreover, it follows from equation (3.1) that φK1=id𝔸\varphi_{K}^{1}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}} and that ∫[0,1]×𝔸K​𝑑t∧ω=0\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}Kdt\wedge\omega=0. This implies that φK​#​H1=φH1\varphi_{K\#H}^{1}=\varphi_{H}^{1} and that ∫[0,1]×𝔸K​#​H​𝑑t∧ω=0\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}K\#Hdt\wedge\omega=0. We deduce that φH1∈Ham0⁡(𝔸)\varphi_{H}^{1}\in\operatorname{Ham}^{0}(\mathbb{A}).
We can therefore apply Theorem 3.1 to φ\varphi. Proving Corollary 3.2 then boils down to proving the following elementary assertion:
Let G0G^{0} and G1G^{1} be two C∞C^{\infty} small ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonians on 𝔸\mathbb{A}. Assume that φG01=φG11\varphi_{G^{0}}^{1}=\varphi_{G^{1}}^{1} and that
(3.2)
∫[0,1]×𝔸Gλ​𝑑t∧ω=0and∫[0,1]Gλ​(t,∂±𝔸)​𝑑t=0\int\limits_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}G^{\lambda}dt\wedge\omega=0\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{[0,1]}G^{\lambda}(t,\partial_{\pm}\mathbb{A})dt=0
for λ∈{0,1}\lambda\in\left\{0,1\right\}. Then there exists a family (Gλ)λ∈[0,1](G^{\lambda})_{\lambda\in[0,1]} of C∞C^{\infty} small ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonians on 𝔸\mathbb{A} connecting G0G^{0} to G1G^{1} and satisfying φGλ1=φG01\varphi_{G^{\lambda}}^{1}=\varphi_{G^{0}}^{1} and identity (3.2) for all λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1].
In order to see this, first note that we can connect G0G^{0} to a Hamiltonian which vanishes on ∂𝔸\partial\mathbb{A} as follows. Define
Kλ:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝKλ​(t,x,y)≔−λ⁡(x​G0​(t,∂+𝔸)+(1−x)​G0​(t,∂−𝔸)).K^{\lambda}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad K^{\lambda}(t,x,y)\coloneqq-\lambda(xG^{0}(t,\partial_{+}\mathbb{A})+(1-x)G^{0}(t,\partial_{-}\mathbb{A})).
As already observed above for the Hamiltonian KK, we have φKλ1=id𝔸\varphi_{K^{\lambda}}^{1}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}} for all λ\lambda. Thus (Kλ​#​G0)λ∈[0,1](K^{\lambda}\#G^{0})_{\lambda\in[0,1]} connects G0G^{0} to the Hamiltonian K1​#​G0K^{1}\#G^{0}, which vanishes on ∂𝔸\partial\mathbb{A}. Via an analogous construction, we connect G1G^{1} to a Hamiltonian vanishing on ∂𝔸\partial\mathbb{A}.
We may therefore replace G0G^{0} and G1G^{1} by Hamiltonians vanishing on ∂𝔸\partial\mathbb{A}. In this situation, the Hamiltonians GjG^{j} for j∈{0,1}j\in\left\{0,1\right\} generate paths (φGjt)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{G^{j}}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} in Ham⁡(𝔸)\operatorname{Ham}(\mathbb{A}) starting at the identity and ending at φ\varphi. The desired claim follows from the fact that Ham⁡(𝔸)\operatorname{Ham}(\mathbb{A}) is locally contractible with respect to the C∞C^{\infty} topology. As in the usual case of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms on symplectic manifolds without boundary, this can be proved using generating functions. We refer to [ABHS18, §2] for a discussion of generating functions in the context of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms which do not necessarily restrict to the identity on the boundary.
∎
Before turning to the proof of Theorem 3.1, we establish some preliminary lemmas.
Lemma 3.3.
Let XX be a topological space and let U⊂XU\subset X be an open subset. Let uu and vv be homeomorphisms of XX supported in UU. Let φ\varphi and ψ\psi be a homeomorphisms of XX such that the three sets UU, φ⁡(U)\varphi(U), and ψ⁡(U)\psi(U) are pairwise disjoint. Then the following identity holds:
(3.3)
[u,v]=[[u,φ],[v,ψ]].[u,v]=[[u,\varphi],[v,\psi]].
Proof.
We observe that
(3.4)
supp⁡([u,φ])⊂U∪φ⁡(U)and[u,φ]|U=u|U.\operatorname{supp}([u,\varphi])\subset U\cup\varphi(U)\quad\text{and}\quad[u,\varphi]|_{U}=u|_{U}.
Similarly, we have
(3.5)
supp⁡([v,ψ])⊂U∪ψ⁡(U)and[v,ψ]|U=v|U.\operatorname{supp}([v,\psi])\subset U\cup\psi(U)\quad\text{and}\quad[v,\psi]|_{U}=v|_{U}.
Since any two homeomorphisms with disjoint supports commute, the desired identity (3.3) is an immediate consequence of (3.4) and (3.5).
∎
Lemma 3.4.
Let U⊂𝔸U\subset\mathbb{A} be an open subset of the annulus. Let α∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R} and assume that the sets UU, Rα​(U)R_{\alpha}(U), and Rα−1​(U)R_{\alpha}^{-1}(U) are pairwise disjoint. Let u,v∈Hamc⁡(U)u,v\in\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(U) be compactly supported Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms. Then we have
(3.6)
[u,v]=φ(u−1)∗​Hα1∘φ−Hα1∘φ−(v−1)∗​Hα1∘φHα1∘φHα1∘φ−(u−1)∗​Hα1∘φ−Hα1∘φ(v−1)∗​Hα1.[u,v]=\varphi_{(u^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-(v^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-(u^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{(v^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}.
Proof.
We apply Lemma 3.3 with φ=Rα\varphi=R_{\alpha} and ψ=Rα−1\psi=R_{\alpha}^{-1} and evaluate the right hand side of identity (3.3).
∎
The following fragmentation lemma is proved in [Edtb].
Lemma 3.5.
Let U1,…,UnU_{1},\dots,U_{n} be an open covering of the annulus 𝔸\mathbb{A}. Then, for every φ∈Ham0⁡(𝔸)\varphi\in\operatorname{Ham}^{0}(\mathbb{A}) which is sufficiently C1C^{1} close to the identity, there exists a fragmentation
φ=φ1∘⋯∘φnwithφi∈Hamc0(Ui).\varphi=\varphi_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{n}\qquad\text{with}\kern 5.0pt\varphi_{i}\in\operatorname{Ham}^{0}_{c}(U_{i}).
The diffeomorphisms φi\varphi_{i} depend continuously on φ\varphi with respect to the C∞C^{\infty} topology on Ham0⁡(𝔸)\operatorname{Ham}^{0}(\mathbb{A}) and if φ=id\varphi=\operatorname{id}, then φi=id\varphi_{i}=\operatorname{id} for all ii.
For every p∈𝔸p\in\mathbb{A} and r>0r>0, let Br​(p)⊂𝔸B_{r}(p)\subset\mathbb{A} denote the open ball of radius rr centered at pp, where we equip 𝔸\mathbb{A} with the distance function induced by the Riemannian metric d​x2+d​y2dx^{2}+dy^{2}.
Lemma 3.6.
For every positive integer k>0k>0, we define the lattice
Pk≔(1k​ℤ∩[0,1])×(1k​ℤ/ℤ)⊂𝔸.P^{k}\coloneqq(\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,1])\times(\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z})\subset\mathbb{A}.
Then there exists a positive integer n>0n>0 with the following property: For every α∈ℝ∖12​ℤ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z} and for every positive integer kk such that 2k<dist⁡(α,12​ℤ)\frac{2}{k}<\operatorname{dist}(\alpha,\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}), there exists an nn-coloring of PkP^{k} such that, for any two distinct points p≠q∈Pkp\neq q\in P^{k} of the same color, the six sets
B1k​(p),Rα​(B1k​(p)),Rα−1​(B1k​(p)),B1k​(q),Rα​(B1k​(q)),Rα−1​(B1k​(q))B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p),\kern 5.0ptR_{\alpha}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p)),\kern 5.0ptR_{\alpha}^{-1}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p)),\kern 5.0ptB_{\frac{1}{k}}(q),\kern 5.0ptR_{\alpha}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(q)),\kern 5.0ptR_{\alpha}^{-1}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(q))
are pairwise disjoint.
Proof.
Let α∈ℝ∖12​ℤ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z} and let kk be a positive integer such that 2k<dist⁡(α,12​ℤ)\frac{2}{k}<\operatorname{dist}(\alpha,\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}). Note that this condition implies that, for every point p∈𝔸p\in\mathbb{A}, the three sets B1k​(p)B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p), Rα​(B1k​(p))R_{\alpha}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p)), and Rα−1​(B1k​(p))R_{\alpha}^{-1}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p)) are pairwise disjoint. We construct a graph GG with vertex set PkP^{k} as follows: For any two distinct p≠q∈Pkp\neq q\in P^{k}, we insert an edge between pp and qq if and only if the intersection
(B1k​(p)∪Rα​(B1k​(p))∪Rα−1​(B1k​(p)))∩(B1k​(q)∪Rα​(B1k​(q))∪Rα−1​(B1k​(q)))(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p)\cup R_{\alpha}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p))\cup R_{\alpha}^{-1}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p)))\cap(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(q)\cup R_{\alpha}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(q))\cup R_{\alpha}^{-1}(B_{\frac{1}{k}}(q)))
is non-empty. Note that vertex colorings of GG, i.e. colorings of the vertex set of GG such that no two vertices of the same color are connected by an edge, are precisely colorings of the lattice PkP^{k} satisfying the property in the statement of the lemma. Hence our task is to show that the chromatic number γ⁡(G)\gamma(G), i.e. the least number of colors needed to color GG, admits an upper bound independent of α\alpha and kk. Let Δ⁡(G)\Delta(G) denote the maximal vertex degree of GG, i.e. the maximal number of incident edges of any vertex of GG. By Brooks’ theorem [Bro41], the chromatic number of any graph satisfies the bound γ⁡(G)≤Δ⁡(G)+1\gamma(G)\leq\Delta(G)+1. We observe that, for each p∈Pkp\in P^{k}, there exist at most 99 distinct points q∈Pkq\in P^{k} such that B1k​(p)B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p) and B1k​(q)B_{\frac{1}{k}}(q) have non-empty intersection. This implies that Δ⁡(G)\Delta(G) admits an upper bound independent of α\alpha and kk. We deduce that the same is true for γ⁡(G)\gamma(G), which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎
Proof of Theorem 3.1.
Pick a positive integer m>0m>0 such that the conclusion of Theorem 2.1 holds. Moreover, pick a positive integer n>0n>0 such that the conclusion of Lemma 3.6 holds. Our goal is to show that Theorem 3.1 holds with
ℓ≔8​m​n.\ell\coloneqq 8mn.
Let α∈ℝ∖12​ℤ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}. Fix a positive integer k>0k>0 such that 2k<dist⁡(α,12​ℤ)\frac{2}{k}<\operatorname{dist}(\alpha,\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}). Pick an nn-coloring of the lattice Pk⊂𝔸P^{k}\subset\mathbb{A} as in Lemma 3.6. Fix a number 22<r<1\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}<r<1. For each color 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n, we define
Ui≔⋃p∈Pkp has color iBrk​(p)andVi≔⋃p∈Pkp has color iB1k​(p)U_{i}\coloneqq\bigcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in P^{k}\\
\text{$p$ has color $i$}\end{subarray}}B_{\frac{r}{k}}(p)\qquad\text{and}\qquad V_{i}\coloneqq\bigcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in P^{k}\\
\text{$p$ has color $i$}\end{subarray}}B_{\frac{1}{k}}(p)
Both unions are disjoint and Ui⋐Vi⋐𝔸U_{i}\Subset V_{i}\Subset\mathbb{A}. By the property of the coloring of PkP^{k} in the statement of Lemma 3.6, the sets ViV_{i}, Rα​(Vi)R_{\alpha}(V_{i}), and Rα−1​(Vi)R_{\alpha}^{-1}(V_{i}) are pairwise disjoint. Moreover, note that (Ui)i(U_{i})_{i} forms an open cover of 𝔸\mathbb{A}.
Suppose that φ∈Hamc0⁡(𝔸)\varphi\in\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(\mathbb{A}) is C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity. By Lemma 3.5, there exists a fragmentation
φ=φ1∘⋯∘φnwith φi∈Hamc0⁡(Ui).\varphi=\varphi_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{n}\quad\text{with $\varphi_{i}\in\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(U_{i})$}.
The diffeomorphisms φi\varphi_{i} depend continuously on φ\varphi and are C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity. By Theorem 2.1, we can write
φi=[ui1,vi1]∘⋯∘[uim,vim]with uij,vij∈Hamc0⁡(Vi).\varphi_{i}=[u_{i}^{1},v_{i}^{1}]\circ\cdots\circ[u_{i}^{m},v_{i}^{m}]\quad\text{with $u_{i}^{j},v_{i}^{j}\in\operatorname{Ham}_{c}^{0}(V_{i})$}.
The diffeomorphisms uiju_{i}^{j} and vijv_{i}^{j} also depend continuously on φ\varphi and are C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity.
By Lemma 3.4, we have
[uij,vij]=φ((uij)−1)∗​Hα1∘φ−Hα1∘φ−((vij)−1)∗​Hα1∘φHα1∘φHα1∘φ−((uij)−1)∗​Hα1∘φ−Hα1∘φ((vij)−1)∗​Hα1.[u_{i}^{j},v_{i}^{j}]=\varphi_{((u_{i}^{j})^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-((v_{i}^{j})^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-((u_{i}^{j})^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{-H_{\alpha}}^{1}\circ\varphi_{((v_{i}^{j})^{-1})^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}.
For an appropriate choice of Hamiltonian diffoemorphisms ψ1,…,ψℓ\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\ell} from the set
{id,(uij)−1,(vij)−1}\left\{\operatorname{id},(u_{i}^{j})^{-1},(v_{i}^{j})^{-1}\right\}
we then have
φ=∏i=1ℓφεi​ψi∗​Hα1.\varphi=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}\varphi_{\varepsilon_{i}\psi_{i}^{*}H_{\alpha}}^{1}.
Since the diffeomorphisms uiju_{i}^{j} and vijv_{i}^{j} depend continuously on φ\varphi and are C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity, the same if true for the diffeomorphisms ψi\psi_{i}.
∎
4. Stratification of subgraphs
In this section we prove Theorem 4.1, which says that the subgraph of a Hamiltonian on the annulus which is a C∞C^{\infty} small perturbation of an affine Hamiltonian can be decomposed into domains symplectomorphic to subgraphs of affine Hamiltonians. This decomposition induces a decomposition into symplectic ellipsoids and polydisks; see Remark 4.2.
It will be useful to introduce the following notation. Given a set XX and functions f,g:X→ℝf,g:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}, we let D⁡(f,g)⊂ℝ×XD(f,g)\subset\mathbb{R}\times X denote the intersection between the supergraph of ff and the subgraph of gg, i.e. we set
D⁡(f,g)≔{(s,x)∈ℝ×X∣f⁡(x)≤s≤g⁡(x)}.D(f,g)\coloneqq\left\{(s,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times X\mid f(x)\leq s\leq g(x)\right\}.
Moreover, we abbreviate D⁡(f)≔D⁡(0,f)D(f)\coloneqq D(0,f). This is the subgraph of ff, truncated at 00.
We are particularly interested in the case that the set XX is of the form X=[0,1]×ΣX=[0,1]\times\Sigma for a symplectic surface (Σ,ω)(\Sigma,\omega). In this case, we equip M≔ℝ×[0,1]×ΣM\coloneqq\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\times\Sigma with the symplectic form Ω≔d​s∧d​t+ω\Omega\coloneqq ds\wedge dt+\omega, where (s,t)(s,t) are the coordinates on ℝ×[0,1]\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]. This turns the domains D⁡(f,g)⊂(M,Ω)D(f,g)\subset(M,\Omega) into symplectic manifolds.
Recall from Section 3 that 𝔸=[0,1]×𝕋\mathbb{A}=[0,1]\times\mathbb{T} denotes the annulus. Moreover, recall that the Hamiltonian Hα:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝH_{\alpha}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} is defined by Hα​(t,x,y)=α​xH_{\alpha}(t,x,y)=\alpha x for α∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}. Given a real number a>0a>0, we let 𝔸⁡(a)\mathbb{A}(a) denote the annulus of area aa. More precisely, we set 𝔸⁡(a)≔[0,a]×𝕋\mathbb{A}(a)\coloneqq[0,a]\times\mathbb{T} and equip it with the area form ω≔d​x∧d​y\omega\coloneqq dx\wedge dy.
Theorem 4.1.
Let h:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝh:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be a Hamiltonian of the form h⁡(t,x,y)=a+b​xh(t,x,y)=a+bx for real numbers aa and bb. Assume that hh is strictly positive. Let H:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝH:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be a ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian. If HH is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to hh, then there exists a stratification 𝒮\mathcal{S} of D⁡(H)D(H) such that the closure S¯\overline{S} of any top-dimensional stratum SS of 𝒮\mathcal{S} is symplectomorphic either to D⁡(Hα|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(c))D(H_{\alpha}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(c)}) for a rational number α=p/q>0\alpha=p/q>0 and a real number c>0c>0 or to D⁡(C|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(c))D(C|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(c)}) for a positive constant function CC and a real number c>0c>0.
