There exists an upper bound on the denominator qq of α\alpha which can be chosen uniformly among all HH sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to a fixed hh. Moreover, there exists a uniform positive lower bound on the constants cc and CC.
Remark 4.2.
The full volume of D⁡(Hα|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(c))D(H_{\alpha}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(c)}) can be filled by a single copy of the ellipsoid E⁡(c,α​c)E(c,\alpha c); see Section 6. Similarly, D⁡(C|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(c))D(C|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(c)}) can be fully filled by the polydisk P⁡(c,C)P(c,C) of widths cc and CC. Theorem 4.1 therefore says that every sufficiently small perturbation of an affine subgraph can be fully packed by some finite collection of polydisks and rational ellipsoids.
We begin with some preliminary lemmas.
Lemma 4.3.
Let Σ\Sigma be a compact surface, possibly with boundary. Let ω\omega be an area form on Σ\Sigma. Let H:[0,1]×Σ→ℝH:[0,1]\times\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be a ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian. Then the map
fH:ℝ×[0,1]×Σ→ℝ×[0,1]×Σ(s,t,p)↦(s+H⁡(t,φHt​(p)),t,φHt​(p))f_{H}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\times\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\times\Sigma\qquad(s,t,p)\mapsto(s+H(t,\varphi_{H}^{t}(p)),t,\varphi_{H}^{t}(p))
is a symplectomorphism of (M,Ω)(M,\Omega). If F,G:[0,1]×Σ→ℝF,G:[0,1]\times\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R} are ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonians, then fHf_{H} maps D⁡(F,G)D(F,G) to D⁡(H​#​F,H​#​G)D(H\#F,H\#G).
Proof.
Both assertions follow from a straightforward direct computation.
∎
Lemma 4.4.
Let Σ\Sigma be a compact connected surface, possibly with boundary. Let ω\omega be an area form on Σ\Sigma. Let H±λ:[0,1]×Σ→ℝH_{\pm}^{\lambda}:[0,1]\times\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be two smooth families of ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonians on Σ\Sigma parametrized by λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1]. We assume that the strict inequality H+λ>H−λH_{+}^{\lambda}>H_{-}^{\lambda} holds for all λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1]. Then the following two statements are equivalent:
(1)
There exists a smooth family (ψλ)λ∈[0,1](\psi^{\lambda})_{\lambda\in[0,1]} of symplectomorphisms of (M,Ω)(M,\Omega) starting at the identity such that ψλ​(D⁡(H−0,H+0))=D⁡(H−λ,H+λ)\psi^{\lambda}(D(H_{-}^{0},H_{+}^{0}))=D(H_{-}^{\lambda},H_{+}^{\lambda}).
(2)
The following three assertions hold:
(i)
There exists a smooth isotopy (χλ)λ∈[0,1](\chi^{\lambda})_{\lambda\in[0,1]} of area preserving diffeomorphisms of Σ\Sigma starting at the identity such that
(φH−λ1)−1∘φH+λ1=χλ∘((φH−01)−1∘φH+01)∘(χλ)−1.(\varphi_{H_{-}^{\lambda}}^{1})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{H_{+}^{\lambda}}^{1}=\chi^{\lambda}\circ((\varphi_{H_{-}^{0}}^{1})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{H_{+}^{0}}^{1})\circ(\chi^{\lambda})^{-1}.
(ii)
For all boundary components SS of Σ\Sigma, the quantity
∫[0,1](H+λ​(t,S)−H−λ​(t,S))​𝑑t\int_{[0,1]}(H_{+}^{\lambda}(t,S)-H_{-}^{\lambda}(t,S))dt
is independent of λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1].
(iii)
The volume
vol⁡(D⁡(H−λ,H+λ))=∫[0,1]×Σ(H+λ−H−λ)​𝑑t∧ω\operatorname{vol}(D(H_{-}^{\lambda},H_{+}^{\lambda}))=\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}(H_{+}^{\lambda}-H_{-}^{\lambda})dt\wedge\omega
is independent of λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1].
Proof.
Let us abbreviate Aλ≔D⁡(H−λ,H+λ)A^{\lambda}\coloneqq D(H_{-}^{\lambda},H_{+}^{\lambda}). Note that AλA^{\lambda} is a manifold with boundary and corners. The boundary ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda} has the following top dimensional strata:
•
∂±Aλ≔graph⁡(H±λ)\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda}\coloneqq\operatorname{graph}(H_{\pm}^{\lambda})
•
∂jAλ≔(ℝ×{j}×Σ)∩Aλ\partial_{j}A^{\lambda}\coloneqq(\mathbb{R}\times\left\{j\right\}\times\Sigma)\cap A^{\lambda} for j∈{0,1}j\in\left\{0,1\right\}
•
∂SAλ≔(ℝ×[0,1]×S)∩Aλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda}\coloneqq(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\times S)\cap A^{\lambda} where SS is a component of ∂Σ\partial\Sigma
Restricting the natural projection M→[0,1]M\rightarrow[0,1] to ∂±Aλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda} yields a fibration of ∂±Aλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda} over the interval [0,1][0,1] with fiber Σ\Sigma. The strata ∂jAλ\partial_{j}A^{\lambda} for j∈{0,1}j\in\left\{0,1\right\} also fiber over the interval with fiber Σ\Sigma. We can choose the fibration such that the fibers are transverse to the vertical vector field ∂s\partial_{s}. Since the Hamiltonians H±λH_{\pm}^{\lambda} are assumed to be ∂\partial-admissible, there exists, for every boundary component SS of ∂Σ\partial\Sigma, a subset QSλ⊂ℝ×[0,1]Q_{S}^{\lambda}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[0,1] such that ∂SAλ=QSλ×S\partial_{S}A^{\lambda}=Q_{S}^{\lambda}\times S. The set QSλQ_{S}^{\lambda} is a smoothly embedded strip in ℝ×[0,1]\mathbb{R}\times[0,1] connecting ℝ×{0}\mathbb{R}\times\left\{0\right\} and ℝ×{1}\mathbb{R}\times\left\{1\right\}. The area of QSλQ_{S}^{\lambda} is given by the integral
area⁡(QSλ)=∫[0,1](H+λ​(t,S)−H−λ​(t,S))​𝑑t.\operatorname{area}(Q_{S}^{\lambda})=\int_{[0,1]}(H_{+}^{\lambda}(t,S)-H_{-}^{\lambda}(t,S))dt.
The symplectic form Ω\Omega on MM induces a characteristic foliation on ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda}. Let us orient this foliation such that the orientation of the foliation followed by the coorientation induced by the restriction of Ω\Omega yields the boundary orientation on ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda}. On the strata ∂±Aλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda}, the characteristic foliation is positively tangent to the vector field
(4.1)
±(∂tH±λ⋅∂s+∂t+XH±λ).\pm(\partial_{t}H_{\pm}^{\lambda}\cdot\partial_{s}+\partial_{t}+X_{H_{\pm}^{\lambda}}).
In particular, it is transverse to the fibers of ∂±Aλ→[0,1]\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda}\rightarrow[0,1]. The boundary of the boundary strata ∂±Aλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda} consists of two types of strata: the fibers over 00 and 11 of the projection ∂±Aλ→[0,1]\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda}\rightarrow[0,1] and the annuli of the form ∂±Aλ∩∂SAλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda}\cap\partial_{S}A^{\lambda} where SS is a component of ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. The characteristic foliation is transverse to the former type. Since the Hamiltonians H±λH_{\pm}^{\lambda} are assumed to be ∂\partial-admissible, the characteristic foliation is tangent to the latter type. On the strata ∂jAλ\partial_{j}A^{\lambda}, the characteristic foliation is positively tangent to (−1)j∂s(-1)^{j}\partial_{s}. For our choice of fibration ∂jAλ→[0,1]\partial_{j}A^{\lambda}\rightarrow[0,1], it is therefore transverse to the fibers. The union
(4.2)
Tλ≔∂+Aλ∪∂1Aλ∪∂−Aλ∪∂0AλT^{\lambda}\coloneqq\partial_{+}A^{\lambda}\cup\partial_{1}A^{\lambda}\cup\partial_{-}A^{\lambda}\cup\partial_{0}A^{\lambda}
is not smooth at the corners ∂±Aλ∩∂jAλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda}\cap\partial_{j}A^{\lambda}. By the above discussion, the characteristic foliation is transverse to these corners. Moreover, it is tangent to the boundary of TλT^{\lambda}. Therefore, the characteristic foliation induces a flow on TλT^{\lambda} which is well-defined up positive reparametrization. This flow is transverse to the fibers of the fibrations of the strata ∂±Aλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda} and ∂jAλ\partial_{j}A^{\lambda}. In fact, each fiber is a surface of section of the flow. Consider the fiber over 00 of the projection ∂+Aλ→[0,1]\partial_{+}A^{\lambda}\rightarrow[0,1]. Let ιλ:Σ↪M\iota^{\lambda}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow M denote the parametrization of this fiber which has the property the composition with the natural projection M→ΣM\rightarrow\Sigma agrees with the identity map of Σ\Sigma. With respect to the parametrization ιλ\iota^{\lambda}, the first return map of the flow is given by (φH−λ1)−1∘φH+λ1(\varphi_{H_{-}^{\lambda}}^{1})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{H_{+}^{\lambda}}^{1}. In other words, we can regard TλT^{\lambda} as a (non-smooth) mapping torus of the diffeomorphism (φH−λ1)−1∘φH+λ1(\varphi_{H_{-}^{\lambda}}^{1})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{H_{+}^{\lambda}}^{1}. On the strata ∂SAλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda}, the characteristic foliation is tangent to the circles {s}×{t}×S\left\{s\right\}\times\left\{t\right\}\times S. Thus we can regard ∂SAλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda} as the mapping torus of the identity map on the strip QSλQ_{S}^{\lambda}.
It is an easy consequence of the above discussion that statement (1) in Lemma 4.4 implies statement (2): A family of symplectomorphisms ψλ\psi^{\lambda} as in (1) clearly has to preserve vol⁡(Aλ)\operatorname{vol}(A^{\lambda}) and area⁡(QSλ)\operatorname{area}(Q_{S}^{\lambda}). Moreover, it maps the characteristic foliation on the boundary of A0A^{0} to the characteristic foliation on the boundary of AλA^{\lambda}. Thus it induces a familiy of conjugating diffeomorphisms for the first return maps of the mapping tori T0T^{0} and TλT^{\lambda}.
Conversely, assume that statement (2) in Lemma 4.4 holds. Our construction of the family of symplectomorphisms ψλ\psi^{\lambda} proceeds in several steps.
Step 1: We reduce ourselves to the case that χλ=id\chi^{\lambda}=\operatorname{id} for all λ∈[0,1]\lambda\in[0,1]. To this end, we define Hamiltonians H~±λ≔H±λ∘(χλ)−1\widetilde{H}_{\pm}^{\lambda}\coloneqq H_{\pm}^{\lambda}\circ(\chi^{\lambda})^{-1}. It follows from assumption (2i) that
(φH~−λ1)−1∘φH~+λ1=(φH~−01)−1∘φH~+01.(\varphi_{\widetilde{H}_{-}^{\lambda}}^{1})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\widetilde{H}_{+}^{\lambda}}^{1}=(\varphi_{\widetilde{H}_{-}^{0}}^{1})^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\widetilde{H}_{+}^{0}}^{1}.
In other words, the Hamiltonians H~±λ\widetilde{H}_{\pm}^{\lambda} satisfy assumption (2i) with χ~λ=id\widetilde{\chi}^{\lambda}=\operatorname{id}. Moreover, the Hamiltonians H~±λ\widetilde{H}_{\pm}^{\lambda} satisfy assumptions (2ii) and (2iii). Let A~λ⊂M\widetilde{A}^{\lambda}\subset M denote the region between the graphs of H~±λ\widetilde{H}_{\pm}^{\lambda}. It is not hard to see that if statement (1) holds for the family of sets A~λ\widetilde{A}^{\lambda}, then it also holds for AλA^{\lambda}. Indeed, given a family of symplectomorphisms ψ~λ\widetilde{\psi}^{\lambda} of MM starting at the identity and mapping A~0\widetilde{A}^{0} to A~λ\widetilde{A}^{\lambda}, simply define
ψλ≔(idℝ×[0,1]×χλ)−1∘ψ~λ.\psi^{\lambda}\coloneqq(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]}\times\chi^{\lambda})^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\psi}^{\lambda}.
The symplectomorphism ψλ\psi^{\lambda} maps A0=A~0A^{0}=\widetilde{A}^{0} to AλA^{\lambda}. After replacing H±λH_{\pm}^{\lambda} by H~±λ\widetilde{H}_{\pm}^{\lambda}, we can therefore assume that χλ=id\chi^{\lambda}=\operatorname{id}.
Step 2: We define a family of homeomorphisms
ψλ:T0→Tλ\psi^{\lambda}:T^{0}\rightarrow T^{\lambda}
with the following properties:
(a)
ψ0=id\psi^{0}=\operatorname{id}.
(b)
ψλ\psi^{\lambda} maps the fiber over 00 of the fibration ∂+A0→[0,1]\partial_{+}A^{0}\rightarrow[0,1] to the fiber over 00 of ∂+Aλ→[0,1]\partial_{+}A^{\lambda}\rightarrow[0,1]. Consider the parametrizations ι0\iota^{0} and ιλ\iota^{\lambda} of these fibers defined above. With respect to these parametrizations, the restriction of ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to these fibers is given by the identity map id\operatorname{id}.
(c)
For every λ\lambda, the boundary of TλT^{\lambda} fibers over ⋃S∂QSλ\bigcup_{S}\partial Q_{S}^{\lambda} with circle fibers of the form {s}×{t}×S\left\{s\right\}\times\left\{t\right\}\times S. The map ψλ\psi^{\lambda} maps circle fibers of ∂T0\partial T^{0} to circle fibers of ∂Tλ\partial T^{\lambda}.
(d)
The restriction of ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to ∂±A0\partial_{\pm}A^{0} is a diffeomorphism onto ∂±Aλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda}.
(e)
For j∈{0,1}j\in\left\{0,1\right\}, the restriction of ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to ∂jA0\partial_{j}A^{0} is a diffeomorphism onto ∂jAλ\partial_{j}A^{\lambda}.
(f)
ψλ\psi^{\lambda} is compatible with Ω\Omega, i.e. (ψλ)∗​Ω|Tλ=Ω|T0(\psi^{\lambda})^{*}\Omega|_{T^{\lambda}}=\Omega|_{T^{0}}. In fact, ψλ\psi^{\lambda} extends to a family of symplectomorphisms between open neighbourhoods of T0T^{0} and TλT^{\lambda} in (M,Ω)(M,\Omega).
The construction uses assumption (2i). Here are the details. Let (φtλ)t(\varphi^{\lambda}_{t})_{t} be a parametrization of the flow on TλT^{\lambda} induced by the characteristic foliation. We can choose φtλ\varphi^{\lambda}_{t} such that the time it takes a flow line to traverse each of the strata ∂±Aλ\partial_{\pm}A^{\lambda} and ∂jAλ\partial_{j}A^{\lambda} is exactly 11. Moreover, we can choose φtλ\varphi_{t}^{\lambda} such that, for each fixed tt, circle fibers on the boundary of TλT^{\lambda} are mapped to circle fibers. It follows from assumption (2i) and the reduction in Step 1 that there exists a unique family of homeomorphisms ψλ:T0→Tλ\psi^{\lambda}:T^{0}\rightarrow T^{\lambda} such that ψλ\psi^{\lambda} is a conjugacy between the flows (φt0)t(\varphi^{0}_{t})_{t} and (φtλ)t(\varphi^{\lambda}_{t})_{t}, i.e. such that
(ψλ)−1∘φtλ∘ψλ=φt0,(\psi^{\lambda})^{-1}\circ\varphi^{\lambda}_{t}\circ\psi^{\lambda}=\varphi^{0}_{t},
and such that property (b) holds. The family of homeomorphisms ψλ\psi^{\lambda} clearly satisfies properties (a) - (e). Moreover, we have (ψλ)∗​Ω|Tλ=Ω|T0(\psi^{\lambda})^{*}\Omega|_{T^{\lambda}}=\Omega|_{T^{0}}. If φtλ\varphi_{t}^{\lambda} is chosen with some care near the non-smooth locus of TλT^{\lambda}, one can extend ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to a symplectomorphism between open neighbourhoods of T0T^{0} and TλT^{\lambda} and therefore also guarantee property (f).
Step 3: We extend the family of homeomorphism ψλ\psi^{\lambda} from Step 2 to a family of homeomorphisms
ψλ:∂A0→∂Aλ\psi^{\lambda}:\partial A^{0}\rightarrow\partial A^{\lambda}
such that:
(g)
ψ0=id\psi^{0}=\operatorname{id}
(h)
ψλ\psi^{\lambda} restricts to a diffeomorphism between ∂SA0\partial_{S}A^{0} and ∂SAλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda} for all SS.
(i)
ψλ\psi^{\lambda} is compatible with Ω\Omega and extends to a symplectomorphism between open neighbourhoods of ∂A0\partial A^{0} and ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda} in (M,Ω)(M,\Omega).
The construction makes use of assumption (2ii). Recall that ∂SAλ=QSλ×S\partial_{S}A^{\lambda}=Q_{S}^{\lambda}\times S. The intersection of TλT^{\lambda} with ∂SAλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda} is equal to
∂(∂SAλ)=∂QSλ×S.\partial(\partial_{S}A^{\lambda})=\partial Q_{S}^{\lambda}\times S.
By property (c), the restriction of ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to ∂QS0×S\partial Q_{S}^{0}\times S maps circle fibers of ∂QS0×S\partial Q_{S}^{0}\times S to circle fibers of ∂QSλ×S\partial Q_{S}^{\lambda}\times S. In particular, it descends to a homeomorphism ψ¯λ:∂QS0→∂QSλ\overline{\psi}^{\lambda}:\partial Q_{S}^{0}\rightarrow\partial Q_{S}^{\lambda} which maps smooth strata diffeomorphically to smooth strata. Since QS0Q_{S}^{0} and QSλQ_{S}^{\lambda} have the same total area by assumption (2ii), we can extend to an area preserving diffeomorphism ψ¯λ:QS0→QSλ\overline{\psi}^{\lambda}:Q_{S}^{0}\rightarrow Q_{S}^{\lambda}. We define the extension of ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to QS0×SQ_{S}^{0}\times S to be a lift QS0×S→QSλ×SQ_{S}^{0}\times S\rightarrow Q_{S}^{\lambda}\times S of ψ¯λ\overline{\psi}^{\lambda} which agrees with the map ψλ\psi^{\lambda} already defined on the boundary ∂QS0×S\partial Q_{S}^{0}\times S. By construction, this extension restricts to a diffeomorphism between ∂SA0\partial_{S}A^{0} and ∂SAλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda}. Moreover, it is compatible with Ω\Omega since it lifts the area preserving diffeomorphism ψ¯λ\overline{\psi}^{\lambda}. By property (a) in Step 2 we can arrange ψ0\psi^{0} to be the identity. For a careful choice of extension and lift, the family ψλ\psi^{\lambda} extends to a family of symplectomorphisms between open neighbourhoods of A0A^{0} and AλA^{\lambda}.
Step 4: Using assumption (2iii), we extend the family of homeomorphisms ψλ\psi^{\lambda} form Step 3 to the desired family of symplectomorphisms of (M,Ω)(M,\Omega). First, extend ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to a family of symplectomorphisms between open neighbourhoods of ∂A0\partial A^{0} and ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda}. It is not hard to further extend to a family of symplectic embeddings
ψλ:nb⁡(M∖A0)→M\psi^{\lambda}:\operatorname{nb}(M\setminus A^{0})\rightarrow M
defined on a neighbourhood of the complement of A0A^{0} and starting at the inclusion ψ0\psi^{0}. The subtle part is to extend ψλ\psi^{\lambda} over the interior of A0A^{0}. This makes use of assumption (2iii). Let XλX^{\lambda} denote the family of symplectic vector fields generating the family of symplectic embeddings ψλ:nb⁡(M∖A0)↪M\psi^{\lambda}:\operatorname{nb}(M\setminus A^{0})\hookrightarrow M. Here the vector field XλX^{\lambda} is defined on a neighbourhood of M∖AλM\setminus A^{\lambda}. Our goal is to extend XλX^{\lambda} to a family of symplectic vector fields defined on all of MM. The desired extension of ψλ\psi^{\lambda} to all of MM will simply be the flow generated by XλX^{\lambda}. Let αλ\alpha^{\lambda} be the unique family of closed 11-forms characterized by the identity ιXλ​Ω=αλ\iota_{X^{\lambda}}\Omega=\alpha^{\lambda}. The closed 11-form αλ\alpha^{\lambda} is defined on nb⁡(M∖Aλ)\operatorname{nb}(M\setminus A^{\lambda}) and we need to extend to a closed 11-form on all of MM. Let us first choose an arbitrary extension of αλ\alpha^{\lambda} to a family of not necessarily closed 11-forms α0λ\alpha_{0}^{\lambda} defined on MM. The differential d​α0λd\alpha_{0}^{\lambda} is a closed 22-form with compact support contained in the interior int⁡(Aλ)\operatorname{int}(A^{\lambda}). We need to show that there exists a 11-form βλ\beta^{\lambda} compactly supported in int⁡(Aλ)\operatorname{int}(A^{\lambda}) such that d​βλ=d​α0λd\beta^{\lambda}=d\alpha_{0}^{\lambda}. Once we have such βλ\beta^{\lambda}, we can take αλ≔α0λ−βλ\alpha^{\lambda}\coloneqq\alpha_{0}^{\lambda}-\beta^{\lambda} to be the desired closed extension of αλ\alpha^{\lambda}. Showing the existence of βλ\beta^{\lambda} is equivalent to showing that the cohomology class [d​α0λ][d\alpha_{0}^{\lambda}] vanishes in Hc2​(int⁡(Aλ),ℝ)H^{2}_{c}(\operatorname{int}(A^{\lambda});\mathbb{R}). This in turn is equivalent to showing that the homomorphism
⟨⋅,d​α0λ⟩:H2​(Aλ,∂Aλ)→ℝ\langle\cdot,d\alpha_{0}^{\lambda}\rangle:H_{2}(A^{\lambda},\partial A^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
vanishes. The homomorphism ⟨⋅,d​α0λ⟩\langle\cdot,d\alpha_{0}^{\lambda}\rangle is given by the composition of the boundary homomorphism ∂:H2​(Aλ,∂Aλ)→H1​(∂Aλ)\partial:H_{2}(A^{\lambda},\partial A^{\lambda})\rightarrow H_{1}(\partial A^{\lambda}) with the homomorphism
⟨⋅,αλ⟩:H1​(∂Aλ)→ℝ.\langle\cdot,\alpha^{\lambda}\rangle:H_{1}(\partial A^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.
Thus we need to verify that ⟨⋅,αλ⟩\langle\cdot,\alpha^{\lambda}\rangle vanishes on the image of ∂\partial. Let γ\gamma be any loop in ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda}. After homotoping and possibly inverting the loop γ\gamma, we can assume that it is given by a finite concatenation of path segments of one of the following two types:
(A)
Path segments contained in ιλ​(Σ)\iota^{\lambda}(\Sigma), i.e. the fiber over 00 of the projection ∂+Aλ→[0,1]\partial_{+}A^{\lambda}\rightarrow[0,1].
(B)
Flow line segments of φtλ\varphi_{t}^{\lambda} starting at a point contained in the interior of ιλ​(Σ)\iota^{\lambda}(\Sigma) and ending at the next intersection point with ιλ​(Σ)\iota^{\lambda}(\Sigma).
Our goal is to show that the integral of αλ\alpha^{\lambda} over paths of type (A) or (B) vanishes. It follows from this assertion that ⟨⋅,αλ⟩\langle\cdot,\alpha^{\lambda}\rangle vanishes on all loops γ\gamma in ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda}. Let us begin with paths of type (A). It follows from property (b) that the restriction of the vector field XλX^{\lambda} to ιλ​(Σ)\iota^{\lambda}(\Sigma) is parallel to ∂s\partial_{s}. Therefore, the pull-back of αλ\alpha^{\lambda} to ιλ​(Σ)\iota^{\lambda}(\Sigma) vanishes. In particular, the integral of αλ\alpha^{\lambda} over type (A) paths is zero. Let us now turn to path segments of type (B). We define
uλ:[0,4]×Σ→Tλuλ​(t,p)≔φtλ​(ιλ​(p)).u^{\lambda}:[0,4]\times\Sigma\rightarrow T^{\lambda}\qquad u^{\lambda}(t,p)\coloneqq\varphi^{\lambda}_{t}(\iota^{\lambda}(p)).
Clearly, upλ≔uλ​(⋅,p)u_{p}^{\lambda}\coloneqq u^{\lambda}(\cdot,p) is a path of type (B) for every p∈Σp\in\Sigma. Since αλ\alpha^{\lambda} is closed in a neighbourhood of ∂Aλ\partial A^{\lambda} and the restriction of αλ\alpha^{\lambda} to ιλ​(Σ)\iota^{\lambda}(\Sigma) vanishes, the integral ∫[0,4](upλ)∗​αλ\int_{[0,4]}(u_{p}^{\lambda})^{*}\alpha^{\lambda} is independent of p∈Σp\in\Sigma. This implies that, for all p∈Σp\in\Sigma, we have
(4.3)
area⁡(Σ)​∫[0,4](upλ)∗​αλ=∫[0,4]×Σ(uλ)∗​αλ∧ω.\operatorname{area}(\Sigma)\int_{[0,4]}(u_{p}^{\lambda})^{*}\alpha^{\lambda}=\int_{[0,4]\times\Sigma}(u^{\lambda})^{*}\alpha^{\lambda}\wedge\omega.
Using that (uλ)∗​Ω=ω(u^{\lambda})^{*}\Omega=\omega, we obtain
(4.4)
(uλ)∗​αλ∧ω\displaystyle(u^{\lambda})^{*}\alpha^{\lambda}\wedge\omega
=\displaystyle=
(uλ)∗​(αλ∧Ω)\displaystyle(u^{\lambda})^{*}(\alpha^{\lambda}\wedge\Omega)
=\displaystyle=
(uλ)∗​(ιXλ​Ω∧Ω)\displaystyle(u^{\lambda})^{*}(\iota_{X^{\lambda}}\Omega\wedge\Omega)
=\displaystyle=
(uλ)∗​ιXλ​(12​Ω∧Ω).\displaystyle(u^{\lambda})^{*}\iota_{X^{\lambda}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Omega\wedge\Omega\right).
We compute
(4.5)
0\displaystyle 0
=\displaystyle=
dd​λ​vol⁡(Aλ)\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}\operatorname{vol}(A^{\lambda})
=\displaystyle=
∫∂AλιXλ​(12​Ω∧Ω)\displaystyle\int_{\partial A^{\lambda}}\iota_{X^{\lambda}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Omega\wedge\Omega\right)
=\displaystyle=
∫TλιXλ​(12​Ω∧Ω)\displaystyle\int_{T^{\lambda}}\iota_{X^{\lambda}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Omega\wedge\Omega\right)
=\displaystyle=
∫[0,4]×Σ(uλ)∗​ιXλ​(12​Ω∧Ω)\displaystyle\int_{[0,4]\times\Sigma}(u^{\lambda})^{*}\iota_{X^{\lambda}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Omega\wedge\Omega\right)
=\displaystyle=
∫[0,4]×Σ(uλ)∗​αλ∧ω\displaystyle\int_{[0,4]\times\Sigma}(u^{\lambda})^{*}\alpha^{\lambda}\wedge\omega
=\displaystyle=
area⁡(Σ)​∫[0,4](upλ)∗​αλ.\displaystyle\operatorname{area}(\Sigma)\int_{[0,4]}(u_{p}^{\lambda})^{*}\alpha^{\lambda}.
Here the first equality follows from assumption (2iii). The second equality is a consequence of Stokes’ theorem. The vector field XλX^{\lambda} is tangent to ∂SAλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda}. Thus the integral of ιXλ​(12​Ω∧Ω)\iota_{X^{\lambda}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Omega\wedge\Omega\right) over ∂SAλ\partial_{S}A^{\lambda} vanishes, which implies the third equality. The forth equality follows because uλu^{\lambda} is a parametrization of TλT^{\lambda}. The fifth equality is immediate from equation (4.4). Finally, the sixth equality follows from identity (4.3). Since equation (4.5) holds for all p∈Σp\in\Sigma, we conclude that the integral of αλ\alpha^{\lambda} over all paths of type (B) vanishes. This concludes Step 4 and therefore the proof of the lemma.
∎
Proof of Theorem 4.1.
It will be useful to introduce the following notation. For every ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian G:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝG:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}, we set
V⁡(G)≔∫[0,1]×𝔸G​𝑑t∧ωandA±​(G)≔∫01G⁡(t,∂±𝔸)​𝑑tV(G)\coloneqq\int\limits_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}Gdt\wedge\omega\quad\text{and}\quad A_{\pm}(G)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1}G(t,\partial_{\pm}\mathbb{A})dt
where ∂+𝔸={1}×𝕋\partial_{+}\mathbb{A}=\left\{1\right\}\times\mathbb{T} and ∂−𝔸={0}×𝕋\partial_{-}\mathbb{A}=\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{T}.
Step 1: Let us begin with the case that hh is constant and that the Hamiltonian HH satisfies V​(H)=A±​(H)V(H)=A_{\pm}(H). Fix an integer ℓ>0\ell>0 such that the conclusion of Theorem 3.1 holds. Pick a positive rational number α>0\alpha>0 not contained in 12​ℤ\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z} such that ℓ​α<12​min⁡h\ell\alpha<\frac{1}{2}\min h. Note that if HH is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to hh, then the Hamiltonian G≔H−V⁡(H)G\coloneqq H-V(H) satisfies the hypotheses of Corollary 3.2. Therefore, there exists a family of Hamiltonians (Gλ)λ∈[0,1](G^{\lambda})_{\lambda\in[0,1]} starting at G0=GG^{0}=G and satisfying all the assertions of Corollary 3.2. Set Hλ≔Gλ+V⁡(H)H^{\lambda}\coloneqq G^{\lambda}+V(H). The constant family of Hamiltonians equal to zero and the family (Hλ)λ∈[0,1](H^{\lambda})_{\lambda\in[0,1]} satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma 4.4. We conclude that D⁡(H)=D⁡(H0)D(H)=D(H^{0}) is symplectomorphic to D⁡(H1)D(H^{1}). Thus it suffices to construct a stratification of the latter subgraph.
It follows from Corollary 3.2 that the Hamiltonian H1H^{1} is given by
H1=V⁡(H)+#i=1ℓ​εi​ψi∗​HαH^{1}=V(H)+\#_{i=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}\psi_{i}^{*}H_{\alpha}
for signs εi∈{±1}\varepsilon_{i}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\} and Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms ψi∈Ham⁡(𝔸)\psi_{i}\in\operatorname{Ham}(\mathbb{A}). Let us define
Hi≔εi​ψi∗​Hα+min⁡(εi​ψi∗​Hα).H_{i}\coloneqq\varepsilon_{i}\psi_{i}^{*}H_{\alpha}+\operatorname{min}(\varepsilon_{i}\psi_{i}^{*}H_{\alpha}).
Note that each HiH_{i} is non-negative and vanishes exactly on one of the boundary components of 𝔸\mathbb{A}. It is straightforward to see that D⁡(Hi)D(H_{i}) is symplectomorphic to D⁡(Hα)D(H_{\alpha}). We have
H1=V⁡(H)−ℓ​α/2+#i=1ℓ​Hi.H^{1}=V(H)-\ell\alpha/2+\#_{i=1}^{\ell}H_{i}.
We inductively define
F0≔V⁡(H)−ℓ​α/2andFi≔Fi−1​#​Hi​for 1≤i≤ℓ.F_{0}\coloneqq V(H)-\ell\alpha/2\quad\text{and}\quad F_{i}\coloneqq F_{i-1}\#H_{i}\kern 5.0pt\text{for $1\leq i\leq\ell$}.
This induces a stratification of D⁡(H1)=D⁡(Fℓ)D(H^{1})=D(F_{\ell}) with ℓ+1\ell+1 top-dimensional strata
D⁡(F0)andD⁡(Fi−1,Fi)​for 1≤i≤ℓ.D(F_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad D(F_{i-1},F_{i})\kern 5.0pt\text{for $1\leq i\leq\ell$}.
By Lemma 4.3, the stratum D⁡(Fi−1,Fi)D(F_{i-1},F_{i}) is symplectomorphic to D⁡(Hi)D(H_{i}), which in turn is symplectomorphic to D⁡(Hα)D(H_{\alpha}). Thus all of these strata are of the desired form. Since F0F_{0} is constant, the stratum D⁡(F0)D(F_{0}) is of the desired form as well. Note that the rational number α=p/q\alpha=p/q was chosen independently of HH. Hence the uniform upper bound on the denominator qq is automatic. Moreover, the parameter c>0c>0 in the statement of the theorem is just equal to 11 for all strata. Finally, the constant CC of the stratum D⁡(F0)D(F_{0}) clearly admits a positive lower bound independent of HH. This concludes the proof of Theorem 4.1 in the case V​(H)=A±​(H)V(H)=A_{\pm}(H).
Step 2: Next, let us assume that
(4.6)
V⁡(H)=12​(A−​(H)+A+​(H))andA+​(H)−A−​(H)∈ℚ.V(H)=\frac{1}{2}(A_{-}(H)+A_{+}(H))\qquad\text{and}\qquad A_{+}(H)-A_{-}(H)\in\mathbb{Q}.
If A+​(H)=A−​(H)A_{+}(H)=A_{-}(H), this is precisely the case treated in Step 1, so we assume that A+​(H)≠A−​(H)A_{+}(H)\neq A_{-}(H). We may in addition assume that α≔A+​(H)−A−​(H)>0\alpha\coloneqq A_{+}(H)-A_{-}(H)>0. We stratify D⁡(H)D(H) into the following two pieces:
D⁡(Hα)andD⁡(Hα,H).D(H_{\alpha})\qquad\text{and}\qquad D(H_{\alpha},H).
The first of the two pieces is already of the desired form. By Lemma 4.3, the second piece is symplectomorphic to D⁡(H¯α​#​H)D(\overline{H}_{\alpha}\#H). It follows from assumption (4.6) and the definition of α\alpha that the Hamiltonian H¯α​#​H\overline{H}_{\alpha}\#H is precisely of the form considered in Step 1. Thus we may further stratify D⁡(H¯α​#​H)D(\overline{H}_{\alpha}\#H) as in Step 1. Note that while the parameters of the substrata of D⁡(H¯α​#​H)D(\overline{H}_{\alpha}\#H) admit uniform bounds independent of HH, this is not true for the stratum D⁡(Hα)D(H_{\alpha}) because α=A+​(H)−A−​(H)\alpha=A_{+}(H)-A_{-}(H) is determined by HH and may have arbitrarily large denominator. This will be rectified in the next step of the proof.
Step 3: Let us now turn to the general case. Consider numbers 0=x0<x1<x2<x3=10=x_{0}<x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}=1 and decompose the annulus 𝔸=[0,1]×𝕋\mathbb{A}=[0,1]\times\mathbb{T} into three smaller annuli 𝔸i≔[xi−1,xi]×𝕋\mathbb{A}_{i}\coloneqq[x_{i-1},x_{i}]\times\mathbb{T} where 1≤i≤31\leq i\leq 3. The area of 𝔸i\mathbb{A}_{i} is given by ai≔xi−xi−1a_{i}\coloneqq x_{i}-x_{i-1}. Or goal is to adjust the numbers xix_{i} and to construct a Hamiltonian G⁡(t,x,y)=G⁡(x)G(t,x,y)=G(x) only depending on xx such that the following properties hold. Let Gi≔G|[0,1]×𝔸iG_{i}\coloneqq G|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{i}} denote the restriction.
(1)
GG is C∞C^{\infty} close to h−12​min⁡hh-\frac{1}{2}\min h
(2)
A−​(H)−A−​(G)=A+​(H)−A+​(G)=V⁡(H)−V⁡(G)A_{-}(H)-A_{-}(G)=A_{+}(H)-A_{+}(G)=V(H)-V(G)
(3)
We have
V⁡(Gi)=12​ai​(A−​(Gi)+A+​(Gi))andαi≔A+​(Gi)−A−​(Gi)ai∈ℚ.V(G_{i})=\frac{1}{2}a_{i}(A_{-}(G_{i})+A_{+}(G_{i}))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha_{i}\coloneqq\frac{A_{+}(G_{i})-A_{-}(G_{i})}{a_{i}}\in\mathbb{Q}.
Moreover, we would like to have an upper bound on the minimal denominators of the rational numbers αi\alpha_{i} and a lower bound on the areas aia_{i}, both of which are uniform in HH.
Before turning to the construction of such a Hamiltonian GG, let us first describe how we use it to stratify D⁡(H)D(H). First, we stratify D⁡(H)D(H) into the following four pieces:
D⁡(G,H)andD⁡(Gi)​for 1≤i≤3D(G,H)\qquad\text{and}\qquad D(G_{i})\kern 5.0pt\text{for $1\leq i\leq 3$}
By Lemma 4.3, the first piece is symplectomorphic to D⁡(G¯​#​H)D(\overline{G}\#H). The Hamiltonian G¯​#​H\overline{G}\#H satisfies the assumption in Step 1 and we can therefore further stratify D⁡(G,H)D(G,H) into pieces of the desired form. Each of the pieces D⁡(Gi)D(G_{i}) is, up to scaling of the symplectic form, of the form considered in Step 2 and can thus be further stratified as well. The result is a stratification of D⁡(H)D(H) into pieces of the desired form. The parameters of the resulting strata admit uniform bounds in HH. For the substrata of D⁡(G,H)D(G,H) this follows from Step 1. For the substrata of D⁡(Gi)D(G_{i}) this follows from the uniform upper bound on the minimal denominators of αi\alpha_{i} and the uniform lower bound on aia_{i}.
It remains to construct GG. Recall that the Hamiltonian hh is given by h⁡(t,x,y)=a+b​xh(t,x,y)=a+bx. Let is pick a rational number α+\alpha_{+} slightly bigger than bb and a rational number α−\alpha_{-} slightly smaller than bb. Note that if HH is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to hh, then α≔A+​(H)−A−​(H)\alpha\coloneqq A_{+}(H)-A_{-}(H) will be contained in the interval (α−,α+)(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+}) and be uniformly bounded away from its entpoints. Let 0<w<α+−αα+−α−0<w<\frac{\alpha_{+}-\alpha}{\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{-}} be a parameter which is to be determined. Define
x1≔wandx2≔w+α−α−α+−α−x_{1}\coloneqq w\quad\text{and}\quad x_{2}\coloneqq w+\frac{\alpha-\alpha_{-}}{\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{-}}
and let g:[0,1]→ℝg:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be the unique continuous piecewise linear function such that
(1)
g​(0)=A−​(H)g(0)=A_{-}(H)
(2)
g​(1)=A+​(H)g(1)=A_{+}(H)
(3)
gg has slope α−\alpha_{-} on the intervals [x0,x1][x_{0},x_{1}] and [x2,x3][x_{2},x_{3}]
(4)
gg has slope α+\alpha_{+} on the interval [x1,x2][x_{1},x_{2}].
By taking HH sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to hh, we can make the difference between V⁡(H)V(H) and 12​(A−​(H)+A+​(H))\frac{1}{2}(A_{-}(H)+A_{+}(H)) arbitrarily small. In particular, the integral ∫01g⁡(x)​𝑑x\int_{0}^{1}g(x)dx will be bigger than V⁡(H)V(H) for ww near 00 and smaller than V⁡(H)V(H) for ww near α+−αα+−α−\frac{\alpha_{+}-\alpha}{\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{-}}. Thus there exists a unique value ww such that ∫01g⁡(x)​𝑑x=V⁡(H)\int_{0}^{1}g(x)dx=V(H). For HH sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to hh, this value ww is uniformly bounded away from the endpoints of the interval (0,α+−αα+−α−)(0,\frac{\alpha_{+}-\alpha}{\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{-}}). Let us pick a smoothing g~\tilde{g} of gg which has the following properties:
(1)
g~\tilde{g} agrees with gg at the points x0x_{0}, x1x_{1}, x2x_{2} and x3x_{3}
(2)
The integrals of g~\tilde{g} and gg agree on each of the intervals [x0,x1][x_{0},x_{1}], [x1,x2][x_{1},x_{2}] and [x2,x3][x_{2},x_{3}].
(3)
g~\tilde{g} is C∞C^{\infty} close to hh. Note that we can control this C∞C^{\infty} distance purely in terms of the inital pick of the rational numbers α±\alpha_{\pm} close to bb and uniformly among all HH sufficiently close to hh.
Now define G⁡(t,x,y)≔g~​(x)−12​min⁡hG(t,x,y)\coloneqq\tilde{g}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\min h. It is straightforward to check that this Hamiltonian has all desired properties.
∎
5. Symplectic ribbon complexes
We introduce the notion of a symplectic ribbon complex. The main result of this section, Theorem 5.4, states that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for all connected symplectic ribbon complexes consisting of balls and polydisks. This result plays a crucial role in our proof of Theorem B, as it allows us to deduce ellipsoid embedding stability of a symplectic 44-manifold from the existence of certain decompositions into balls and polydisks; see Section 6.
Recall that the 44-dimensional symplectic ellipsoid E⁡(a,b)E(a,b) of widths a,b>0a,b>0 is defined by
E⁡(a,b)≔{z∈ℂ2|π​|z1|2a+π​|z2|2b≤1}.E(a,b)\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\kern 5.0pt\Big|\kern 5.0pt\frac{\pi|z_{1}|^{2}}{a}+\frac{\pi|z_{2}|^{2}}{b}\leq 1\right\}.
The 44-dimensional ball of width a>0a>0 is the ellipsoid B4​(a)≔E⁡(a,a)B^{4}(a)\coloneqq E(a,a). The polydisk of widths a,b>0a,b>0 is given by
P⁡(a,b)≔{z∈ℂ2∣π​|z1|2≤a​and​π​|z2|2≤b}.P(a,b)\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid\pi|z_{1}|^{2}\leq a\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\pi|z_{2}|^{2}\leq b\right\}.
We define the symplectic ribbon R⁡(a)R(a) of width a>0a>0 to be
R⁡(a)≔[0,1]×B2​(a)R(a)\coloneqq[0,1]\times B^{2}(a)
where B2​(a)B^{2}(a) denotes the 22-dimensional disk of area aa. The area form on B2​(a)B^{2}(a) induces a closed 22-form ω\omega on R⁡(a)R(a) which is characterized by the requirements that the restriction of ω\omega to the fibers {∗}×B2​(a)\left\{*\right\}\times B^{2}(a) agrees with the area form on B2​(a)B^{2}(a) and that the contraction ι∂t​ω\iota_{\partial_{t}}\omega vanishes, where tt denotes the coordinate of the [0,1][0,1] factor. The leaves of the characteristic foliation on R⁡(a)R(a) induced by ω\omega are therefore simply the fibers [0,1]×{∗}[0,1]\times\left\{*\right\}. The ribbon R⁡(a)R(a) has two ends {j}×B2​(a)\left\{j\right\}\times B^{2}(a), where j∈{0,1}j\in\left\{0,1\right\}. We think of R⁡(a)R(a) as not carrying a preferred orientation, i.e. we ignore the obvious orientation of the interval [0,1][0,1].
Now consider a smooth 44-dimensional star-shaped domain XX and a symplectic ribbon RR. Let BB be one of its ends. An attaching map from this end to XX is simply a smooth embedding ι:B→∂X\iota:B\rightarrow\partial X such that the pull-back of the symplectic form on XX via ι\iota agrees with the standard area form on BB. We would also like to allow certain non-smooth star-shaped domains XX such as polydisks. In this case, we always assume that the image of an attaching map is disjoint from the non-smooth locus of the boundary of the domain.
Consider a finite collection of star-shaped domains and ribbons of variable widths. For some ribbon ends, fix an attaching map to one of the star-shaped domains. We require that the images of these attaching maps are pairwise disjoint. Moreover, we require that, for each ribbon, we have an attaching map for at least one of its ends. Take the disjoint union of all the star-shaped domains and ribbons and then take the quotient by gluing ribbon ends to the star-shaped domains via the chosen attaching maps. We call the resulting object a symplectic ribbon complex. Note that not all ribbon ends are required to be attached to some star-shaped domain. We call such an end a free end. The corresponding ribbon is called a free ribbon.
We can alternatively think of a symplectic ribbon complex as the following datum:
•
a collection 𝒳\mathcal{X} of finitely many star-shaped domains;
•
a collection 𝒟\mathcal{D} of finitely many disjoint symplectic disks in the boundaries of domains contained in 𝒳\mathcal{X};
•
a partition of 𝒟\mathcal{D} into individual disks and pairs of disks;
•
for each pair of disks, a diffeomorphism between them compatible with the restriction of the symplectic form to the disks.
Here the individual disks in the partition correspond to ribbons with a free end. The area-preserving diffeomorphism between a pair of disks corresponds to the map obtained by traversing a ribbon following the characteristic foliation. A symplectomorphism between two symplectic ribbon complexes is a symplectomorphism between the disjoint unions of the underlying shar-shaped domains which is compatible with the collections of disks, their partitions, and the transition maps between pairs of disks.
Example 5.1.
Consider an ellipsoid E⁡(a,b)E(a,b). For every 0<c<b0<c<b and for every angle θ∈ℝ/2​π​ℤ\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}, there exists a unique map
(5.1)
ι⁡(θ):B2​(c)→∂E⁡(a,b)\iota(\theta):B^{2}(c)\rightarrow\partial E(a,b)
satisfying the following properties: For j∈{1,2}j\in\left\{1,2\right\}, let prj:ℂ2→ℂ\operatorname{pr}_{j}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C} be the projection onto the jj-th factor. Then the composition pr2∘ι⁡(θ)\operatorname{pr}_{2}\circ\iota(\theta) is simply the inclusion of B2​(c)B^{2}(c) into B2​(b)B^{2}(b) and the image of the composition pr1∘ι⁡(θ)\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ\iota(\theta) is contained in the ray ℝ≥0⋅ei​θ\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot e^{i\theta}. An easy computation shows that the pull-back of the standard symplectic form on ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2} via ι⁡(θ)\iota(\theta) agrees with the standard area form on B2​(c)B^{2}(c). For each positive integer nn, let E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n) be the symplectic ribbon complex built from one copy of the ellipsoid E⁡(a,b)E(a,b) and nn copies of the ribbon R⁡(c)R(c) as follows: For each 0≤j<n0\leq j<n, attach a copy of R⁡(c)R(c) to ∂E⁡(a,b)\partial E(a,b) on one of its ends via the attaching map ι⁡(2​π​j/n)\iota(2\pi j/n). The complex E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n) has nn free ends. By construction, each of these free ends comes with a preferred identification with B2​(c)B^{2}(c). This preferred identification is not part of the datum of a symplectic ribbon complex, but we will occasionally make use of it below.
If we replace the collection of attaching angles 2​π​j/n2\pi j/n by any other collection of nn distinct attaching angles θ0<⋯<θn−1\theta_{0}<\dots<\theta_{n-1}, the resulting symplectic ribbon complex is symplectomorphic to E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n). Indeed, there exists a symplectomorphism of E⁡(a,b)E(a,b) which, for every 0≤j<n0\leq j<n, maps the image of ι⁡(θj)\iota(\theta_{j}) to the image of ι⁡(2​π​j/n)\iota(2\pi j/n). Note, however, that this isomorphism is not compatible with the preferred identifications of the free ribbon ends with B2​(c)B^{2}(c) mentioned above.
If E​(a,a)=B4​(a)E(a,a)=B^{4}(a) is a ball, let us abbreviate the resulting complex by B4​(a,c,n)B^{4}(a;c,n).
Example 5.2.
Consider a ribbon graph GG, i.e. a graph such that the incident edges of each vertex are cyclically ordered. Assume that the vertices of GG, except possibly for some of the leaves of GG, are labelled by positive real numbers. Let AA denote the labelling of GG. Fix real numbers 0<c<b0<c<b. Given these data, we can construct a symplectic ribbon complex K⁡(G,A,b,c)K(G,A,b,c) as follows: For each labelled vertex vv of GG, let ava_{v} denote its label, nvn_{v} the number of edges incident to vv, and take a copy of E⁡(av,b,c,nv)E(a_{v},b;c,n_{v}). Note that the free ends of E⁡(av,b,c,nv)E(a_{v},b;c,n_{v}) are naturally cyclically ordered. To each incident edge of vv, assign a free end of E⁡(av,b,c,nv)E(a_{v},b;c,n_{v}) respecting the cyclic order. Now glue together any pair of free ends assigned to the same edge via the identity map of B2​(c)B^{2}(c) to obtain a ribbon complex K⁡(G,A,b,c)K(G,A,b,c). The free ends of this ribbon complex correspond precisely to the unlabelled vertices of GG.
We observe that in contrast to Example 5.1, the angles θj\theta_{j} at which ribbons are attached to the ellipsoid E⁡(av,b)E(a_{v},b) can no longer be changed freely if the graph GG has cycles: Indeed, consider the simplest example of just one ribbon R⁡(c)R(c) attached to an ellipsoid E⁡(a,b)E(a,b) at both of its ends via ι⁡(θ0)\iota(\theta_{0}) and ι⁡(θ1)\iota(\theta_{1}) for two angles θ0<θ1\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}. The disk im⁡ι⁡(θ1)\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{1}) can be obtained from im⁡ι⁡(θ0)\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{0}) by flowing forward via the Reeb flow on ∂E⁡(a,b)\partial E(a,b) for some amount of time. This induces a transition map im⁡ι⁡(θ0)→im⁡ι⁡(θ1)\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{0})\rightarrow\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{1}) which assigns to each point in im⁡ι⁡(θ0)\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{0}) the first intersection point of the trajectory starting at that point with im⁡ι⁡(θ1)\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{1}). Traversing the ribbon following the characteristic foliation induces a map im⁡ι⁡(θ1)→im⁡ι⁡(θ0)\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{0}). Composing these two maps yields an area preserving diffeomorphism of im⁡ι⁡(θ0)\operatorname{im}\iota(\theta_{0}) which is a rotation by angle a⁡(θ1−θ0)b\frac{a(\theta_{1}-\theta_{0})}{b}. Clearly, this number is invariant under symplectomorphisms of ribbon complexes. On the other hand, if GG is a tree, then only the cyclic order of the ribbons attached to an ellipsoid matters and the angles can be varied freely.
Let (M4,ω)(M^{4},\omega) be a 44-dimensional symplectic manifold. A symplectic embedding of a symplectic ribbon complex KK into MM is an injective continuous map φ:K​↪𝑠​M\varphi:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M satisfying the following properties:
•
The restriction φX\varphi_{X} of φ\varphi to each domain XX of KK is a smooth symplectic embedding.
•
The restriction φR\varphi_{R} of φ\varphi to each ribbon RR of KK is a smooth embedding which pulls back the symplectic form on MM to the closed 22-from ω\omega on RR.
•
Suppose that RR is attached to ∂X\partial X at one of its ends. Then φR\varphi_{R} meets φX​(∂X)\varphi_{X}(\partial X) transversely, i.e. ∂tφR\partial_{t}\varphi_{R} is no-where tangent to φX​(∂X)\varphi_{X}(\partial X) along the image of the gluing region of XX and RR.
Remark 5.3.
Every symplectic ribbon complex KK admits a symplectic embedding into some symplectic 44-manifold. There is a neighbourhood theorem for symplectic embeddings of symplectic ribbon complexes: Let KK be a symplectic ribbon complex and let φ:K​↪𝑠​M\varphi:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M and φ′:K​↪𝑠​M′\varphi^{\prime}:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M^{\prime} be two symplectic embeddings into symplectic 44-manifolds MM and M′M^{\prime}. If the manifolds have bounary, we assume that the images of the embeddings are contained in the interior. Then there exist open neighbourhoods UU of φ⁡(K)\varphi(K) in MM and U′U^{\prime} of φ′​(K)\varphi^{\prime}(K) in M′M^{\prime} and a symplectomorphism ψ:U→U′\psi:U\rightarrow U^{\prime} such that φ′=ψ∘φ\varphi^{\prime}=\psi\circ\varphi. This can be proved using Gotay’s neighbourhood theorem for coisotropic submanifolds [Got82].
Let KK be a non-empty symplectic ribbon complex. The volume vol⁡(K)\operatorname{vol}(K) of KK is defined to be the sum of the volumes of its domains. Let (ai)i(a_{i})_{i} be the collection of all the Gromov widths of the domains of KK and let (bj)j(b_{j})_{j} be the collection of all widths of ribbons of KK. We define the width of KK to be the quantity
w⁡(K)≔min⁡{mini⁡ai,minj⁡bj}.w(K)\coloneqq\min\left\{\min_{i}a_{i},\min_{j}b_{j}\right\}.
Moreover, we define the volume normalized width by
w¯​(K)≔w⁡(K)vol⁡(K).\overline{w}(K)\coloneqq\frac{w(K)}{\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(K)}}.
Note that this quantity is invariant under scalings of the symplectic form on KK. For every a≥1a\geq 1, we define the ellipsoid embedding number paE​(K)p^{E}_{a}(K) of KK to be
paE​(K)≔supλvol⁡(E⁡(λ,λ​a))vol⁡(K)∈[0,1],p^{E}_{a}(K)\coloneqq\sup_{\lambda}\frac{\operatorname{vol}(E(\lambda,\lambda a))}{\operatorname{vol}(K)}\in[0,1],
where the supremum is taken over all λ>0\lambda>0 such that, for every symplectic embedding φ:K​↪𝑠​M\varphi:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M into the interior of a symplectic 44-manifold MM and for every open neighbourhood UU of φ⁡(K)\varphi(K) in MM, the ellipsoid E⁡(λ,λ​a)E(\lambda,\lambda a) symplectically embeds into UU.
Theorem 5.4.
Let KK be a non-empty connected symplectic ribbon complex built using only balls and polydisks. Then ellipsoid embedding stability holds for KK, i.e. there exists a finite number a0a_{0} such that paE​(K)=1p^{E}_{a}(K)=1 for all a≥a0a\geq a_{0}. Moreover, for every δ>0\delta>0, the number a0a_{0} can be chosen uniformly among all KK with w¯​(K)≥δ\overline{w}(K)\geq\delta.
The remainder of this section is concerned with the proof of Theorem 5.4.
Lemma 5.5.
For every e0≥1e_{0}\geq 1, there exists a0a_{0} such that
paE​(E⁡(1,e))=paE​(P⁡(1,e))=1p^{E}_{a}(E(1,e))=p^{E}_{a}(P(1,e))=1
for all 1≤e≤e01\leq e\leq e_{0} and all a≥a0a\geq a_{0}.
Proof.
The statement about paE​(E⁡(1,e))p^{E}_{a}(E(1,e)) is an immediate consequence of [BH13, Theorem 1.4]. The statement about paE​(P⁡(1,e))p^{E}_{a}(P(1,e)) can be proved via the same method. Indeed, a 44-dimensional ellipsoid EE symplectically embeds into a 44-dimensional polydisk PP if and only if ck​(E)≤ck​(P)c_{k}(E)\leq c_{k}(P) for all k>0k>0 where ckc_{k} denote the ECH capacities, see [Cri19]. One can therefore prove the assertion by analysing the formulas for ECH capacities of ellipsoids and polydisks. Alternatively, the statement also follows from [BHO16, Theorem 3] in combination with the fact that the problem of embedding an ellipsoid into a polydisk is equivalent to embedding a certain collection of balls associated to the weight sequence of the ellipsoid, see [Cri19]. Note that [BHO16, Theorem 3] applies to target manifolds which are pseudoballs, see [BHO16, Definition 1.3]. While polydisks are not pseudoballs, they are contained in the closure of the space of pseudoballs. The proof of [BHO16, Theorem 3] also applies to polydisks without changes.
∎
Lemma 5.6.
Let (G,A)(G,A) be a connected labelled ribbon graph and let 0<c<b0<c<b be two positive numbers. Let K=K⁡(G,A,b,c)K=K(G,A,b,c) be the ribbon complex constructed in Example 5.2. Define α≔∑vav\alpha\coloneqq\sum_{v}a_{v} where the sum runs over all labelled vertices of GG. Let nn denote the number of free ends of KK and let K′K^{\prime} denote the ribbon complex obtained from KK by deleting all ribbons with a free end. Then for every symplectic embedding φ:K​↪𝑠​M\varphi:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M into a symplectic 44-manifold MM, possibly with boundary, such that φ⁡(K′)⊂int⁡(M)\varphi(K^{\prime})\subset\operatorname{int}(M), every open neighbourhood UU of φ⁡(K′)\varphi(K^{\prime}) in MM, and every 0<γ<β≤c0<\gamma<\beta\leq c, there exists a symplectic embedding ψ:E⁡(α,β,γ,n)​↪𝑠​M\psi:E(\alpha,\beta;\gamma,n)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M such that the following is true:
(1)
The image ψ⁡(E⁡(α,β))\psi(E(\alpha,\beta)) is contained in UU.
(2)
For each free ribbon RR of KK, there exists exactly one free ribbon SS of E⁡(α,β,γ,n)E(\alpha,\beta;\gamma,n) such that ψ⁡(S)⊂φ⁡(R)\psi(S)\subset\varphi(R). Moreover, the image of the free end of SS under ψ\psi is contained in the image of the free end of RR under φ\varphi.
Proof.
After possibly deleting some ribbons of KK, we can assume that GG is a tree. Let us begin by constructing a special symplectic embedding φ:K​↪𝑠​ℂ2\varphi:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{2}. By Remark 5.3, it will be sufficient to prove the lemma for this specific embedding. Let us begin by embedding the ribbon tree GG into the complex plane ℂ\mathbb{C} such that all edges are straight line segments. For each labelled vertex vv, take a copy of B2​(av)B^{2}(a_{v}) centered at vv. After dilating the embedding of the tree GG if necessary, we can assume that the disks centered at two distinct vertices are disjoint and that no unlabelled vertex is contained in any of the disks. Moreover, we can assume that each edge only meets the disks centered at the vertices it is incident to. It is straightforward to construct a symplectic embedding φ:K​↪𝑠​ℂ2\varphi:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{2} such that the image φ⁡(K)\varphi(K) is the union of one copy of the ellipsoid E⁡(av,b)E(a_{v},b) for each labelled vertex vv, translated such that it is centered at {v}×{0}∈ℂ2\left\{v\right\}\times\left\{0\right\}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}, and all the sets of the form e×{B2​(c)}e\times\left\{B^{2}(c)\right\} where ee is an edge. Note that the projection pr1⁡(φ⁡(K))\operatorname{pr}_{1}(\varphi(K)) to the first factor of ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2} is precisely given by the above configuration of disks and line segments. In order to simplify notation, let us identify KK with its image under φ\varphi.
Fix an arbitrary open neighbourhood UU of K′K^{\prime} inside ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2} and numbers 0<γ<β≤c0<\gamma<\beta\leq c. Let D⊂ℂ≅ℂ×{0}D\subset\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{C}\times\left\{0\right\} be a topological disk which contains pr1⁡(K′)\operatorname{pr}_{1}(K^{\prime}) in its interior and which is contained in U∩(ℂ×{0})U\cap(\mathbb{C}\times\left\{0\right\}). For ε>0\varepsilon>0, Let Kε′K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon} denote the ribbon complex obtained from K′K^{\prime} by replacing each domain E⁡(av,b)E(a_{v},b) by the smaller domain E⁡(av,c+ε)E(a_{v},c+\varepsilon) while leaving the widths of all ribbons unchanged. If ε>0\varepsilon>0 is sufficiently small, it is possible to find a smooth non-negative function f:D→ℝ≥0f:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0} with the following properties:
(1)
ff vanishes on the boundary ∂D\partial D.
(2)
ff has precisely one local maximum and no other critical points.
(3)
The set
A(D,f)≔{(z1,z2)∈ℂ2∣z1∈D,π|z2|2≤f(z1)}A(D,f)\coloneqq\left\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid z_{1}\in D,\kern 5.0pt\pi|z_{2}|^{2}\leq f(z_{1})\right\}
is contained in UU and contains Kε′K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon} in its interior.
Choose an area preserving diffeomorphism ψ\psi of ℂ\mathbb{C} such that D~≔ψ⁡(D)\tilde{D}\coloneqq\psi(D) is a round disk cetered at the origin and such that f~≔f∘ψ−1\tilde{f}\coloneqq f\circ\psi^{-1} is rotation invariant. Clearly, ψ×idℂ\psi\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{C}} is a symplectomorphism mapping A⁡(D,f)A(D,f) onto A⁡(D~,f~)A(\tilde{D},\tilde{f}). Our goal is to show that A⁡(D~,f~)A(\tilde{D},\tilde{f}) contains the ellipsoid E⁡(α,β)E(\alpha,\beta). Once we know this, (ψ×idℂ)−1(\psi\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1} gives a symplectic embedding of E⁡(α,β)E(\alpha,\beta) into UU.
Consider a general ellipsoid E⁡(p,q)E(p,q). Then we have
(5.2)
area⁡({z∈B2​(p)∣area⁡(E⁡(p,q)∩{z}×ℂ)≥s})=p⁡(q−s)q.\operatorname{area}(\left\{z\in B^{2}(p)\mid\operatorname{area}(E(p,q)\cap\left\{z\right\}\times\mathbb{C})\geq s\right\})=\frac{p(q-s)}{q}.
It follows from this identity, the fact that Kε′K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon} is contained in A⁡(D,f)A(D,f), and the fact that ψ\psi is area preserving that
area⁡({z∈D~∣f~​(z)≥s})=area⁡({z∈D∣f⁡(z)≥s})≥∑vav​(c+ε−s)c+ε=α⁡(c+ε−s)c+ε≥a⁡(β−s)β.\operatorname{area}\left(\left\{z\in\tilde{D}\mid\tilde{f}(z)\geq s\right\}\right)=\operatorname{area}(\left\{z\in D\mid f(z)\geq s\right\})\geq\sum_{v}\frac{a_{v}(c+\varepsilon-s)}{c+\varepsilon}=\frac{\alpha(c+\varepsilon-s)}{c+\varepsilon}\geq\frac{a(\beta-s)}{\beta}.
Again using identity (5.2) and the fact that f~\tilde{f} is rotation invariant and non-increasing along the radial direction, we see that E⁡(α,β)E(\alpha,\beta) is contained in A⁡(D~,f~)A(\tilde{D},\tilde{f}).
Finally, we observe that it is straightforward to attach nn copies of the ribbon R⁡(γ)R(\gamma) to (ψ×idℂ)−1​(E⁡(α,β))(\psi\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(E(\alpha,\beta)) in such a way that property (2) in the statement of the lemma holds. The existence of the desired embedding of E⁡(α,β,γ,n)E(\alpha,\beta;\gamma,n) is an immediate consequence.
∎
Let 0<c<a0<c<a. Consider the ball B4​(a)⊂ℂ2B^{4}(a)\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} and let ρ:∂B4​(a)→ℂ​P1​(a)\rho:\partial B^{4}(a)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{1}(a) be the Hopf map. Here ℂ​P1​(a)\mathbb{C}P^{1}(a) denotes the 22-sphere of area aa. We call a symplectic embedding ψ:B2​(c)↪∂B4​(a)\psi:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial B^{4}(a) free if the composition ρ∘ψ:B2​(c)→ℂ​P1​(a)\rho\circ\psi:B^{2}(c)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{1}(a) is an embedding. We call a finite collection (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} of symplectic embeddings ψj:B2​(c)↪∂B4​(a)\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial B^{4}(a) free if the compositions ρ∘ψj:B2​(c)→ℂ​P1​(a)\rho\circ\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{1}(a) are pairwise disjoint embeddings.
Lemma 5.7.
Let 0<c<a0<c<a and n>0n>0. Then the symplectomorphism group of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) acts transitively on free nn-tuples (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} of symplectic embeddings ψj:B2​(c)↪∂B4​(a)\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial B^{4}(a).
Proof.
Consider two free nn-tuples of symplectic embeddings (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} and (ψj′)j(\psi_{j}^{\prime})_{j}. First note that the symplectomorphism group of ℂ​P1​(a)\mathbb{C}P^{1}(a) acts transitively on nn-tuples of pairwise disjoint symplectic embeddings of B2​(c)B^{2}(c). Any symplectomorphism of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) maps Hopf fibers on the boundary ∂B4​(a)\partial B^{4}(a) to Hopf fibers and therefore induces a symplectomorphism of ℂ​P1​(a)\mathbb{C}P^{1}(a). Conversely, any symplectomorphism of ℂ​P1​(a)\mathbb{C}P^{1}(a) lifts to a symplectomorphism of B4​(a)B^{4}(a). Thus we can find a symplectomorphism χ′\chi^{\prime} of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) such that ρ∘ψj′=ρ∘χ′∘ψj\rho\circ\psi_{j}^{\prime}=\rho\circ\chi^{\prime}\circ\psi_{j} for all jj. Consider two symplectic embeddings ψ,ψ′:B2​(c)↪∂B4​(a)\psi,\psi^{\prime}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial B^{4}(a) which are both free. If ρ∘ψ=ρ∘ψ′\rho\circ\psi=\rho\circ\psi^{\prime}, then ψ\psi and ψ′\psi^{\prime} are related by a symplectomorphism of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) whose support can be taken to be contained in an arbitrarily small neighbourhood of the preimage ρ−1​(im⁡(ρ∘ψ))\rho^{-1}(\operatorname{im}(\rho\circ\psi)). Using this observation, we can correct χ′\chi^{\prime} to a symplectomorphism χ\chi of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) which satisfies ψj′=χ∘ψj\psi_{j}^{\prime}=\chi\circ\psi_{j} for all jj.
∎
Proposition 5.8.
Let B⊂ℂ2B\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} be a ball and let nn be a non-negative integer. Then the following is true for every sufficiently small b>0b>0: Let 0<a0<a such that vol⁡(E⁡(a,b))<vol⁡(B)\operatorname{vol}(E(a,b))<\operatorname{vol}(B). Let 0<c<b0<c<b. For 0≤j<n0\leq j<n, let ψj:B2​(c)↪∂B\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial B be a symplectic embedding and assume that the collection (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} is free. Then there exists a symplectic embedding φ:E⁡(a,b,c,n)​↪𝑠​B\varphi:E(a,b;c,n)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B whose restriction to the jj-th free ribbon end of E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n) agrees with the embedding ψj\psi_{j}. Here we use the preferred identification of the free ribbon ends with B2​(c)B^{2}(c), see Example 5.1. Moreover, the image im⁡(φ)\operatorname{im}(\varphi) intersects ∂B\partial B precisely at the free ends and the intersection is transverse.
Before turning to the proof of Proposition 5.8, we need to establish some preliminary results.
Lemma 5.9.
Let a,b,β>0a,b,\beta>0 and suppose that the ellipsoid E=E⁡(a,b)⊂ℂ2E=E(a,b)\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} symplectically embeds into M=ℂ​P2​(β)M=\mathbb{C}P^{2}(\beta). Let C⊂MC\subset M be a complex line. If b<βb<\beta, then there exists a symplectic embedding φ:E​↪𝑠​M\varphi:E\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M such that φ−1​(C)=E∩({0}×ℂ)\varphi^{-1}(C)=E\cap(\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{C}).
Proof.
This is a special case of [BHO16, Lemma 1.6].
∎
Lemma 5.10.
Let a,b>0a,b>0 and consider the ellipsoid E⁡(a,b)E(a,b). Let 0<c<b0<c<b and r>1r>1. Then there exists a symplectic embedding φ:E⁡(a,b,c,1)​↪𝑠​E​(r​a,r​b)\varphi:E(a,b;c,1)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}E(ra,rb) such that the restriction of φ\varphi maps the free end of E⁡(a,b,c,1)E(a,b;c,1) into {0}×ℂ\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{C} and such that the image of φ\varphi does not intersect the set {0}×ℂ\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{C} in any other point.
Proof.
Consider the 33-dimensional half space H≔ℝ≥0×ℂH\coloneqq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{C}. One can find an explicit Hamiltonian diffeomorphism ψ\psi compactly supported in the interior of E⁡(r​a,r​b)E(ra,rb) such that the image of {0}×ℂ\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{C} is disjoint from E⁡(a,b)E(a,b), contained in HH, and of the form
ψ⁡({0}×ℂ)={(f⁡(z),z)∣z∈B2​(r​b)}\psi(\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{C})=\left\{(f(z),z)\mid z\in B^{2}(rb)\right\}
for some compactly supported non-negative function f:B2​(r​b)→ℝ≥0f:B^{2}(rb)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}. We can connect the boundary of E⁡(a,b)E(a,b) to the image ψ⁡({0}×ℂ)\psi(\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{C}) via a ribbon R⁡(c)R(c) contained in the half space HH. Finally, apply ψ−1\psi^{-1} to obtain the desired embedding of the ribbon complex E⁡(a,b,c,1)E(a,b;c,1).
∎
Proof of Proposition 5.8 in the case n≤1n\leq 1.
After scaling, we may assume that B=B4​(1)B=B^{4}(1) has symplectic width equal to 11.
The case n=0n=0 follows immediately from the well-known statement that a sufficiently skinny ellipsoid symplectically embeds into a ball if and only if the volume constraint is satisfied, see Lemma 5.5.
The case n=1n=1 of Proposition 5.8 follows from Lemmas 5.9 and 5.10. Indeed, let b>0b>0 be small and consider 0<c<b0<c<b and 0<a0<a such that vol⁡(E⁡(a,b))<vol⁡(B)\operatorname{vol}(E(a,b))<\operatorname{vol}(B). If bb is sufficiently small, then E⁡(a,b)E(a,b) symplectically embeds into ℂ​P2=ℂ​P2​(1)\mathbb{C}P^{2}=\mathbb{C}P^{2}(1) by the case n=0n=0. We can pick r>1r>1 sufficiently close to 11 such that E⁡(r​a,r​b)E(ra,rb) still embeds into ℂ​P2\mathbb{C}P^{2}. Fix a complex line S⊂ℂ​P2S\subset\mathbb{C}P^{2}. Combining Lemmas 5.9 and 5.10, we see that there exists a symplectic embedding E⁡(a,b,c,1)​↪𝑠​ℂ​P2E(a,b;c,1)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}\mathbb{C}P^{2} which maps the free ribbon end into SS and is otherwise disjoint from SS. Consider a map B→ℂ​P2B\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{2} which maps the interior of BB symplectomorphically onto the complement of SS and whose restriction to the boundary of BB is simply the Hopf map ρ:∂B→S\rho:\partial B\rightarrow S. Via this map, we can pull back the symplectic embedding E⁡(a,b,c,1)​↪𝑠​ℂ​P2E(a,b;c,1)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}\mathbb{C}P^{2} to a symplectic embedding φ:E⁡(a,b,c,1)​↪𝑠​B\varphi:E(a,b;c,1)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B. Clearly, φ\varphi embeds the free ribbon end of E⁡(a,b,c,1)E(a,b;c,1) into ∂B\partial B and the composition with the Hopf map ρ\rho is still an embedding. Since any other free embedding of B2​(c)B^{2}(c) into ∂B\partial B is related to this embedding via a symplectomorphism of BB by Lemma 5.7, this concludes the proof of Proposition 5.8 in the case n=1n=1.
∎
Lemma 5.11.
Let B⊂ℂ2B\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} be a ball and let nn be a non-negative integer. Then for all sufficiently small b>0b>0 the following is true: Consider 0<c<b0<c<b and 0<a0<a such that vol⁡(E⁡(a,b))<vol⁡(B)\operatorname{vol}(E(a,b))<\operatorname{vol}(B). Let ψ:B2​(c)↪∂B\psi:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial B be a free embedding. Let D1,…,Dn−1⊂∂BD_{1},\dots,D_{n-1}\subset\partial B be embedded disks such that, for each jj, the restriction of the Hopf map ρ\rho to DjD_{j} is an embedding with the same image as ρ∘ψ\rho\circ\psi. Assume that the disks im⁡(ψ),D1,…,Dn−1\operatorname{im}(\psi),D_{1},\dots,D_{n-1} are pairwise disjoint. Then there exists a symplectic embedding φ:E⁡(a,b,c,n)​↪𝑠​B\varphi:E(a,b;c,n)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B such that the restriction of φ\varphi to one free ribbon end of E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n) is given by ψ\psi and each DjD_{j} for 1≤j≤n−11\leq j\leq n-1 is the image of a free ribbon end of E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n).
Proof.
By the special case n=1n=1 of Proposition 5.8 we just proved, we can find a symplectic embedding φ:E⁡(a,b,c,1)​↪𝑠​B\varphi:E(a,b;c,1)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B such that the restriction of φ\varphi to the free ribbon end of E⁡(a,b,c,1)E(a,b;c,1) is given by ψ\psi. Let R=R⁡(c)R=R(c) denote the ribbon of E⁡(a,b,c,1)E(a,b;c,1). For ε>0\varepsilon>0, we define the thickened ribbon
Rε≔[0,ε]×R=[0,ε]×[0,1]×B2​(c)R_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq[0,\varepsilon]\times R=[0,\varepsilon]\times[0,1]\times B^{2}(c)
and equip it with the symplectic form d​s∧d​t+ωds\wedge dt+\omega, where (s,t)(s,t) are the coordinates on [0,ε]×[0,1][0,\varepsilon]\times[0,1] and ω\omega is the standard area form on B2​(c)B^{2}(c). If ε>0\varepsilon>0 is sufficiently small, we can find a symplectic embedding α:Rε​↪𝑠​B\alpha:R_{\varepsilon}\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B such that the restriction of α\alpha to {0}×R≅R\left\{0\right\}\times R\cong R agrees with the restriction of φ\varphi to RR and α\alpha maps the two boundary strata [0,ε]×{j}×B2​(c)[0,\varepsilon]\times\left\{j\right\}\times B^{2}(c) for j∈{0,1}j\in\left\{0,1\right\} into ∂φ⁡(E⁡(a,b))\partial\varphi(E(a,b)) and ∂B\partial B, respectively. By the assumption that ρ⁡(Dj)=im⁡(ρ∘ψ)\rho(D_{j})=\operatorname{im}(\rho\circ\psi), up to applying a symplectomorphism of BB and relabelling the disks DjD_{j}, we can assume that Dj=α⁡({j/(n−1)}×{1}×B2​(c))D_{j}=\alpha(\left\{j/(n-1)\right\}\times\left\{1\right\}\times B^{2}(c)) for 1≤j≤n−11\leq j\leq n-1. For each jj, let us attach the ribbon α⁡({j/(n−1)}×R)\alpha(\left\{j/(n-1)\right\}\times R) to φ⁡(E⁡(a,b))\varphi(E(a,b)). As observed in Example 5.1, the resulting ribbon complex is symplectomorphic to E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n). It is the image of a symplectic embedding E⁡(a,b,c,n)​↪𝑠​BE(a,b;c,n)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B satisfying all the desired properties at the free ribbon ends.
∎
Consider the triangle T⊂ℝ2T\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} with vertices (0,0)(0,0), (1,0)(1,0) and (0,1)(0,1) and the square Q⊂ℝ2Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} with vertices (0,0)(0,0), (1,0)(1,0), (1,1)(1,1) and (0,1)(0,1). It is well-known that the interior of the Lagrangian product T×LQT\times_{L}Q is symplectomorphic to the interior of the ball B4​(1)B^{4}(1) (see [Tra95, Proposition 5.2]). Even though T×LQT\times_{L}Q is not smooth everywhere, one can still make sense of a generalized Reeb flow on its boundary. The dynamics of this generalized Reeb flow is equivalent to the Minkowski billard dynamics of TT and QQ (see [GT02] and [AO14, §2.4]). In the following, we give a detailed description of the generalized Reeb flow. For this purpose, let us abbreviate the edges of TT by tlt_{l}, tbt_{b} and tdt_{d}, as indicated in Figure 5.1. Moreover, let qlq_{l}, qbq_{b}, qrq_{r} and qtq_{t} denote the edges of QQ.

Figure 5.1. The triangle TT and the square QQ.
The boundary of T×LQT\times_{L}Q has seven 33-dimensional faces, three of the form t∗×Qt_{*}\times Q and four of the form T×q∗T\times q_{*}. On each 33-dimensional face, the characteristic foliation is very simple: It is parallel to J0​νJ_{0}\nu, where ν\nu is the unit outer normal vector of the face. There are twelve 22-dimensional faces t∗×q∗t_{*}\times q_{*}. Four of these 22-dimensional faces, tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l}, tl×qrt_{l}\times q_{r}, tb×qbt_{b}\times q_{b} and tb×qtt_{b}\times q_{t}, are Lagrangian, i.e. the restriction of the symplectic form vanishes on them. The remaining eight 22-dimensional faces are symplectic. At each of the symplectic 22-dimensional faces, the Reeb flow simply transitions between the two 33-dimensional faces meeting there. At the Lagrangian 22-dimensional faces, the generalized Reeb flow is discontinuous. The Reeb flow admits a very simple description, see Figure 5.2.

Let us for example start at the symplectic 22-face tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l}. At this 22-face, the Reeb flow enters the 33-face tl×Qt_{l}\times Q. It traverses tl×Qt_{l}\times Q horizontally from left to right, until it meets the 22-face tl×qrt_{l}\times q_{r}, where it leaves tl×Qt_{l}\times Q and enters T×qrT\times q_{r}. Clearly, the transition map from tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l} to tl×qrt_{l}\times q_{r} is affine linear. As indicated in Figure 5.2, the Reeb flow passes through all the symplectic 22-faces and then ends up back at tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l}. The return map is simply given by the identity.
Let 0<c<10<c<1. Let D⊂tl×qlD\subset t_{l}\times q_{l} be a smooth disk of area cc obtained by first taking a square inside tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l} of area cc and with the same center as tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l} and then slightly smoothing its corners. Using the Reeb flow, we produce copies of DD contained in the interiors of t∗×Qt_{*}\times Q as follows. Let vv and hh denote the vertical and horizontal line segments dividing the square QQ into two rectangles of equal areas. Moreover, let dd denote the diagonal of QQ connecting the vertices (0,1)(0,1) and (1,0)(1,0). If we start at tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l}, the Reeb flow meets each of the cross sections tl×vt_{l}\times v and tb×ht_{b}\times h exactly once before it returns to tl×qlt_{l}\times q_{l}. Similarly, it meets the cross section td×dt_{d}\times d exactly twice, each time at a different half of td×dt_{d}\times d. We define D~\tilde{D} to be the intersection of all Reeb trajectories starting at DD with the union of the cross sections tl×vt_{l}\times v, tb×ht_{b}\times h and td×dt_{d}\times d. The set D~\tilde{D} consists of four copies of DD.
Lemma 5.12.
Let c<a<1c<a<1. Then there exists a symplectic embedding B4​(a,c,4)​↪𝑠​T×LQB^{4}(a;c,4)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}T\times_{L}Q which maps the four free ribbon ends of B4​(a,c,4)B^{4}(a;c,4) exactly to the four components of D~\tilde{D}.
Proof.
It is well known that B4​(a)B^{4}(a) admits a symplectic embedding into the interior of T×LQT\times_{L}Q, see [Tra95]. What we need to show is that there exists an embedding whose restriction to the boundary of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) is sufficiently tame such that we can insert four ribbons of width cc starting at the boundary of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) and ending at the four components of D~\tilde{D}. In order to see that this is possible, let us recall the following symplectic embedding construction from [Sch03, §2] and [Sch05a, §4.1]. Let U⊂ℝ2U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be the open ball of area 11 centered at 00 and let V⊂ℝ2V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be an open axis parallel square or area 11. A family ℒ\mathcal{L} of loops in VV is called admissible if there exists a diffeomorphism β:U∖{0}→V∖{p}\beta:U\setminus\left\{0\right\}\rightarrow V\setminus\left\{p\right\} for some point p∈Vp\in V such that circles in UU centered at 00 are mapped to members of ℒ\mathcal{L} and the restriction of β\beta to a neighbourhood of 00 is a translation. Schlenk proves that given an admissible family ℒ\mathcal{L}, there exists a symplectomorphism φ:U→V\varphi:U\rightarrow V mapping circles centered at 00 to members of ℒ\mathcal{L}, see [Sch03, Lemma 2.5] or [Sch05a, Lemma 4.2]. One can arrange the family ℒ\mathcal{L} such that the image of the restriction of the symplectomorphism φ×φ:U×U→V×V\varphi\times\varphi:U\times U\rightarrow V\times V to B4​(a)B^{4}(a) is an arbitrarily close approximation of a​T×LQaT\times_{L}Q, see Figure 5.3. Here a​TaT is the scaling of TT by a factor aa.
Figure 5.3. A special admissible family of loops ℒ\mathcal{L}.
For a careful choice of the family of loops ℒ\mathcal{L}, the generalized Reeb orbits on the boundary of a​T×LQaT\times_{L}Q away from the Lagrangian 22-faces are approximated arbitrarily well by images of Hopf circles on the boundary of B4​(a)B^{4}(a). Note that a​T×LQaT\times_{L}Q is symplectomorphic to a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q. Let us translate a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q such that it is contained in the interior of T×LQT\times_{L}Q. Then the above embedding construction yields an embedding of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) into the interior of T×LQT\times_{L}Q which approximates a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q arbitrarily well and has the property that generalized Reeb orbits on a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q away from the Lagrangian 22-strata are approximated by images of Hopf circles.
Inside the boundary of a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q we have a collection E~\tilde{E} of four disks of area cc each which is constructed in the same way as D~\tilde{D}. The disks E~\tilde{E} can be constructed in such a way that it is possible to insert four copies of the ribbon R⁡(c)R(c) in the complement of a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q connecting the components of E~\tilde{E} to the corresponding components of D~\tilde{D}. Since the image of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) under our symplectic embedding approximates a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q arbitrarily well and generalized Reeb orbits on the boundary of a​T×La​Q\sqrt{a}T\times_{L}\sqrt{a}Q which intersect E~\tilde{E} are approximated by images of Hopf circles, we can modify the four ribbons in such a way that they connect the components of D~\tilde{D} to the boundary of the image of B4​(a)B^{4}(a) and give rise to the desired embedding of B⁡(a,c,4)B(a;c,4).
∎
Proof of Proposition 5.8 in the case n≥2n\geq 2.
We assume that B=B4​(1)B=B^{4}(1). For simplicity, let us first treat the case n=2n=2. The ball BB admits a full packing by four copies of B4​(1/2)B^{4}(1/2). Such a packing can be seen explicitly as follows. As mentioned above, the interior of BB is symplectomorphic to the interior of the Lagrangian product T×LQT\times_{L}Q. We divide TT into four triangles T0,T1,T2,T3T_{0},T_{1},T_{2},T_{3} as indicated in Figure 5.4. This induces a decomposition of T×LQT\times_{L}Q into four pieces Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q, the interior of each of which is symplectomorphic to the interior of B4​(1/2)B^{4}(1/2).

Recall that we introduced, for every 0<γ<10<\gamma<1, a subset D~\tilde{D} of the boundary of T×LQT\times_{L}Q consisting of four disks of symplectic area γ\gamma each. The same construction yields, for every jj and every 0<γ<1/20<\gamma<1/2, a subset D~j\tilde{D}_{j} of the boundary of Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q consisting of four disks of area γ\gamma each. These disks can be arranged such that, for 1≤j≤31\leq j\leq 3, we have
D~0∩((T0∩Tj)×LQ)=D~j∩((T0∩Tj)×LQ),\tilde{D}_{0}\cap((T_{0}\cap T_{j})\times_{L}Q)=\tilde{D}_{j}\cap((T_{0}\cap T_{j})\times_{L}Q),
i.e. the components of D~0\tilde{D}_{0} and D~j\tilde{D}_{j} match at the interface between T0×LQT_{0}\times_{L}Q and Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q.
Let b>0b>0 be a small positive number. If necessary, we will further shrink bb over the course of the proof. Let 0<c<b0<c<b be arbitrary and let a>0a>0 be such that vol⁡(E⁡(a,b))<vol⁡(B)\operatorname{vol}(E(a,b))<\operatorname{vol}(B). Our goal is to construct a symplectic embedding E⁡(a,b,c,2)​↪𝑠​BE(a,b;c,2)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B mapping the two free ends into the boundary ∂B\partial B such that the resulting collection of two disk embeddings is free.
If bb is sufficiently small, we may pick positive numbers 0<γ<α0<\gamma<\alpha such that vol⁡(E⁡(a,b))<4​vol⁡(B4​(α))<1\operatorname{vol}(E(a,b))<4\operatorname{vol}(B^{4}(\alpha))<1 and γ>b\gamma>b. For 0≤j≤30\leq j\leq 3, let D~j\tilde{D}_{j} be the subset of the boundary of Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q mentioned above consisting of four disks of area γ\gamma each. Note that L0L_{0} and LjL_{j} have one matching pair of free ends if j∈{1,2}j\in\left\{1,2\right\} and two matching pairs of free ends if j=3j=3. We build a ribbon complex LL embedded in T×LQT\times_{L}Q by taking the union ⋃jLj\bigcup_{j}L_{j} and gluing all matching pairs of free ends.
By Lemma 5.11, after possibly shrinking bb, we can find f>bf>b and a symplectic embedding E⁡(a/4,f,b,4)​↪𝑠​B4​(α)E(a/4,f;b,4)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}B^{4}(\alpha) which maps the jj-th free end of E⁡(a/4,f,b,4)E(a/4,f;b,4) into the image of the map ι⁡(2​π​j/4):B2​(γ)→∂B4​(α)\iota(2\pi j/4):B^{2}(\gamma)\rightarrow\partial B^{4}(\alpha) for 0≤j≤30\leq j\leq 3. This implies that we can also embed E⁡(a/4,f,b,4)E(a/4,f;b,4) into B4​(α,γ,4)B^{4}(\alpha;\gamma,4) in such a way that the four free ends of E⁡(a/4,f,b,4)E(a/4,f;b,4) are contained in the four free ends of B4​(α,γ,4)B^{4}(\alpha;\gamma,4): simply elongate each ribbon of E⁡(a/4,f,b,4)E(a/4,f;b,4) by attaching an appropriate ribbon which is contained in the corresponding ribbon of B4​(α,γ,4)B^{4}(\alpha;\gamma,4). Take a copy K0K_{0} of E⁡(a/4,f,b,4)E(a/4,f;b,4). By the above discussion, we can embed it into the complex LL in such a way that the ellipsoid part of K0K_{0} is mapped into the ball component of LL contained in T0×LQT_{0}\times_{L}Q and the four free ribbon ends are mapped to the boundaries of the three remaining ball components of LL. Two free ends of K0K_{0} are mapped to the boundry of the ball component of LL inside T3×LQT_{3}\times_{L}Q and one free end is mapped to the boundary of the ball component inside Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q for j∈{1,2}j\in\left\{1,2\right\}. We pick one of the two ribbons of K0K_{0} whose end is contained in the ball component of LL inside T3×LQT_{3}\times_{L}Q and delete it. The resulting ribbon complex is still denoted by the same symbol K0K_{0}.
By the case n=1n=1 of Proposition 5.8, we may take a copy K3K_{3} of E⁡(a/4,f,b,1)E(a/4,f;b,1) and embed it into the ball component of LL contained in T3×LQT_{3}\times_{L}Q in such a way that the free end of K3K_{3} is mapped to the boundary of this ball component via exactly the same map as one the the three free ends of K0K_{0}. Similarly, by Lemma 5.11, we may take a copy KjK_{j} of E⁡(a/4,f,b,2)E(a/4,f;b,2) for j∈{1,2}j\in\left\{1,2\right\} and embed it into the ball component of LL contained in Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q in such a way that one of the two free ends of KjK_{j} is mapped to the boundary of the ball component via exactly the same map as one of the free ends of K0K_{0} and the image of the other free end of KjK_{j} is contained in the attaching region of one of the the ribbons of LL connecting to the diagonal part of the boundary of Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q. We elongate the latter ribbon of KjK_{j} inside the ribbon of LL such that its free end is mapped to the boundary of Tj×LQT_{j}\times_{L}Q.
We form a ribbon complex KK by taking the union ⋃jKj\bigcup_{j}K_{j} and gluing all matching pairs of free ends. See Figure 5.5 for a schematic of LL and KK. The resulting complex KK is symplectomorphic to the complex K⁡(G,A,f,b)K(G,A,f,b) constructed in Example 5.2 where (G,A)(G,A) is the labelled ribbon graph displayed in Figure 5.6.

T×LQ→B(μ1,μ2,θ1,θ2)↦(μ1π​e2​π​i​θ1,μ2π​e2​π​i​θ2).T\times_{L}Q\rightarrow B\qquad(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mapsto(\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{1}}{\pi}}e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}},\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{2}}{\pi}}e^{2\pi i\theta_{2}}).
