Before turning to the proof of Theorem 5.4, we need to state and prove a version of Proposition 5.8 for polydisks. Consider a polydisk P=P⁡(a1,a2)P=P(a_{1},a_{2}). Then the smooth locus of the boundary of PP has two components
∂1P=int⁡(B2​(a1))×∂B2​(a2)and∂2P=∂B2​(a1)×int⁡(B2​(a2)).\partial_{1}P=\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a_{1}))\times\partial B^{2}(a_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{2}P=\partial B^{2}(a_{1})\times\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a_{2})).
Let i∈{1,2}i\in\left\{1,2\right\}. Note that we have a projection pri:∂iP→int⁡(B2​(ai))\operatorname{pr}_{i}:\partial_{i}P\rightarrow\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a_{i})). Let c>0c>0 be a positive real number. A symplectic embedding ψ:B2​(c)↪∂iP\psi:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial_{i}P is called free if the composition pri∘ψ:B2​(c)→int⁡(B2​(ai))\operatorname{pr}_{i}\circ\psi:B^{2}(c)\rightarrow\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a_{i})) is an embedding. Let nn be a non-negative integer. Consider symplectic embeddings ψj:B2​(c)↪∂iP\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial_{i}P for 1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n. We call the tuple (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} free if the compositions pri∘ψj\operatorname{pr}_{i}\circ\psi_{j} are pairwise disjoint symplectic embeddings. If (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} is a tuple of symplectic embeddings ψj:B2​(c)↪∂1P∪∂2P\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial_{1}P\cup\partial_{2}P, we call it free if and only if the two subcollections of embeddings into ∂1P\partial_{1}P and ∂2P\partial_{2}P are free.
Lemma 5.13.
Let (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} and (ψj′)j(\psi^{\prime}_{j})_{j} be two free collection of symplectic embeddings ψj:B2​(c)↪∂1P∪∂2P\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial_{1}P\cup\partial_{2}P. Then there exists a symplectomorphism χ\chi of PP preserving ∂1P\partial_{1}P and ∂2P\partial_{2}P and satisfying ψj′=χ∘ψj\psi^{\prime}_{j}=\chi\circ\psi_{j} for all jj if and only if, for every jj, the images of ψj\psi_{j} and ψj′\psi^{\prime}_{j} are contained in the same component of ∂1P∪∂2P\partial_{1}P\cup\partial_{2}P.
Proof.
If there exists such a symplectomorphism χ\chi, then clearly the images of ψj\psi_{j} and ψj′\psi_{j}^{\prime} must be contained in the same component of ∂1P∪∂2P\partial_{1}P\cup\partial_{2}P for all jj.
In order to show the converse direction, first note that the compactly supported symplectomorphism group of int⁡(B2​(ai))\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a_{i})) acts transitively on nn-typles of pairwise disjoint symplectic embedings of B2​(c)B^{2}(c) into int⁡(B2​(ai))\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a_{i})) for every n>0n>0. Since any compactly supported symplectomorphism of int⁡(B2​(ai))\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a_{i})) lifts to a symplectomorphism of PP preserving ∂1P\partial_{1}P and ∂2P\partial_{2}P, we can find such a symplectomorphism χ′\chi^{\prime} with the property that pri∘ψj′=pri∘χ′∘ψj\operatorname{pr}_{i}\circ\psi_{j}^{\prime}=\operatorname{pr}_{i}\circ\chi^{\prime}\circ\psi_{j} for every jj and ii such that the images of ψj\psi_{j} and ψj′\psi_{j}^{\prime} are contained in ∂iP\partial_{i}P. Finally, observe that if ψ,ψ′:B2​(c)→∂iP\psi,\psi^{\prime}:B^{2}(c)\rightarrow\partial_{i}P are to free symplectic embeddings such that pri∘ψ=pri∘ψ′\operatorname{pr}_{i}\circ\psi=\operatorname{pr}_{i}\circ\psi^{\prime}, then ψ\psi and ψ′\psi^{\prime} are related by a symplectomorphism of PP which is supported near pri−1⁡(im⁡(pri∘ψ))\operatorname{pr}_{i}^{-1}(\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{pr}_{i}\circ\psi)). This shows the existence of the desired symplectomorphism χ\chi with the property that ψj′=χ∘ψj\psi_{j}^{\prime}=\chi\circ\psi_{j}.
∎
Proposition 5.14.
Let PP be a 44-dimensional symplectic polydisk and let nn be a non-negative integer. Then there exists b0>0b_{0}>0 such that the following is true for every 0<b≤b00<b\leq b_{0}: Let 0<a0<a such that vol⁡(E⁡(a,b))<vol⁡(P)\operatorname{vol}(E(a,b))<\operatorname{vol}(P). Let 0<c<b0<c<b. For 0≤j<n0\leq j<n, let ψj:B2​(c)↪∂1P∪∂2P\psi_{j}:B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial_{1}P\cup\partial_{2}P be a symplectic embedding into the smooth locus of ∂P\partial P and assume that the collection (ψj)j(\psi_{j})_{j} is free. After possibly permuting the indices of ψj\psi_{j}, there exists a symplectic embedding φ:E⁡(a,b,c,n)​↪𝑠​P\varphi:E(a,b;c,n)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}P whose restriction to the jj-th free ribbon end of E⁡(a,b,c,n)E(a,b;c,n) agrees with the embedding ψj\psi_{j}. Here we use the preferred identification of the free ribbon ends with B2​(c)B^{2}(c), see Example 5.1. Moreover, the image im⁡(φ)\operatorname{im}(\varphi) intersects ∂P\partial P precisely at the free ends and the intersection is transverse.
For fixed n>0n>0 and a compact subset C⊂ℝ>02C\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}, one can choose b0b_{0} uniformly for all polydisks P⁡(x,y)P(x,y) with (x,y)∈C(x,y)\in C.
Lemma 5.15.
Let a,b,α,β>0a,b,\alpha,\beta>0 and suppose that the ellipsoid E=E⁡(a,b)⊂ℂ2E=E(a,b)\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} symplectically embeds into M=ℂ​P1​(α)×ℂ​P1​(β)M=\mathbb{C}P^{1}(\alpha)\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}(\beta). Define C≔{∗}×ℂ​P1​(β)C\coloneqq\left\{*\right\}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}(\beta). If b<βb<\beta, then there exists a symplectic embedding φ:E​↪𝑠​M\varphi:E\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M such that φ−1​(C)=E∩({0}×ℂ)\varphi^{-1}(C)=E\cap(\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{C}).
Proof.
In contrast to Lemma 5.9, which is an analogous statement with target manifold ℂ​P2\mathbb{C}P^{2} instead of ℂ​P1×ℂ​P1\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}, we cannot directly apply [BHO16, Lemma 1.6] because this result is only stated for ℂ​P2\mathbb{C}P^{2}. However, as we sketch below, the proof of [BHO16, Lemma 1.6], which relies on the symplectic blow up construction for ellipsoid embeddings introduced in [McD09] and the singular inflation techniques of [MO15], readily adapts to show Lemma 5.15.
The strategy of the proof of [BHO16, Lemma 1.6] is the following. For τ>0\tau>0 sufficiently small, there clearly exists an embedding τ​E​↪𝑠​M\tau E\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M intersecting the symplectic sphere CC in the desired way. Blowing up this ellipsoid embedding yields a nodal symplectic sphere configuration SS inside a suitable blowup M^\hat{M} of MM. Since τ​E\tau E is in special position with respect to CC, one also obtains a sympelctic surface C^\hat{C} inside M^\hat{M} which is a proper transform of CC and intersects SS positively and transverely. The embedding of the full ellipsoid EE is obtained by changing the symplectic areas of the sphere components of SS via symplectic inflation relative to the singular symplectic surface S∪C^S\cup\hat{C}. In order to carry out this inflation, one needs an embedded symplectic surface intersecting S∪C^S\cup\hat{C} positively and transversely and representing a suitable homology class with positive self intersection number. In the proof of [BHO16, Lemma 1.6], the existence of such a symplectic surface is deduced from [MO15, Corollary 1.2.17], which deals with blow ups of ℂ​P2\mathbb{C}P^{2}. In the present setting of M=ℂ​P1​(α)×ℂ​P1​(β)M=\mathbb{C}P^{1}(\alpha)\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}(\beta), one can obtain the desired symplectic surface from [MO15, Theorem 1.2.16], which more generally applies to blow ups of arbitrary rational or ruled symplectic manifolds.
∎

The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition 5.8. The case n=0n=0 is immediate from Lemma 5.5. The case n=1n=1 is deduced from Lemma 5.10 in combination with Lemma 5.15.

Let b>0b>0 be sufficiently small and a>0a>0 such that vol⁡(E⁡(a,b))<vol⁡(P)\operatorname{vol}(E(a,b))<\operatorname{vol}(P). Here we use Lemma 5.12 and its polydisk analogue. Then we connect matching free ribbons at the interfaces between neighbouring cells. A schematic of the resulting ribbon complex LL is depicted in Figure 5.11.

This uses the case n=1n=1 of Proposition 5.14 and Lemma 5.11. Composition with an appropriate embedding S×LQ​↪𝑠​PS\times_{L}Q\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}P yields an embedding of KK into PP mapping the free ribbon ends into ∂P\partial P via a free collection of nn embeddings B2​(c)↪∂1P∪∂2PB^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\partial_{1}P\cup\partial_{2}P. We can build KK such that the number of free ribbon ends of KK which land in ∂1P\partial_{1}P (resp. ∂2P\partial_{2}P) matches the number of embeddings ψi\psi_{i} with image in ∂1P\partial_{1}P (resp. ∂2P\partial_{2}P). Moreover, we can arrange the parameters of KK such that Lemma 5.6 yields an embedding E⁡(a,b,c,n)​↪𝑠​PE(a,b;c,n)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}P, again mapping the free ribbon ends into ∂1P∪∂2P\partial_{1}P\cup\partial_{2}P via a free collection of maps. By Lemma 5.13 we can assume that these maps precisely agree with the ψi\psi_{i}, after possibly permuting the indices of ψi\psi_{i}.
Finally, observe that if n>0n>0 is fixed and (x,y)(x,y) range over a compact subset C⊂ℝ>02C\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}, then the parameters of all polydisks and balls in our division of S×LQS\times_{L}Q are uniformly bounded away from zero. Therefore, it ultimately follows from Lemma 5.5 that the threshold b0b_{0} can be chosen uniformly.

Proof of Theorem 5.4.
Let us normalized the volume of KK to be equal to 11. In this situation, we have w​(K)=w¯​(K)w(K)=\overline{w}(K). Let δ>0\delta>0 and assume that w⁡(K)≥δw(K)\geq\delta.
Observe that we can bound the total number of domains of KK from above in terms of δ\delta. After possibly removing some ribbons, we can assume that the graph underlying the ribbon complex KK is a tree. In this situation, we can in addition bound the total number of ribbons of KK from above in terms of δ\delta. After possibly shrinking the widths of the ribbons of KK, we can assume that, for every domain XX of KK, the collection of all attaching maps of ribbons attached to XX is free. Note that we can carry out this simplification in such a way that the new width w⁡(K)w(K) can be bounded from below just in terms of δ\delta.
For each domain XX of KK, let nXn_{X} denote the number of ribbons attached to XX. Let us choose b>0b>0 sufficiently small such that, for every domain XX of KK, the assertion of Proposition 5.8 holds for the pair (X,nX)(X,n_{X}) if XX is a ball and the assertion of Proposition 5.14 holds if XX is a polydisk. Moreover, we choose bb smaller than any width of a ribbon of KK. Note that this choice of bb can be made to depend only on δ\delta. The reason is that the parameters of the balls, polydisks, and ribbons of KK are bounded away from zero in terms of δ\delta and that all nXn_{X} are bounded from above in terms of δ\delta. Let ε>0\varepsilon>0 be arbitrary. Pick a positive number cc such that b−ε<c<bb-\varepsilon<c<b. For every domain XX of KK, pick aX>0a_{X}>0 such that vol⁡(X)−ε<vol⁡(E⁡(aX,b))<vol⁡(X)\operatorname{vol}(X)-\varepsilon<\operatorname{vol}(E(a_{X},b))<\operatorname{vol}(X).
Consider a domain XX of KK. Let (ψj)1≤j≤nX(\psi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n_{X}} denote the collection of attaching maps of all the ribbons attached to XX. Pick a symplectic embedding φX:E⁡(aX,b,c,nX)​↪𝑠​X\varphi_{X}:E(a_{X},b;c,n_{X})\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}X which maps the free ribbon ends into ∂X\partial X via the restricted attaching maps ψj|B2​(c)\psi_{j}|_{B^{2}(c)}. Such a symplectic embedding exists by Proposition 5.8 if XX is a ball and by Proposition 5.14 if XX is a polydisk. We connect the complexes E⁡(aX,b,c,nX)E(a_{X},b;c,n_{X}) to a single complex LL as follows. Let R=[0,1]×B2​(d)R=[0,1]\times B^{2}(d) be a ribbon of KK. Consider one of its ends {i}×B2​(d)\left\{i\right\}\times B^{2}(d). Let XX be the domain of KK this end is attached to. The image of one of the free ends of E⁡(aX,b,c,nX)E(a_{X},b;c,n_{X}) is contained in the end {i}×B2​(d)\left\{i\right\}\times B^{2}(d), which we identify with its image in ∂X\partial X. The restriction of φX\varphi_{X} to the free end B2​(c)B^{2}(c) of E⁡(aX,b,c,nX)E(a_{X},b;c,n_{X}) is simply given by inclusion B2​(c)↪{i}×B2​(d)B^{2}(c)\hookrightarrow\left\{i\right\}\times B^{2}(d). Let us glue the free end to the end {i}×B2​(c)\left\{i\right\}\times B^{2}(c) of the ribbon [0,1]×B2​(c)⊂R[0,1]\times B^{2}(c)\subset R. We perform this gluing at all the free ends of all the complexes E⁡(aX,b,c,nX)E(a_{X},b;c,n_{X}) and let LL denote the resulting complex. By construction, this complex LL symplectically embeds into KK. Moreover, it is a complex of the form considered in Lemma 5.6.
Now suppose that φ:K​↪𝑠​M\varphi:K\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}M is a symplectic embedding and let UU be an open neighbourhood of φ⁡(K)\varphi(K). By Lemma 5.6, the ellipsoid E⁡(a,c)E(a,c) symplectically embeds into UU. Letting ε\varepsilon tend to zero, we see that pa/bE​(K)=1p^{E}_{a/b}(K)=1. Note that a/ba/b can be bounded from above just in terms of δ\delta. It therefore follows from Lemma 5.5 that there exists a threshold a0a_{0} only depending on δ\delta such that peE​(K)=1p^{E}_{e}(K)=1 for all e≥a0e\geq a_{0}.
∎
6. Tame packings by balls and polydisks
In this section, we introduce the notion of a tame packing by balls and polydisks. The motivation behind this definition is that the existence of a tame packing by balls and polydisks implies ellipsoid embedding stability. This is an easy corollary of Theorem 5.4. We also introduce the stronger notion of a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks. The advantage of this stronger notion is that if a symplectic manifold XX can be decomposed into pieces which admit ∂\partial-tame packings by balls and polydisks, then so does XX. Using Theorem 4.1, we show that, for every ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian HH on the annulus 𝔸\mathbb{A} which is a C∞C^{\infty} small perturbation of a strictly positive affine Hamiltonian, the subgraph D⁡(H)D(H) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks and therefore satisfies ellipsoid embedding stability; see Corollary 6.6. We extend this result to Hamiltonians HH which are allowed to vanish on one boundary component of 𝔸\mathbb{A}. This is the content of Theorem 6.7.
Let MM be a symplectic 44-manifold of finite volume. We say that MM admits a tame packing by balls and polydisks (or simply a tame packing) if there exists δ>0\delta>0 such that, for every ε>0\varepsilon>0, there exists a connected symplectic ribbon complex KK, all of whose domains are balls or polydisks, which admits a symplectic embedding into int⁡(M)\operatorname{int}(M), and which satisfies w⁡(K)≥δw(K)\geq\delta and vol⁡(K)≥vol⁡(M)−ε\operatorname{vol}(K)\geq\operatorname{vol}(M)-\varepsilon.
The following is an immediate corollary of Theorem 5.4.
Corollary 6.1.
Let MM be a symplectic 44-manifold of finite volume. If MM admits a tame packing by balls and polydisks, then ellipsoid embedding stability holds for MM, i.e. paE​(M)=1p_{a}^{E}(M)=1 for all sufficiently large aa.
Let MM be a compact symplectic 44-manifold with boundary and corners. We say that MM admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks (or simply a ∂\partial-tame packing) if there exists an open and dense subset U⊂∂MU\subset\partial M contained in the smooth locus of the boundary of MM with the following property: For every finite collection 𝒟\mathcal{D} of pairwise disjoint closed symplectic disks contained UU, there exists δ>0\delta>0 such that, for every ε>0\varepsilon>0, there exists a connected symplectic ribbon complex KK, all of whose domains are balls or polydisks, which admits a symplectic embedding into MM such that the images of the free ribbon ends of KK are precisely given by the disks 𝒟\mathcal{D}, and which satisfies w⁡(K)≥δw(K)\geq\delta and vol⁡(K)≥vol⁡(M)−ε\operatorname{vol}(K)\geq\operatorname{vol}(M)-\varepsilon.
Lemma 6.2.
Let MM be a compact connected symplectic 44-manifold with boundary and corners. Suppose that MM admits a stratification 𝒯\mathcal{T} such that each top-dimensional stratum of 𝒯\mathcal{T} admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks. Then MM admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks.
Proof.
For each top-dimensional stratum TT of 𝒯\mathcal{T}, pick an open and dense subset UT⊂∂TU_{T}\subset\partial T as in the definition of a ∂\partial-tame packing. Let U⊂∂MU\subset\partial M be the intersection with ∂M\partial M of the union of all the sets UTU_{T}. The set UU is open and dense in ∂M\partial M. Let 𝒟⊂U\mathcal{D}\subset U be an arbitrary collection of pairwise disjoint symplectic disks. Our goal is to fill the volume of MM up to arbitrarily small error by a connected symplectic ribbon complex with free ribbon ends at the disks 𝒟\mathcal{D} and with width bounded away from zero. For any pair of distinct top-dimensional strata TT and T′T^{\prime} of 𝒯\mathcal{T} which meet at a stratum SS of codimension 11, pick a closed symplectic disk contained in S∩UT∩UT′S\cap U_{T}\cap U_{T^{\prime}}. Let ℰ\mathcal{E} denote the resulting collection of symplectic disks. For each top-dimensional stratum TT of 𝒯\mathcal{T}, we can fill arbitrarily much of the volume of TT by a connected symplectic ribbon complex KTK_{T} with free ends at the disks ∂T∩(𝒟∪ℰ)\partial T\cap(\mathcal{D}\cup\mathcal{E}) and with width bounded away from zero. Now connect all the ribbon complexes KTK_{T} to a single connected ribbon complex KK by gluing all pairs of free ribbon ends meeting at disks in ℰ\mathcal{E}. The free ends of KK are precisely the disks 𝒟\mathcal{D}. Moreover, we can arrange KK to fill the volume of MM up to arbitrarily small error while keeping the width w⁡(K)w(K) bounded away from zero.
∎
Example 6.3.
For a>0a>0, let Δ⁡(a)⊂ℝ2\Delta(a)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} denote the triangle with vertices (0,0)(0,0), (a,0)(a,0) and (0,a)(0,a). Let Q≔[0,1]2Q\coloneqq[0,1]^{2} denote the square. Then the Lagrangian product Δ⁡(a)×LQ\Delta(a)\times_{L}Q, whose interior is symplectomorphic to the interior of the ball B4​(a)B^{4}(a), admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. Indeed, recall from the proof of Lemma 5.12 that the ball B4​(r​a)B^{4}(ra) symplectically embeds into Δ⁡(a)×LQ\Delta(a)\times_{L}Q for every r∈(0,1)r\in(0,1). Moreover, the image of the embedding can be arranged to approximate Δ⁡(r​a)×Lr​Q\Delta(\sqrt{r}a)\times_{L}\sqrt{r}Q, translated into the interior of Δ⁡(a)×LQ\Delta(a)\times_{L}Q, arbitrarily well. Let 𝒟\mathcal{D} be a collection of symplectic disks in the smooth locus of the boundary of Δ⁡(a)×LQ\Delta(a)\times_{L}Q. For every r∈(0,1)r\in(0,1) sufficiently close to 11, we can connect the disks 𝒟\mathcal{D} to the boundary of the symplectically embedded ball B4​(r​a)B^{4}(ra) via a disjoint collection of symplectic ribbons. We can view the result as an embedding of a symplectic ribbon complex KK consisting of a single ball and ribbons whose free ends are mapped to the disks 𝒟\mathcal{D}. As rr approaches 11, the volume of KK tends to the volume of Δ⁡(a)×LQ\Delta(a)\times_{L}Q, while the width w⁡(K)w(K) remains bounded away from 00, showing that Δ⁡(a)×LQ\Delta(a)\times_{L}Q admits a ∂\partial-tame packing.
Example 6.4.
For a,b>0a,b>0, let □⁡(a,b)⊂ℝ2\square(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} denote the rectangle with vertices (0,0)(0,0), (a,0)(a,0), (a,b)(a,b) and (0,b)(0,b). Again, let Q=[0,1]2Q=[0,1]^{2} denote the square. Then the Lagrangian product □⁡(a,b)×LQ\square(a,b)\times_{L}Q, whose interior is symplectomorphic to the interior of the polydisk P⁡(a,b)P(a,b), admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. In order to see this, note that we can alternatively view □⁡(a,b)×LQ\square(a,b)\times_{L}Q as the symplectic product □⁡(a,1)×□⁡(b,1)\square(a,1)\times\square(b,1). For r∈(0,1)r\in(0,1), pick a symplectic embedding B2​(r​a)​↪𝑠​□​(a,1)B^{2}(ra)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}\square(a,1) whose image is a rectangle with slightly rounded corners contained in the interior of □⁡(a,1)\square(a,1). Pick an analogous embedding B2​(r​b)​↪𝑠​□​(b,1)B^{2}(rb)\overset{s}{\hookrightarrow}\square(b,1). The product of these two embeddings yields a symplectic embedding of P⁡(r​a,r​b)P(ra,rb) into □⁡(a,1)×□⁡(b,1)\square(a,1)\times\square(b,1). As in Example 6.3, we can connect the image of this symplectic embedding to any given collection of symplectic disks in the smooth locus of the boundary of □⁡(a,b)×LQ\square(a,b)\times_{L}Q via disjoint symplectic ribbons. Letting rr tend to 11, we see that □⁡(a,b)×LQ\square(a,b)\times_{L}Q admits a ∂\partial-tame packing.
Example 6.5.
For a,b>0a,b>0, let Δ⁡(a,b)⊂ℝ2\Delta(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} denote the triangle with vertices (0,0)(0,0), (a,0)(a,0) and (0,b)(0,b). We claim that if a/ba/b is rational, then the Lagrangian product Δ⁡(a,b)×LQ\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}Q, whose interior is symplectomorphic to the interior of the ellipsoid E⁡(a,b)E(a,b), admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. First, we argue that Δ⁡(a,b)×L𝕋2\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} has a ∂\partial-tame packing, where 𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2} is the 22-torus obtained from the square QQ by identifying opposite sides. Recall that for a/ba/b rational, one can decompose Δ⁡(a,b)\Delta(a,b) into finitely many triangles TiT_{i}, each of which can be obtained from a triangle of the form Δ⁡(ai)\Delta(a_{i}) by translations and the action of SL⁡(2,ℤ)\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z}) (see e.g. [McD09, §3.1]). This yields a stratification of Δ⁡(a,b)×L𝕋2\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} with top-dimensional strata given by Ti×L𝕋2T_{i}\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2}. Each of these strata is symplectomorphic to Δ⁡(ai)×L𝕋2\Delta(a_{i})\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2}. Recall from Example 6.3 that Δ⁡(ai)×LQ\Delta(a_{i})\times_{L}Q admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. In view of the natural inclusion int⁡(Δ⁡(ai)×LQ)↪Δ⁡(ai)×L𝕋2\operatorname{int}(\Delta(a_{i})\times_{L}Q)\hookrightarrow\Delta(a_{i})\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2}, this implies that Δ⁡(ai)×L𝕋2\Delta(a_{i})\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} and hence Ti×L𝕋2T_{i}\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} has a ∂\partial-tame packing. Lemma 6.2 thus implies that that Δ⁡(a,b)×𝕋2\Delta(a,b)\times\mathbb{T}^{2} admits a ∂\partial-tame packing as well.
It is well-known that Δ⁡(r​a,r​b)×L𝕋2\Delta(ra,rb)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} symplectically embeds into Δ⁡(a,b)×LQ\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}Q for all r∈(0,1)r\in(0,1). Explicit embeddings can be obtained via a variant of the symplectic embedding construction from [Sch03, §2] and [Sch05a, §4.1] which we reviewed in the proof of Lemma 5.12. For s>0s>0, let 𝔸⁡(s)≔[0,s]×𝕋\mathbb{A}(s)\coloneqq[0,s]\times\mathbb{T} denote the annulus of area ss. Note that we can regard the Lagrangian product Δ⁡(a,b)×L𝕋2\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} as a subset of the symplectic product 𝔸⁡(a)×𝔸⁡(b)\mathbb{A}(a)\times\mathbb{A}(b). Similarly, we can regard the Lagrangian product Δ⁡(a,b)×LQ\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}Q as a subset of the symplectic product □⁡(a,1)×□⁡(b,1)\square(a,1)\times\square(b,1). Consider a family of loops ℒa\mathcal{L}_{a} in the complement of a line segment LaL_{a} in the rectangle int⁡(□⁡(a,1))\operatorname{int}(\square(a,1)) as depicted in Figure 6.1. Then there exists a symplectomorphism φa:int⁡(𝔸⁡(a))→int⁡(□⁡(a,1))∖La\varphi_{a}:\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{A}(a))\rightarrow\operatorname{int}(\square(a,1))\setminus L_{a} which maps circles of the form {x}×𝕋\left\{x\right\}\times\mathbb{T} in 𝔸⁡(a)\mathbb{A}(a) to members of ℒa\mathcal{L}_{a}. Let φb:int⁡(𝔸⁡(b))→int⁡(□⁡(b,1))∖Lb\varphi_{b}:\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{A}(b))\rightarrow\operatorname{int}(\square(b,1))\setminus L_{b} be the analogous symplectomorphism arising from a family of loops ℒb\mathcal{L}_{b} in the rectangle int⁡(□⁡(b,1))\operatorname{int}(\square(b,1)). Now consider the triangle Δ⁡(r​a,r​b)\Delta(ra,rb) and slightly translate it off the axes into the interior of Δ⁡(a,b)\Delta(a,b). Let Δ′​(r​a,r​b)\Delta^{\prime}(ra,rb) denote the resulting triangle. We can arrange the families of loops ℒa\mathcal{L}_{a} and ℒb\mathcal{L}_{b} such that the image of Δ′​(r​a,r​b)×L𝕋2\Delta^{\prime}(ra,rb)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} under φa×φb\varphi_{a}\times\varphi_{b} approximates Δ⁡(r​a,r​b)×LQ\Delta(ra,rb)\times_{L}Q arbitrarily well. This yields the desired embedding of Δ⁡(r​a,r​b)×L𝕋2\Delta(ra,rb)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} into Δ⁡(a,b)×Q\Delta(a,b)\times Q. Given a collection of symplectic disks 𝒟\mathcal{D} in the smooth locus of the boundary of Δ⁡(a,b)×LQ\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}Q, we can connect them to symplectic disks in the boundary of the image of the symplectic embedding of Δ⁡(r​a,r​b)×L𝕋2\Delta(ra,rb)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} via symplectic ribbons. Since we already know that Δ⁡(r​a,r​b)×L𝕋2\Delta(ra,rb)\times_{L}\mathbb{T}^{2} admits a ∂\partial-tame packing, we can conclude that the same is true for Δ⁡(a,b)×LQ\Delta(a,b)\times_{L}Q.

Figure 6.1. A special admissible family of loops ℒa\mathcal{L}_{a} in □⁡(a,1)∖La\square(a,1)\setminus L_{a}.
Corollary 6.6.
In the setting of Theorem 4.1, the subgraph D⁡(H)D(H) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks. Therefore, ellipsoid embedding stability holds for D⁡(H)D(H).
Proof.
Theorem 4.1 yields a stratification of D⁡(H)D(H) into subgraphs of the form D⁡(Hα|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(c))D(H_{\alpha}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(c)}) for rational α\alpha and D⁡(C|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(c))D(C|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(c)}). Cutting the annulus 𝔸⁡(c)=[0,c]×𝕋\mathbb{A}(c)=[0,c]\times\mathbb{T} along the line segment [0,c]×{0}[0,c]\times\left\{0\right\} turns the former type of stratum into Δ⁡(c,α​c)×LQ\Delta(c,\alpha c)\times_{L}Q and the latter type into □⁡(c,C)×LQ\square(c,C)\times_{L}Q. By Examples 6.5 and 6.4, both Lagrangian products admit ∂\partial-tame packings. Thus by Lemma 6.2 the same is true for D⁡(H)D(H).
∎
Theorem 6.7.
Let h:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝh:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be a Hamiltonian of the form h⁡(t,x,y)=a​xh(t,x,y)=ax for a real number a>0a>0. Let H:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝH:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be a ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian which vanishes on [0,1]×{0}×𝕋[0,1]\times\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{T}. If HH is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to hh, then D⁡(H)D(H) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks.
Proof.
We divide the annulus 𝔸\mathbb{A} into three smaller annuli
𝔸0≔[0,13]×𝕋𝔸1≔[13,23]×𝕋𝔸2≔[23,1]×𝕋.\mathbb{A}_{0}\coloneqq[0,\frac{1}{3}]\times\mathbb{T}\qquad\mathbb{A}_{1}\coloneqq[\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3}]\times\mathbb{T}\qquad\mathbb{A}_{2}\coloneqq[\frac{2}{3},1]\times\mathbb{T}.
Pick a rational number α\alpha close to a2\frac{a}{2}. Note that both HαH_{\alpha} and H​#​H¯αH\#\overline{H}_{\alpha} are C∞C^{\infty} close to h/2h/2. We may therefore chose a Hamiltonian G:[0,1]×𝔸→ℝG:[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} such that
•
G=H​#​H¯αG=H\#\overline{H}_{\alpha} in a neighbourhood of [0,1]×𝔸0[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{0}
•
G=HαG=H_{\alpha} in a neighbourhood of [0,1]×𝔸2[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{2}
•
GG is C∞C^{\infty} close to h/2h/2.
We divide D⁡(H)D(H) into two pieces D⁡(G)D(G) and D⁡(G,H)D(G,H). By Lemma 6.2, it suffices to show that each of these two pieces D⁡(G)D(G) and D⁡(G,H)D(G,H) has a ∂\partial-tame packing.
By Lemma 4.3, D⁡(G,H)D(G,H) is symplectomorphic to D⁡(G¯​#​H)D(\overline{G}\#H), so it suffices to find a ∂\partial-tame packing for D⁡(G¯​#​H)D(\overline{G}\#H). On the annulus 𝔸0\mathbb{A}_{0}, we have
G¯​#​H=Hα​#​H¯​#​H=Hα.\overline{G}\#H=H_{\alpha}\#\overline{H}\#H=H_{\alpha}.
We stratify D⁡(G¯​#​H)D(\overline{G}\#H) into two pieces D⁡(G¯​#​H|[0,1]×𝔸0)=D⁡(Hα|[0,1]×𝔸0)D(\overline{G}\#H|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{0}})=D(H_{\alpha}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{0}}) and D⁡(G¯​#​H|[0,1]×(𝔸1∪𝔸2))D(\overline{G}\#H|_{[0,1]\times(\mathbb{A}_{1}\cup\mathbb{A}_{2})}). The former piece admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by Example 6.5. The latter piece is precisely of the form considered in Theorem 4.1 and therefore admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by Corollary 6.6. Applying again Lemma 6.2, we see that D⁡(G¯​#​H)D(\overline{G}\#H) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing.
Let us now turn to D⁡(G)D(G). Define FF by
F≔min⁡{G,2​α3}.F\coloneqq\min\left\{G,\frac{2\alpha}{3}\right\}.
We stratify D⁡(G)D(G) into D⁡(F)D(F) and clos⁡(int⁡D⁡(F,G))\operatorname{clos}(\operatorname{int}D(F,G)). Note that the latter stratum is symplectomorphic to D⁡(Hα|[0,1]×𝔸0)D(H_{\alpha}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{0}}) and hence admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. It remains to deal with D⁡(F)D(F). On a neighbourhood of [0,1]×𝔸2[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{2}, the Hamiltonian FF is autonomous. This is not necessarily the case outside of this neighbourhood, but it is possible to construct a Hamiltonian F~\tilde{F} which is C∞C^{\infty} close to FF, agrees with FF on a neighbourhood of [0,1]×𝔸2[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{2}, satisfies F~t=F~0\tilde{F}_{t}=\tilde{F}_{0} for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1] sufficiently close to the endpoints of [0,1][0,1], and has the property that D⁡(F)D(F) and D⁡(F~)D(\tilde{F}) are symplectomorphic. Since F~t=F~0\tilde{F}_{t}=\tilde{F}_{0} for tt close to 00 and 11, the Hamiltonian F~\tilde{F} descends to a 11-periodic Hamiltonian defined on 𝕋×𝔸\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{A}. Let us write F~|𝕋×𝔸\tilde{F}|_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{A}} to refer to this 11-periodic Hamiltonian.
We claim that D⁡(F~|𝕋×𝔸)D(\tilde{F}|_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{A}}) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. Indeed, consider the symplectomorphism
σ:(ℝ×𝕋)2→(ℝ×𝕋)2(s,t,x,y)↦(x,y,s,t)\sigma:(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})^{2}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})^{2}\qquad(s,t,x,y)\mapsto(x,y,s,t)
swapping the two factors. The image of D⁡(F~|𝕋×𝔸)D(\tilde{F}|_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{A}}) under σ\sigma is of the form D⁡(E)D(E) for a ∂\partial-admissible Hamiltonian E:𝕋×𝔸⁡(2​α/3)→ℝE:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{A}(2\alpha/3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R} which is a C∞C^{\infty} small perturbation of
e:𝕋×𝔸⁡(2​α/3)→ℝe⁡(t,x,y)=1−x2​α.e:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{A}(2\alpha/3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad e(t,x,y)=1-\frac{x}{2\alpha}.
By Corollary 6.6, D⁡(E|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(2​α/3))D(E|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(2\alpha/3)}) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. In view of the natural inclusion
int⁡(D⁡(E|[0,1]×𝔸⁡(2​α/3)))⊂D⁡(E),\operatorname{int}(D(E|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}(2\alpha/3)}))\subset D(E),
the same is true for D⁡(E)D(E) and hence for D⁡(F~|𝕋×𝔸)D(\tilde{F}|_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{A}}).
It remains to deduce that D⁡(F~)D(\tilde{F}) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. This follows from an argument similar to the one explained in Example 6.5. Set r≔2​α3r\coloneqq\frac{2\alpha}{3}. Then D⁡(F~)D(\tilde{F}) is a subset of the symplectic product 𝔸⁡(r)×𝔸\mathbb{A}(r)\times\mathbb{A} and D⁡(F~|[0,1]×𝔸)D(\tilde{F}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}) is a subset of □⁡(r,1)×𝔸\square(r,1)\times\mathbb{A}. As in Example 6.5, we obtain a symplectomorphism φ\varphi between int⁡(𝔸⁡(r))\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{A}(r)) and the complement of a line segment in □⁡(r,1)\square(r,1), see Figure 6.1. The image of int⁡(D⁡(F~))\operatorname{int}(D(\tilde{F})) under φ×id𝔸\varphi\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}} is an approximation of D⁡(F~|[0,1]×𝔸)D(\tilde{F}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}). Let Z⊂int⁡(D⁡(F~))Z\subset\operatorname{int}(D(\tilde{F})) be a domain conformally symplectomorphic to D⁡(F~)D(\tilde{F}) obtained by slightly pushing the boundary of D⁡(F~)D(\tilde{F}) inwards. Given a collection of disks in a suitable open and dense subset of the boundary of D⁡(F~|[0,1]×𝔸)D(\tilde{F}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}), we can connect them to the image of ZZ under φ×id𝔸\varphi\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}} via symplectic ribbons. Since we already know that ZZ admits a ∂\partial-tame packing, we can conclude that the same is true for D⁡(F~|[0,1]×𝔸)D(\tilde{F}|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}}).
∎
7. Symplectic frusta
In view of Lemma 6.2 and Corollary 6.1, in order to show that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for a symplectic 44-manifold MM, we need to decompose MM into pieces which admit ∂\partial-tame packings by balls and polydisks. We introduce the notion of a perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts, which will be technically convenient for this purpose. The main result of this section, Theorem 7.3, states that each perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks. This is a relatively straightforward consequence of Corollary 6.6 and Theorem 6.7.
Let a,b,c>0a,b,c>0 be positive real numbers and suppose that b<cb<c. Define the Hamiltonian
Ha,b,c:[0,1]×B2​(c)→ℝHa,b,c​(t,p)≔a⋅min⁡{1,c−π​|p|2c−b}.H_{a,b,c}:[0,1]\times B^{2}(c)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad H_{a,b,c}(t,p)\coloneqq a\cdot\min\left\{1,\frac{c-\pi|p|^{2}}{c-b}\right\}.
The symplectic frustum F⁡(a,b,c)F(a,b,c) is defined to be
F⁡(a,b,c)≔D⁡(Ha,b,c)⊂ℝ×[0,1]×B2​(c)⊂ℝ4.F(a,b,c)\coloneqq D(H_{a,b,c})\subset\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\times B^{2}(c)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}.
Any symplectic frustum F=F⁡(a,b,c)F=F(a,b,c) is a manifold with boundary and corners. We abbreviate the five top-dimensional strata of the boundary ∂F\partial F by
∂0F\displaystyle\partial_{0}F
≔\displaystyle\coloneqq
∂F∩(ℝ×{0}×ℝ2)\displaystyle\partial F\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
∂1F\displaystyle\partial_{1}F
≔\displaystyle\coloneqq
∂F∩(ℝ×{1}×ℝ2)\displaystyle\partial F\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\left\{1\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
∂+F\displaystyle\partial_{+}F
≔\displaystyle\coloneqq
∂F∩({a}×ℝ×ℝ2)\displaystyle\partial F\cap(\left\{a\right\}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
∂−F\displaystyle\partial_{-}F
≔\displaystyle\coloneqq
∂F∩({0}×ℝ×ℝ2)\displaystyle\partial F\cap(\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
∂∙F\displaystyle\partial_{\bullet}F
≔\displaystyle\coloneqq
clos⁡(∂F∩(0,a)×(0,1)×ℝ2).\displaystyle\operatorname{clos}(\partial F\cap(0,a)\times(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}).
See Figure 7.1 for an illustration. The 22-dimensional strata of ∂F\partial F are either symplectic, meaning that the ambient symplectic form on ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} restricts to an area form, or Lagrangian, meaning that the symplectic form vanishes. The symplectic strata are given by ∂jF∩∂±F\partial_{j}F\cap\partial_{\pm}F and ∂jF∩∂∙F\partial_{j}F\cap\partial_{\bullet}F for j∈{0,1}j\in\left\{0,1\right\}. The Lagrangian strata are ∂±F∩∂∙F\partial_{\pm}F\cap\partial_{\bullet}F.

A domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} is called a perturbed symplectic frustum if there exist a symplectic frustum F⊂ℝ4F\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} and a diffeomorphism φ\varphi of ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} such that the following properties are satisfied:
•
φ⁡(F)=X\varphi(F)=X
•
All 22-dimensional boundary strata of XX are either symplectic or Lagrangian.
•
φ\varphi maps symplectic 22-dimensional boundary strata of ∂F\partial F to symplectic 22-dimensional boundary strata of ∂X\partial X. It preserves the orientations of these strata induced by the symplectic form, but not necessarily the symplectic form itself.
•
φ\varphi maps Lagrangian 22-dimensional boundary strata of ∂F\partial F to Lagrangian 22-dimensional boundary strata of ∂X\partial X.
If X=φ⁡(F)X=\varphi(F) is a perturbed symplectic frustum, we abbreviate its top-dimensional boundary strata by ∂∗X≔φ⁡(∂∗F)\partial_{*}X\coloneqq\varphi(\partial_{*}F) for ∗∈{0,1,+,−,∙}*\in\left\{0,1,+,-,\bullet\right\}. We endow the space of perturbed symplectic frusta with the C∞C^{\infty} topology. A sequence of perturbed symplectic frusta (Xk)k(X_{k})_{k} converges to a perturbed symplectic frustum XX with respect to this topology if and only if there exists a sequence of diffeomorphisms (φk)k(\varphi_{k})_{k} of ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} converging to the identity in the C∞C^{\infty} topology such that Xk=φk​(X)X_{k}=\varphi_{k}(X) for all sufficiently large kk.
Theorem 7.1.
Let FF be a symplectic frustum. Then each perturbed symplectic frustum XX which is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to FF admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks.
Lemma 7.2.
Let F=F⁡(a,b,c)F=F(a,b,c) be a symplectic frustum and let XX be a perturbed symplectic frustum which is C∞C^{\infty} close to FF. Then there exist positive real numbers a′,b′,c′a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime} close to a,b,ca,b,c, respectively, and Hamiltonians G,H:[0,1]×B2​(c′)→ℝG,H:[0,1]\times B^{2}(c^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{R} such that
•
both GG and HH vanish on [0,1]×∂B2​(c′)[0,1]\times\partial B^{2}(c^{\prime}).
•
GG is C∞C^{\infty} close to 00.
•
HH agrees with the Hamiltonian Ha′,b′,c′H_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}} on the set [0,1]×B2​(b′)[0,1]\times B^{2}(b^{\prime}) and is C∞C^{\infty} close to Ha′,b′,c′H_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}} on the complement of this set.
•
XX is symplectomorphic to D⁡(G,H)D(G,H).
Proof.
We prove the lemma in two steps.
Step 1: We show that it suffices to prove Lemma 7.2 under the additional assumption
(7.1)
∂0X⊂ℝ×{0}×ℝ2and∂1X⊂ℝ×{1}×ℝ2.\partial_{0}X\subset\mathbb{R}\times\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{1}X\subset\mathbb{R}\times\left\{1\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Define the submanifold V1≔ℝ×{0}×ℝ2⊂ℝ4V_{1}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}\times\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}. Let V0⊂ℝ4V_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} be a C∞C^{\infty} small perturbation of V1V_{1} which is supported in the region (−1,a+1)×{0}×B2​(c+1)(-1,a+1)\times\left\{0\right\}\times B^{2}(c+1) and contains ∂0X\partial_{0}X. Let (Vt)t∈[0,1](V_{t})_{t\in[0,1]} be a smooth family of C∞C^{\infty} small perturbations of V1V_{1} supported in this region and connecting V0V_{0} to V1V_{1}. We define a family of diffeomorphisms αt:V0→Vt\alpha_{t}:V_{0}\rightarrow V_{t} as follows. On the set (−∞,−1)×{0}×ℝ2(-\infty,-1)\times\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}, we define αt\alpha_{t} to be the identity. Outside of this set, αt\alpha_{t} is uniquely determined by the requirements that it maps leaves of the characteristic foliation on V0V_{0} to leaves of the characteristic foliation on VtV_{t} and that it preserves the coordinate of the first ℝ\mathbb{R} factor of ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}, i.e. that it is of the form αt​(s,t,p)=(s,∗,∗)\alpha_{t}(s,t,p)=(s,*,*). Note that we have αt∗​ω=α0∗​ω\alpha_{t}^{*}\omega=\alpha_{0}^{*}\omega for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]. This identity holds trivially in the region (−∞,−1)×{0}×ℝ2(-\infty,-1)\times\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}. On the complement of this region, it follows easily from the fact that αt\alpha_{t} respects the characteristic foliations on V0V_{0} and VtV_{t}. We define the vector field XtX_{t} along VtV_{t} by Xt≔∂tαt∘αt−1X_{t}\coloneqq\partial_{t}\alpha_{t}\circ\alpha_{t}^{-1}. Clearly, the family (Vt)t∈[0,1](V_{t})_{t\in[0,1]} of C∞C^{\infty} small perturbations of V1V_{1} can be chosen such that the family of vector fields (Xt)t∈[0,1](X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]} is C∞C^{\infty} small. We will assume in the following that this is the case. It follows from the identity αt∗​ω=α0∗​ω\alpha_{t}^{*}\omega=\alpha_{0}^{*}\omega and Cartan’s magic formula that ιXt​ω\iota_{X_{t}}\omega defines a closed 11-form on VtV_{t}. It is therefore possible to find a C∞C^{\infty} small Hamiltonian E:[0,1]×ℝ4→ℝE:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} with compact support contained in [0,1]×ℝ×(−1/2,1/2)×ℝ2[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\times(-1/2,1/2)\times\mathbb{R}^{2} such that the restriction of the Hamiltonian vector field XEtX_{E_{t}} to Vt∩(−1,a+1)×ℝ×ℝ2V_{t}\cap(-1,a+1)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2} agrees with the restriction of XtX_{t} to this set. Note that this implies that φEt|∂0X=αt|∂0X\varphi_{E}^{t}|_{\partial_{0}X}=\alpha_{t}|_{\partial_{0}X}. After replacing XX by the symplectomorphic domain φE1​(X)\varphi_{E}^{1}(X), we can therefore assume that ∂0X⊂ℝ×{0}×ℝ2\partial_{0}X\subset\mathbb{R}\times\left\{0\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}. Note that this replacement does not affect the boundary stratum ∂1X\partial_{1}X. An analogous construction therefore allows us to further reduce to the case ∂1X⊂ℝ×{1}×ℝ2\partial_{1}X\subset\mathbb{R}\times\left\{1\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Step 2: Let us now assume that XX satisfies (7.1). If XX is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to FF, we may choose compactly supported and C∞C^{\infty} small Hamiltonians E,E±:[0,1]×ℝ2→ℝE,E_{\pm}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} satisfying the following properties:
•
∂−X\partial_{-}X is contained in the graph of E−E_{-}.
•
∂+X\partial_{+}X is contained in the graph of a+E+a+E_{+}.
•
EE agrees with E−E_{-} on the set [0,1]×(ℝ2∖B2​(b+3​(c−b)/5))[0,1]\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B^{2}(b+3(c-b)/5)).
•
EE agrees with E+E_{+} on the set [0,1]×B2​(b+2​(c−b)/5)[0,1]\times B^{2}(b+2(c-b)/5).
By Lemma 4.3, the domain D⁡(E−,a+E+)D(E_{-},a+E_{+}) is symplectomorphic to D⁡(E¯​#​E−,E¯​#​(a+E+))D(\overline{E}\#E_{-},\overline{E}\#(a+E_{+})). Since EE is C∞C^{\infty} small, the proof of Lemma 4.3 in fact yields a symplectomorphism ψ\psi of ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} which is C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity and maps D⁡(E−,a+E+)D(E_{-},a+E_{+}) to D⁡(E¯​#​E−,E¯​#​(a+E+))D(\overline{E}\#E_{-},\overline{E}\#(a+E_{+})). If XX is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to FF, then E¯​#​E−\overline{E}\#E_{-} vanishes on the set [0,1]×(ℝ2∖B2​(b+4​(c−b)/5))[0,1]\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B^{2}(b+4(c-b)/5)) and E¯​#​(a+E+)\overline{E}\#(a+E_{+}) is equal to aa on the set [0,1]×B2​(b+(c−b)/5)[0,1]\times B^{2}(b+(c-b)/5). This implies that the Lagrangian annulus ψ⁡(∂−X∩∂∙X)\psi(\partial_{-}X\cap\partial_{\bullet}X) is of the form {0}×[0,1]×C\left\{0\right\}\times[0,1]\times C where C⊂ℝ2C\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} is a circle C∞C^{\infty} close to ∂B2​(c)\partial B^{2}(c). Moreover, it implies that the solid cylinder ψ⁡(∂+X)\psi(\partial_{+}X) is of the form {a}×[0,1]×B\left\{a\right\}\times[0,1]\times B where B⊂ℝ2B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} is a disk C∞C^{\infty} close to B2​(b)B^{2}(b). We may pick an area preserving diffeomorphism α\alpha of ℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2} which is C∞C^{\infty} close to the identity and satisfies α⁡(C)=∂B2​(c′)\alpha(C)=\partial B^{2}(c^{\prime}) and α⁡(B)=∂B2​(b′)\alpha(B)=\partial B^{2}(b^{\prime}) with b′,c′b^{\prime},c^{\prime} close to b,cb,c, respectively. The resulting domain (idℝ2×α)∘ψ⁡(X)(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\times\alpha)\circ\psi(X) is then of the form D⁡(G,H)D(G,H) where G,H:[0,1]×B2​(c′)→ℝG,H:[0,1]\times B^{2}(c^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{R} satisfy all the properties listed in Lemma 7.2.
∎
Proof of Theorem 7.1.
Fix a symplectic frustum F=F⁡(a,b,c)F=F(a,b,c) and let XX be a perturbed symplectic frustum C∞C^{\infty} close to FF. Let the positive numbers a′,b′,c′a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime} and the Hamiltonians G,HG,H satisfy all assertions in Lemma 7.2. Since XX is symplectomorphic to D⁡(G,H)D(G,H), it suffices to construct a ∂\partial-tame packing of the latter domain.
Pick a number d>0d>0 close to a′/2a^{\prime}/2 such that d/c′d/c^{\prime} is not an integer. Define the Hamitonian
E:[0,1]×B2​(c′)→ℝE⁡(t,p)≔d⁡(1−π​|p|2/c′)E:[0,1]\times B^{2}(c^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad E(t,p)\coloneqq d(1-\pi|p|^{2}/c^{\prime})
We divide D⁡(G,H)D(G,H) into D⁡(G,E)D(G,E) and D⁡(E,H)D(E,H). By Lemma 6.2, it suffices to show that both pieces admit ∂\partial-tame packings.
The piece D⁡(G,E)D(G,E) is symplectomorphic to D⁡(G¯​#​E)D(\overline{G}\#E). Note that G¯​#​E\overline{G}\#E is a C∞C^{\infty} small perturbation of EE. The time 11-map φE1\varphi_{E}^{1} is a rotation by 2​π​d/c′2\pi d/c^{\prime}. Since d/c′d/c^{\prime} is not an integer, the center of the disk B2​(c′)B^{2}(c^{\prime}) is a non-degenerate fixed point. Therefore, the time-11-map φG¯​#​E1\varphi_{\overline{G}\#E}^{1} has a non-degenerate fixed point pp near the center. After conjugation by a area preserving diffeomorphism of B2​(c′)B^{2}(c^{\prime}) which is C∞C^{\infty}-close to the identity, we can assume that p=0p=0. Moreover, it is not hard to construct a Hamiltonian E~\tilde{E} which vanishes on [0,1]×∂B2​(c′)[0,1]\times\partial B^{2}(c^{\prime}), is C∞C^{\infty} close to EE, has the property that the center is the unique maximizer of E~t\tilde{E}_{t} for all t∈[0,1]t\in[0,1], and such that D⁡(E~)D(\tilde{E}) is symplectomorphic to D⁡(G¯​#​E)D(\overline{G}\#E). Consider the area preserving map
ψ:𝔸⁡(c′)→B2​(c′)(x,y)↦xπ​e2​π​i​θ.\psi:\mathbb{A}(c^{\prime})\rightarrow B^{2}(c^{\prime})\qquad(x,y)\mapsto\sqrt{\frac{x}{\pi}}e^{2\pi i\theta}.
Note that the pullback ψ∗​E~\psi^{*}\tilde{E} is a Hamiltonian as in Theorem 6.7 and therefore admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. Since every embedding of a symplectic ribbon complex into D⁡(ψ∗​E~)D(\psi^{*}\tilde{E}) induces an embedding into D⁡(E~)D(\tilde{E}) via idℝ2×ψ\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\times\psi, we deduce that D⁡(E~)D(\tilde{E}) admits a ∂\partial-tame packing as well.
Next, consider the piece D⁡(E,H)D(E,H), which is symplectomorphic to D⁡(E¯​#​H)D(\overline{E}\#H). Again, let us consider the pullback ψ∗​(E¯​#​H)\psi^{*}(\overline{E}\#H) to a Hamiltonian on 𝔸⁡(a′)\mathbb{A}(a^{\prime}). It suffices to construct a ∂\partial-tame packing of D​(ψ∗​(E¯​#​H))D(\psi^{*}(\overline{E}\#H)). Let us divide 𝔸⁡(c′)\mathbb{A}(c^{\prime}) into two smaller annuli
𝔸1≔[0,b′]×𝕋and𝔸2≔[b′,c′]×𝕋.\mathbb{A}_{1}\coloneqq[0,b^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{T}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{A}_{2}\coloneqq[b^{\prime},c^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{T}.
We stratify D​(ψ∗​(E¯​#​H))D(\psi^{*}(\overline{E}\#H)) into two pieces D⁡(ψ∗​(E¯​#​H)|[0,1]×𝔸1)D(\psi^{*}(\overline{E}\#H)|_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{1}}) and D⁡(ψ∗​(E¯​#​H)[0,1]×𝔸2)D(\psi^{*}(\overline{E}\#H)_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{A}_{2}}). On the annulus 𝔸1\mathbb{A}_{1}, the Hamiltonian ψ∗​(E¯​#​H)\psi^{*}(\overline{E}\#H) is strictly positive and affine. Therefore, the first stratum admits a ∂\partial-tame packing as a special case of Corollary 6.6. On the annulus 𝔸2\mathbb{A}_{2}, the Hamiltonian ψ∗​(E¯​#​H)\psi^{*}(\overline{E}\#H) is of the form considered in Theorem 6.7. Thus, the second stratum also admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. We conclude that the same is true for D⁡(E,H)D(E,H).
∎
Let X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} be a perturbed symplectic frustum. In the following, we introduce a cut operation which produces a new domain X~⊂ℝ4\tilde{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}. Let ε>0\varepsilon>0 and a>0a>0 be positive real numbers. Suppose that φ\varphi is a symplectomorphism of ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4} such that
(7.2)
φ(X)∩ℝ2×(−ε,+∞)×(−ε,ε)={0≤s≤min{a,a−ax},0≤t≤1,−ε<x≤1,−ε<y<ε}.\varphi(X)\kern 5.0pt\cap\kern 5.0pt\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(-\varepsilon,+\infty)\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)=\left\{0\leq s\leq\min\left\{a,a-ax\right\},0\leq t\leq 1,-\varepsilon<x\leq 1,-\varepsilon<y<\varepsilon\right\}.
Let ψ:ℝ2∖([0,+∞)×{0})→ℝ2\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus([0,+\infty)\times\left\{0\right\})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2} be an area preserving embedding. Let us assume that the restriction of ψ\psi to each of the open quadrants {x>0,±y>0}\left\{x>0,\pm y>0\right\} extends to a smooth area preserving embedding of the closed quadrant ψ±:{x≥0,±y≥0}→ℝ2\psi_{\pm}:\left\{x\geq 0,\pm y\geq 0\right\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}. Moreover, assume that the boundary of the image of ψ\psi is piecewise smooth; see Figure 7.2.
ψ

ψ
Figure 7.2. An illustration of different possibilities for the map ψ\psi.
The new domain X~\tilde{X} obtained from XX by cutting is defined by
X~≔clos((idℝ2×ψ)(φ(X)∖ℝ2×[0,+∞)×{0})),\tilde{X}\coloneqq\operatorname{clos}((\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\times\psi)(\varphi(X)\setminus\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,+\infty)\times\left\{0\right\})),
see Figure 7.3.
Figure 7.3. For the different choices of ψ\psi illustrated in Figure 7.2, this figure shows the intersection of X~\tilde{X} with ℝ×{t}×ℝ2\mathbb{R}\times\left\{t\right\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2} near the cut region for 0≤t≤10\leq t\leq 1.
We call a domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} a perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts if, up to ambient symplectomorphism, it can be obtained from a perturbed symplectic frustum by finitely many disjoint cut operations.
Theorem 7.3.
Let FF be a symplectic frustum. Then each perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts XX obtained by cutting a perturbed symplectic frustum sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to FF admits a ∂\partial-tame packing by balls and polydisks.
Proof.
Let XX be a perturbed symplectic frustum C∞C^{\infty} close to FF and let X~\tilde{X} be a domain obtained from XX by finitely many cuts. By Theorem 7.1, the domain XX admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. In order to deduce that the same is true for X~\tilde{X}, we follow the same strategy that we have already used repeatedly in Section 6. Let Z⊂int⁡(X)Z\subset\operatorname{int}(X) be a domain conformally symplectomorphic to XX obtained by slightly pushing the boundary of XX inwards. Our goal is to produce an explicit controlled symplectic embedding of ZZ into int⁡(X~)\operatorname{int}(\tilde{X}) which has the property that symplectic disks in the smooth locus of the boundary of X~\tilde{X} can be connected to the boundary of the image of the symplectic embedding of ZZ via symplectic ribbons. Once we have this, the fact that XX and hence ZZ admits a ∂\partial-tame packing implies that X~\tilde{X} also admits a ∂\partial-tame packing.
For simplicity, let us consider the case that X~\tilde{X} is obtained from XX by a single cut. The general case can be treated by iterating the construction explained below. Let ε,a,φ,ψ\varepsilon,a,\varphi,\psi be the parameters involved in the cut which transforms XX into X~\tilde{X}. In order to simplify notation, let us replace XX by the symplectomorphic domain φ⁡(X)\varphi(X).
We carry out a variant of Schlenk’s embedding construction [Sch03, §2], [Sch05a, §4.1] which we have used several times in Sections 5 and 6. Consider the strip (−ε,+∞)×(−ε,ε)(-\varepsilon,+\infty)\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon). Then there exists a symplectomorphism
α:(−ε,+∞)×(−ε,ε)→(−ε,+∞)×(−ε,ε)∖[0,+∞)×{0}\alpha:(-\varepsilon,+\infty)\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\rightarrow(-\varepsilon,+\infty)\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\setminus[0,+\infty)\times\left\{0\right\}
which maps maps vertical line segments of the form {x}×(−ε,ε)\left\{x\right\}\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon) to members of a family of line segments ℒ\mathcal{L} as depicted in Figure 7.4.
Figure 7.4. A family ℒ\mathcal{L} of line segments in (−ε,+∞)×(−ε,ε)∖[0,+∞)×{0}(-\varepsilon,+\infty)\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\setminus[0,+\infty)\times\left\{0\right\}.
Near the boundary of the strip, the map α\alpha is close to the identity. After a small perturbation near the boundary, we can assume that α\alpha is exactly equal to the identity in this region. Extend α\alpha to a symplectomorphism between ℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2} and ℝ2∖[0,+∞)×{0}\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus[0,+\infty)\times\left\{0\right\} by setting it equal to the identity outside the strip. Consider the symplectic embedding
ρ:ℝ4→ℝ4ρ≔idℝ2×(ψ∘α).\rho:\mathbb{R}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{4}\qquad\rho\coloneqq\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\times(\psi\circ\alpha).
Note that the family of line segments ℒ\mathcal{L} can be arranged such that the image ρ⁡(X)\rho(X) approximates X~\tilde{X} arbitrarily well. The desired embedding of ZZ into int⁡(X~)\operatorname{int}(\tilde{X}) is simply given by the restriction ρ|Z\rho|_{Z}.
∎
8. Proof of ellipsoid embedding stability
The goal of this section is to prove Theorem B, i.e. that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for every compact connected symplectic 44-manifold with smooth boundary.
We will make use of the following technical stratification lemma, whose proof we postpone to Section 9. Given positive numbers a,b>0a,b>0, we define the symplectic cuboid Q⁡(a,b)≔□⁡(a,b)×LQQ(a,b)\coloneqq\square(a,b)\times_{L}Q; see Example 6.4.
Lemma 8.1.
There exist a compact set ℱ\mathcal{F} of symplectic frusta and a compact set 𝒬\mathcal{Q} of symplectic cuboids such that the following is true for every C∞C^{\infty} open neighbourhood 𝒰\mathcal{U} of ℱ\mathcal{F} inside the space of all perturbed symplectic frusta: Let MM be a symplectic 44-manifold which is diffeomorphic to [0,1]×Y[0,1]\times Y for some closed 33-manifold YY. Then there exists r>0r>0 such that, for all 0≤σ0<σ1≤10\leq\sigma_{0}<\sigma_{1}\leq 1 satisfying σ1−σ0<r\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}<r, there exists a stratification of [σ0,σ1]×Y[\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}]\times Y such that the closure of any top-dimensional stratum is conformally symplectomorphic to a symplectic cuboid contained in 𝒬\mathcal{Q} or to a domain obtained from an element of 𝒰\mathcal{U} by cutting.
Proof of Theorem B.
Our first step is to show that every symplectic 44-manifold diffeomorphic to [0,1]×Y[0,1]\times Y for some closed connected 33-manifold YY admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. Let M=[0,1]×YM=[0,1]\times Y be such a symplectic 44-manifold. Let ℱ\mathcal{F} be a compact set of symplectic frusta and 𝒬\mathcal{Q} be a compact set of symplectic cuboids as in Lemma 8.1. It follows from Theorem 7.3 that there exists an open neighbourhood 𝒰\mathcal{U} of ℱ\mathcal{F} inside the space of perturbed symplectic frusta such that every domain obtained from an element of 𝒰\mathcal{U} by cutting admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. This clearly implies that every domain conformally symplectomorphic to a domain obtained from an element of 𝒰\mathcal{U} by cutting also admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. Moreover, recall from Example 6.4 that every symplectic cuboid admits a ∂\partial-tame packing.
Pick r>0r>0 satisfying the assertion of Lemma 8.1. Let k>0k>0 be a positive integer such that k−1<rk^{-1}<r. Divide MM into kk slices Mi≔[(i−1)/k,i/k]×YM_{i}\coloneqq[(i-1)/k,i/k]\times Y for 1≤i≤k1\leq i\leq k. Let us stratify each slice MiM_{i} into symplectic cuboids and perturbed symplectic frusta with cuts as in Lemma 8.1. It follows from Lemma 6.2 that each MiM_{i} admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. Applying Lemma 6.2 again, we see that the same is true for MM.
Let us now assume that MM is a general compact connected symplectic 44-manifold with smooth boundary. Our goal is to show that MM admits a tame packing. Once we know this, it follows from Corollary 6.1 that ellipsoid embedding stability holds for MM. Choose a Morse function f:M→ℝf:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R} which is constant on each boundary component of MM. We may arrange ff such that each critical level contains exactly one critical point. We divide MM into finitely many strata along regular level sets of ff such that each of the resulting strata contains at most one critical point. By Lemma 6.2, it suffices to construct a ∂\partial-tame packing for each of the strata. Since strata without critical points are of product form and admit a ∂\partial-tame packing as proved above, we may reduce ourselves to the case that ff has exactly one critical point pp. After replacing ff by −f-f if necessary, we can further assume that pp has Morse index 00, 11 or 22. In addition, we may assume that f⁡(p)=0f(p)=0.
Consider the case of Morse index 00. We modify ff such that it is given by f⁡(z)=|z|2f(z)=|z|^{2} in local Darboux coordinates (z1,z2)=(x1,y1,x2,y2)(z_{1},z_{2})=(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2}) near p=0p=0. For δ>0\delta>0 sufficiently small, we divide MM into two strata along the level set f−1​(δ)f^{-1}(\delta). One of the strata is a ball and the other one is of product form. Both strata admit a ∂\partial-tame packing. For the balls this is obvious and for the stratum of product form this was proved above. Hence Lemma 6.2 implies that MM admits a ∂\partial-tame packing as well.
Next, consider the case of Morse index 22. We modify ff such that it is given by f⁡(z)=|z1|2−|z2|2f(z)=|z_{1}|^{2}-|z_{2}|^{2} in local Darboux coordinates near p=0p=0. By Lemma 6.2, it suffices to show that both f−1((−∞,0])f^{-1}((-\infty,0]) and f−1​([0,∞))f^{-1}([0,\infty)) admit ∂\partial-tame packings. Up to replacing ff by −f-f, it suffices to consider the sublevel set X≔f−1((−∞,0])X\coloneqq f^{-1}((-\infty,0]). Consider the standard moment map
μ:ℂ2→(ℝ≥0)2(z1,z2)↦(π​|z1|2,π​|z2|2)\mu:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{2}\qquad(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(\pi|z_{1}|^{2},\pi|z_{2}|^{2})
and the moment regions Ω,Ω′⊂ℝ≥02\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2} defined by
Ω≔{μ2≥μ1}andΩ′≔{μ2≥μ1+1}.\Omega\coloneqq\left\{\mu_{2}\geq\mu_{1}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega^{\prime}\coloneqq\left\{\mu_{2}\geq\mu_{1}+1\right\}.
Note that the toric domain μ−1​(Ω)\mu^{-1}(\Omega) is smooth away from the singular point 00 and that a neighbourhood of pp in XX is symplectomorphic to a neighbourhood of 00 in μ−1​(Ω)\mu^{-1}(\Omega). The toric domain μ−1​(Ω′)\mu^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime}) is smooth everywhere. The moment region Ω\Omega is obtained from Ω′\Omega^{\prime} by translation along the vector (0,−1)(0,-1). This translation lifts to a map
T:μ−1​(Ω′)→μ−1​(Ω)(μ1π​e2​π​i​θ1,μ2π​e2​π​i​θ2)↦(μ1π​e2​π​i​θ1,μ2−1π​e2​π​i​θ2).T:\mu^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime})\rightarrow\mu^{-1}(\Omega)\qquad(\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{1}}{\pi}}e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}},\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{2}}{\pi}}e^{2\pi i\theta_{2}})\mapsto(\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{1}}{\pi}}e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}},\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{2}-1}{\pi}}e^{2\pi i\theta_{2}}).
which restricts to a symplectomorphism T:μ−1​(Ω′∖{(0,1)})→μ−1​(Ω∖{(0,0)})T:\mu^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime}\setminus\left\{(0,1)\right\})\rightarrow\mu^{-1}(\Omega\setminus\left\{(0,0)\right\}). We construct a smooth symplectic manifold X′X^{\prime} out of XX by replacing a neighbourhood of pp, which we symplectically identify with a neighbourhood UU of 00 in μ−1​(Ω)\mu^{-1}(\Omega), with T−1​(U)T^{-1}(U). Note that X′X^{\prime} is of product form and therefore admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. This implies that the same is true for XX. Indeed, any collection of symplectic disks 𝒟\mathcal{D} in the smooth locus of the boundary of XX lifts to a collection of smooth disks 𝒟′\mathcal{D}^{\prime} in the boundary of X′X^{\prime}. Moreover, the map TT sends any symplectic ribbon complex in X′X^{\prime} with free ends at the disks 𝒟′\mathcal{D}^{\prime} to a symplectic ribbon complex in XX with free ends at 𝒟\mathcal{D}.
Finally, let us consider the case that pp has index 11. In this case, we modify ff such that there exist δ>0\delta>0, a>0a>0, and 0<b<a/π0<b<\sqrt{a/\pi} such that the ball B4​(a)B^{4}(a) is contained in a Darboux neighbourhood of pp, the function ff takes the value δ\delta on the set ∂B4(a)∩{|x1|≤b}\partial B^{4}(a)\cap\left\{|x_{1}|\leq b\right\}, and B4(a)∩{|x1|≤b}B^{4}(a)\cap\left\{|x_{1}|\leq b\right\} is contained in the sublevel set f−1((−∞,δ])f^{-1}((-\infty,\delta]). Let us divide MM into two strata f−1((−∞,δ])f^{-1}((-\infty,\delta]) and f−1​([δ,∞))f^{-1}([\delta,\infty)). Since the latter stratum is of product form, we may replace MM by f−1​((∞,δ])f^{-1}((\infty,\delta]). We cut MM along the 33-dimensional ball B4(a)∩{x1=0}B^{4}(a)\cap\left\{x_{1}=0\right\}. The result is a symplectic manifold XX with boundary and corners with either one or two connected components. Near the non-smooth regions of the boundary, XX looks like a half ball B+4(a)≔B4(a)∩{x1≥0}B^{4}_{+}(a)\coloneqq B^{4}(a)\cap\left\{x_{1}\geq 0\right\}. Note that after smoothing the corners, XX is diffeomorphic to a manifold of product form.
Our goal is to show that each component of XX admits a ∂\partial-tame packing. For simplicity, let us assume that XX is connected in the following. As in the case of an index 22 critical point, our strategy is to construct a symplectic 44-manifold X′X^{\prime} with smooth boundary such that the existence of a ∂\partial-tame packing for X′X^{\prime} implies the existence of such a packing for XX. The symplectic manifold X′X^{\prime} is obtained by carefully smoothing the corners of XX.
Let us first explain the corner smoothing in the case of the half ball X=B+4​(a)X=B^{4}_{+}(a). In this case, X′X^{\prime} is given by the ellipsoid E⁡(a/2,a)E(a/2,a), which has the same volume. We need to explain how to obtain a ∂\partial-tame packing of B+4​(a)B^{4}_{+}(a) from a ∂\partial-tame packing of E⁡(a/2,a)E(a/2,a). For every r∈(0,1)r\in(0,1), we construct a controlled symplectic embedding of the ellipsoid E⁡(r​a/2,r​a)E(ra/2,ra) into the half ball B+4​(a)B^{4}_{+}(a). Let us regard the ellipsoid E⁡(a/2,a)E(a/2,a) as a family of disks of varying sizes over B2​(a/2)B^{2}(a/2). The area of the disk over z∈B2​(a/2)z\in B^{2}(a/2) is given by f1​(z)≔2​(a/2−π​|z|2)f_{1}(z)\coloneqq 2(a/2-\pi|z|^{2}). Similarly, we regard the half ball B+4​(a)B^{4}_{+}(a) as a family of disks over the half disk B+2(a)≔B2(a)∩{x1≥0}B^{2}_{+}(a)\coloneqq B^{2}(a)\cap\left\{x_{1}\geq 0\right\}. The area of the disk over z∈B+2​(a)z\in B^{2}_{+}(a) is f2​(z)≔a−π​|z|2f_{2}(z)\coloneqq a-\pi|z|^{2}. Observe that, for every r≥0r\geq 0, we have
(8.1)
area⁡({z∈B2​(a/2)∣f1​(z)≥r})=area⁡({z∈B+2​(a)∣f2​(z)≥r}).\operatorname{area}\left(\left\{z\in B^{2}(a/2)\mid f_{1}(z)\geq r\right\}\right)=\operatorname{area}\left(\left\{z\in B^{2}_{+}(a)\mid f_{2}(z)\geq r\right\}\right).
We apply the embedding construction of Schlenk [Sch03, §2], [Sch05a, §4.1] that we have already used several times. To this end, consider the family of admissible loops ℒ\mathcal{L} in the half disk int⁡(B+2​(a))\operatorname{int}(B^{2}_{+}(a)) depicted in Figure 8.1.
Figure 8.1. A special admissible family of loops ℒ\mathcal{L} in the half disk B+2​(a)B^{2}_{+}(a).
Then by [Sch03, Lemma 2.5], there exists a symplectomorphism φ:int⁡(B2​(a/2))→int⁡(B+2​(a))\varphi:\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a/2))\rightarrow\operatorname{int}(B^{2}_{+}(a)) which maps circles in int⁡(B2​(a/2))\operatorname{int}(B^{2}(a/2)) centered at the origin to members of the family of loops ℒ\mathcal{L}. We can arrange the family of loops ℒ\mathcal{L} such that the image of E⁡(r​a/2,r​a)E(ra/2,ra) unter φ×idℂ\varphi\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{C}} is contained in int⁡(B+4​(a))\operatorname{int}(B^{4}_{+}(a)). This makes important use of identity (8.1). Moreover, if the family of loops ℒ\mathcal{L} is chosen carefully, we can make sure that the top-dimensional boundary strata of B+4​(a)B^{4}_{+}(a) are approximated in Cloc∞C^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}} by parts of the boundary of the image of E⁡(r​a/2,r​a)E(ra/2,ra) as rr tends to 11. Symplectic disks in the boundary of B+4​(a)B^{4}_{+}(a) can therefore be connected to symplectic disks in the boundary of the image of E⁡(r​a/2,r​a)E(ra/2,ra) via symplectic ribbons, showing that the existence of a ∂\partial-tame packing of E⁡(a/2,a)E(a/2,a) implies the existence of a ∂\partial-tame packing of B+4​(a)B^{4}_{+}(a).
The adaptation of the corner smoothing from the special case B+4​(a)B^{4}_{+}(a) to the case of general XX is straightforward. The smoothing X′X^{\prime} is obtained by replacing an open neighbourhood B+4(a)∩{x1<b}B^{4}_{+}(a)\cap\left\{x_{1}<b\right\} of the non-smooth locus of the boundary of XX by an appropriate open subset of the ellipsoid E⁡(a/2,a)E(a/2,a). The resulting symplectic manifold X′X^{\prime} has smooth boundary and is of product form. It therefore admits a ∂\partial-tame packing and we can deduce that the same is true for XX.
∎
9. Proof of the stratification lemma
The goal of this section is to prove the technical stratification result Lemma 8.1.
9.1. Stratifications compatible with a flow
Let YY be a closed 33-manifold equipped with a nowhere vanishing vector field RR. The main example of interest is that YY is the boundary of a symplectic manifold and that RR is tangent to the characteristic foliation. Let φt\varphi^{t} denote the flow generated by RR. Let Σ⊂Y\Sigma\subset Y be a compact embedded surface, possibly with boundary. We say that Σ\Sigma is a transverse surface if it is everywhere transverse to the vector field RR. We emphasize that we also assume Σ\Sigma to be transverse to RR at the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. In particular, the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma does not consist of periodic orbits, which is a familiar assumption in the context of global surfaces of section. We say that Σ\Sigma is exhaustive if there exists a compact subset K⊂int⁡ΣK\subset\operatorname{int}\Sigma such that every flow line of φt\varphi^{t} meets KK infinitely often forward and backward in time. It is not hard to see that an exhaustive transverse surface always exists. For example, one can construct such a surface by taking the disjoint union of sufficiently many small disks transverse to the flow.
Given an exhaustive transverse surface Σ\Sigma, we define
T=TΣ:Y→ℝ>0T⁡(p)≔inf{t>0∣φt​(p)∈Σ}.T=T_{\Sigma}:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}\qquad T(p)\coloneqq\inf\{t>0\mid\varphi^{t}(p)\in\Sigma\}.
Note that TT indeed takes values in ℝ>0\mathbb{R}_{>0}. Since the flow line φt​(p)\varphi^{t}(p) intersects Σ\Sigma forward in time and is transverse to Σ\Sigma, the infimum T⁡(p)T(p) is finite and non-zero. Let us define
Φ:Y→ΣΦ⁡(p)≔φT⁡(p)​(p).\Phi:Y\rightarrow\Sigma\qquad\Phi(p)\coloneqq\varphi^{T(p)}(p).
We abbreviate τ≔T|Σ\tau\coloneqq T|_{\Sigma} and φ≔Φ|Σ\varphi\coloneqq\Phi|_{\Sigma}. We observe that φ\varphi is a bijection of Σ\Sigma. In general, it is not continuous.
We say that an exhaustive transverse surface Σ\Sigma is in good position if the following is true for every point p∈∂Σp\in\partial\Sigma. For k∈ℤk\in\mathbb{Z}, set pk≔φk​(p)p_{k}\coloneqq\varphi^{k}(p).
•
Suppose that p1∈∂Σp_{1}\in\partial\Sigma. Then the immersion ℝ×∂Σ→Y\mathbb{R}\times\partial\Sigma\rightarrow Y induced by the flow φt\varphi^{t} intersects ∂Σ\partial\Sigma transversely at (τ⁡(p),p)∈ℝ×∂Σ(\tau(p),p)\in\mathbb{R}\times\partial\Sigma. Moreover, the points p−2p_{-2}, p−1p_{-1}, p2p_{2} and p3p_{3} are contained in the interior of Σ\Sigma.
•
Suppose that p1p_{1} is not contained in ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. If p2p_{2} is contained in ∂Σ\partial\Sigma, then the immersion ℝ×∂Σ→Y\mathbb{R}\times\partial\Sigma\rightarrow Y induced by the flow φt\varphi^{t} intersects ∂Σ\partial\Sigma transversely at (τ⁡(p)+τ⁡(p1),p)∈ℝ×∂Σ(\tau(p)+\tau(p_{1}),p)\in\mathbb{R}\times\partial\Sigma.
Note that an exhaustive transverse surface can be brought into good position by a generic perburbation.
Given an exhaustive transverse surface Σ⊂Y\Sigma\subset Y in good position, we construct a stratification 𝒴\mathcal{Y} of YY. Constructing 𝒴\mathcal{Y} amounts to constructing a filtration
Y=Y3⊃Y2⊃Y1⊃Y0Y=Y_{3}\supset Y_{2}\supset Y_{1}\supset Y_{0}
by closed subsets Yi⊂YY_{i}\subset Y such that each complement Yi∖Yi−1Y_{i}\setminus Y_{i-1} is a submanifold of dimension ii. We define Y2Y_{2} to be the union of a subset Y2⟂Y_{2}^{\perp} transverse to the vector field RR and a subset Y2⊤Y_{2}^{\top} tangent to RR. The transverse subset Y2⟂Y_{2}^{\perp} is simply defined to be Y2⟂≔ΣY_{2}^{\perp}\coloneqq\Sigma. Let Σ+⊂Y\Sigma^{+}\subset Y be a surface obtained by slightly enlarging Σ\Sigma, i.e. by slightly pushing ∂Σ\partial\Sigma in the direction of the outward normal vector field. The tangent subset Y2⊤Y_{2}^{\top} is constructed by flowing every point p∈∂Σ∪∂Σ+p\in\partial\Sigma\cup\partial\Sigma^{+} forward and backward until Σ\Sigma is hit for the first time. In formulas this means
Y2⟂≔ΣY2⊤≔Φ−1​(∂Σ)∪Φ−1​(φ⁡(∂Σ))∪Φ−1​(Φ⁡(∂Σ+))¯Y2≔Y2⟂∪Y2⊤.Y_{2}^{\perp}\coloneqq\Sigma\qquad Y_{2}^{\top}\coloneqq\overline{\Phi^{-1}(\partial\Sigma)\cup\Phi^{-1}(\varphi(\partial\Sigma))\cup\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(\partial\Sigma^{+}))}\qquad Y_{2}\coloneqq Y_{2}^{\perp}\cup Y_{2}^{\top}.
We define Y1⊂Y2Y_{1}\subset Y_{2} to be the set of non-smooth points of Y2Y_{2} and Y0⊂Y1Y_{0}\subset Y_{1} to be the set of non-smooth points of Y1Y_{1}. It follows from the assumption that Σ\Sigma is in good position that this actually defines a stratification of 𝒴\mathcal{Y}.
Let us now describe the structure of 𝒴\mathcal{Y} in more detail. Let S⊂ΣS\subset\Sigma be the set of all points p∈Σp\in\Sigma such that the first intersection of the forward flow line starting at pp with Σ\Sigma is contained in the boundary ∂Σ\partial\Sigma. In other words, we set S≔φ−1​(∂Σ)S\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(\partial\Sigma). The map φ\varphi is continuous on Σ∖S\Sigma\setminus S. Since Σ\Sigma is in good position, the set SS is a smooth 11-dimensional manifold except for finitely many T-shaped singularities, i.e. singularities modeled on ℝ>0∪i​ℝ⊂ℂ\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cup i\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}. Each smooth point p∈Sp\in S has a preferred normal orientation constructed as follows. The function τ\tau has a jump discontinuity along SS. We equip p∈Sp\in S with the normal orientation along which τ\tau jumps up. Consider a T-shaped singularity of SS identified with ℝ>0∪i​ℝ\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cup i\mathbb{R}. It is not hard to check that the normal orientation at points in i​ℝi\mathbb{R} must point in the direction of positive real part.
Let S+S^{+} denote the set of all points p∈Σp\in\Sigma such that the forward flow line starting at pp meets ∂Σ+\partial\Sigma^{+} before it meets Σ\Sigma for the first time, i.e. S+≔φ−1​(Φ⁡(∂Σ+))S^{+}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(\Phi(\partial\Sigma^{+})). If ∂Σ+\partial\Sigma^{+} is sufficiently close to ∂Σ\partial\Sigma, then S+S^{+} is a small push-off of SS in the direction of the normal orientation. The model at a T-shaped singularity of SS is given by
S=ℝ>0∪i​ℝ⊂ℂandS+=(i​ε+ℝ>0)∪(ε+i​ℝ)S=\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cup i\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}\qquad\text{and}\qquad S^{+}=(i\varepsilon+\mathbb{R}_{>0})\cup(\varepsilon+i\mathbb{R})
for ε>0\varepsilon>0. See Figure 9.1 for an illustration of the sets SS and S+S^{+}.
Σ

∂
