Figure 9.1. Intersection of the surfaces Σ\Sigma (green) and Σ+\Sigma^{+} (blue) with a flow box. The sets SS and S+S^{+} are highlighted by dashed lines.
The sets SS and S+S^{+} can be used to define a stratification 𝒮\mathcal{S} of Σ\Sigma. We define the 11-skeleton of this stratification to be the union ∂Σ∪S∪S+\partial\Sigma\cup S\cup S^{+} and the 00-skeleton to be the set of non-smooth points of ∂Σ∪S∪S+\partial\Sigma\cup S\cup S^{+}. The set of top-dimensional strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} consists of the following (see Figure 9.2):
(S1)
a small square at each T-shaped singularity of SS;
(S2)
a thin rectangle along each smooth arc of SS and a thin annulus along each smooth circle component of SS;
(S3)
for each component CC of Σ∖S\Sigma\setminus S, a set C−C^{-} which is obtained from CC by slightly pushing inwards some of its boundary arcs.
Figure 9.2. Stratification 𝒮\mathcal{S} of a disk component of Σ\Sigma. Strata of type (S1) are drawn in yellow, strata of type (S2) in blue, and strata of type (S3) in green.
The top-dimensional strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} and 𝒴\mathcal{Y} are in bijection. Given a top-dimensional stratum CC of 𝒮\mathcal{S}, the corresponding top-dimensional stratum of 𝒴\mathcal{Y} is given by the flow box
C𝒴≔{φt​(p)∣p∈C​and​t∈(0,τ⁡(p))}.C_{\mathcal{Y}}\coloneqq\left\{\varphi^{t}(p)\mid p\in C\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0ptt\in(0,\tau(p))\right\}.
We conclude this subsection by constructing exhaustive transverse surfaces Σ\Sigma which are in good position in a precise quantitative sense. Let gg be an auxiliary Riemannian metric on YY such that RR has unit norm. Given a small positive number r>0r>0 and a point p∈Yp\in Y, we define the transverse geodesic disk D⁡(p,r)⊂YD(p,r)\subset Y by
D(p,r)≔{expp(v)∣v∈TpY,v⟂Rp,|v|≤r}.D(p,r)\coloneqq\left\{\exp_{p}(v)\mid v\in T_{p}Y,\kern 5.0ptv\perp R_{p},\kern 5.0pt|v|\leq r\right\}.
Suppose that ρ>0\rho>0 is a small positive number. If ρ\rho is sufficiently small, we can build an exhaustive transverse surface Σ\Sigma by taking the disjoint union of finitely many transverse geodesic disks of radius ρ\rho. By adding more disks if necessary, we can make sure that max⁡τ≤ρ\max\tau\leq\rho. In the following we assume that this is the case.
Now consider an arbitrary point p∈Yp\in Y and choose an isometric identification η:ℝ2≅⟨Rp⟩⟂\eta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\cong\langle R_{p}\rangle^{\perp}. Define the embedding
ψ:(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)→Yψ⁡(t,z)≔φρ​t​(expp⁡(ρ​z))\psi:(-10,10)\times B(0,10)\rightarrow Y\qquad\psi(t,z)\coloneqq\varphi^{\rho t}(\exp_{p}(\rho z))
where B⁡(0,10)⊂ℝ2B(0,10)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} is the ball of radius 1010 centered at 00. Let g0g_{0} be the Riemannian metric on (−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)(-10,10)\times B(0,10) obtained by restricting the standard metric on ℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}. Moreover, define the vector field R0≔∂tR_{0}\coloneqq\partial_{t}, where tt is the coordinate of the factor (−10,10)(-10,10). Note that we have
ρ​ψ∗​R=R0anddistC∞⁡(ρ−2​ψ∗​g,g0)=O⁡(ρ)(ρ→0).\rho\psi^{*}R=R_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{dist}_{C^{\infty}}(\rho^{-2}\psi^{*}g,g_{0})=O(\rho)\quad(\rho\rightarrow 0).
Let E⊂(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)E\subset(-10,10)\times B(0,10) denote the preimage under ψ\psi of all disk components of Σ\Sigma which are fully contained in the image of ψ\psi. Since ρ\rho is small, each component of EE is C∞C^{\infty} close to a disk of the form {t}×B¯​(z,1)\left\{t\right\}\times\overline{B}(z,1) where (t,z)∈(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)(t,z)\in(-10,10)\times B(0,10). This C∞C^{\infty} distance is controlled by ρ\rho and goes to zero as ρ\rho tends to zero.
Let δ>0\delta>0 be a positive number. We say that the surface Σ\Sigma is in δ\delta-good position if the following is true:
(1)
min⁡τ≥δ​ρ\min\tau\geq\delta\rho
(2)
Let ψ:(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)→Y\psi:(-10,10)\times B(0,10)\rightarrow Y be an embedding and E⊂(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)E\subset(-10,10)\times B(0,10) a surface as defined above for an arbitrary choice of point p∈Yp\in Y and identification ℝ2≅⟨Rp⟩⟂\mathbb{R}^{2}\cong\langle R_{p}\rangle^{\perp}. Let pr:(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)→B⁡(0,10)\operatorname{pr}:(-10,10)\times B(0,10)\rightarrow B(0,10) be the projection onto the second factor. Consider the collection of circles pr⁡(∂D)⊂B⁡(0,10)\operatorname{pr}(\partial D)\subset B(0,10), where DD ranges over all disk components of EE. Then this collection of circles does not have any tangency or triple intersection points. Moreover, the distance between any two disjoint circles and the distance between any two distinct intersection points is at least δ\delta.
It is not hard to see that if the centers of the disk components of Σ\Sigma are chosen generically, then Σ\Sigma is in δ\delta-good position for all sufficiently small δ>0\delta>0.
Suppose that Σ\Sigma is in δ\delta-good position. Given ε>0\varepsilon>0, let Σ+\Sigma^{+} be the collection of transverse geodesic disks obtained from Σ\Sigma by increasing the radius of every disk component from ρ\rho to (1+ε)​ρ(1+\varepsilon)\rho while leaving the center fixed. If ε\varepsilon is sufficiently small, then the pair (Σ,Σ+)(\Sigma,\Sigma^{+}) gives rise to a stratification 𝒴\mathcal{Y} of YY as described above. We can choose ε\varepsilon to only depend on δ\delta, for example ε⁡(δ)≔δ2/100\varepsilon(\delta)\coloneqq\delta^{2}/100.
Lemma 9.1.
There exists a universal constant δ>0\delta>0 with the following property. Let YY be a closed 33-manifold, RR a nowhere vanishing vector field on YY, and gg a Riemannian metric such that RR has unit norm. Then, for all sufficiently small ρ>0\rho>0, there exists an exhaustive transverse surface Σ⊂Y\Sigma\subset Y constructed as above as the disjoint union of finitely many transverse geodesic disks of radius ρ\rho such that Σ\Sigma is in δ\delta-good position and such that max⁡τ≤ρ\max\tau\leq\rho.
Proof.
The proof of the lemma is based on the following simple observation. For every δ>0\delta>0, there exists a constant δ′=δ′​(δ)>0\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{\prime}(\delta)>0 such that the following is true for every Riemannian metric gg on (−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)(-10,10)\times B(0,10) which is sufficiently C∞C^{\infty} close to g0g_{0}. Consider a surface E⊂(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)E\subset(-10,10)\times B(0,10) consisting of finitely many disjoint, transverse (with respect to the vector field R0R_{0}) geodesic disks of radius 11. Assume that EE is in δ\delta-good position. By this we mean that the flow line of the vector field R0R_{0} starting at any point in EE can only meet EE again after time at least δ\delta and that the projections of the boundary circles of EE to B⁡(0,10)B(0,10) satisfy the properties in item (2) above. Then there exists a surface E′E^{\prime} obtained from EE by adding disjoint transverse geodesic disks of radius 11 such that E′E^{\prime} is in δ′\delta^{\prime}-good position and such that, for every point (t,z)∈(−5,5)×B⁡(0,5)(t,z)\in(-5,5)\times B(0,5), the flow line of the vector field R0R_{0} starting at (t,z)(t,z) meets E′E^{\prime} within time strictly less than 11.
Let ρ>0\rho>0 be small. Note that there exist a universal postive integer N>0N>0 independent of any of the other quantities involved, a finite set Ψ\Psi of embeddings ψ:(−10,10)×B⁡(0,10)→Y\psi:(-10,10)\times B(0,10)\rightarrow Y defined as above for various choices of points p∈Yp\in Y and trivializations η\eta, and an NN-coloring of Ψ\Psi such that the images of any two distinct embeddings of the same color are disjoint and such that the union over all ψ∈Ψ\psi\in\Psi of the sets ψ⁡((−5,5)×B⁡(0,5))\psi((-5,5)\times B(0,5)) covers YY. Let Ψ=Ψ1⊔⋯⊔ΨN\Psi=\Psi_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\Psi_{N} be the partition of Ψ\Psi induced by the coloring.
Set δ0≔1\delta_{0}\coloneqq 1. For 1≤i≤N1\leq i\leq N, recursively define δi≔δi−1′\delta_{i}\coloneqq\delta_{i-1}^{\prime}, where δi−1′\delta_{i-1}^{\prime} is obtained from δi−1\delta_{i-1} via the above observation. We claim that the assertion of the lemma holds with δ≔δN\delta\coloneqq\delta_{N}. Set Σ0≔∅\Sigma_{0}\coloneqq\emptyset. Then Σ0\Sigma_{0} is clearly in δ0\delta_{0}-good position. Since the images of any two distinct embeddings in Ψ1\Psi_{1} are disjoint, we can apply the above observation for each such embedding separately and obtain a surface Σ1\Sigma_{1} consisting of disjoint transverse geodesic disks of radius ρ\rho which is in δ1\delta_{1}-good position and has the property that TΣ1​(p)≤ρT_{\Sigma_{1}}(p)\leq\rho for all points pp in the union ⋃ψ∈Ψ1ψ⁡((−5,5)×B⁡(0,5))\bigcup_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}}\psi((-5,5)\times B(0,5)). We repeat the same step for all embeddings ψ∈Ψ2\psi\in\Psi_{2} and obtain a surface Σ2\Sigma_{2} in δ2\delta_{2}-good position such that TΣ2​(p)≤ρT_{\Sigma_{2}}(p)\leq\rho for all points pp in ⋃ψ∈Ψ1∪Ψ2ψ⁡((−5,5)×B⁡(0,5))\bigcup_{\psi\in\Psi_{1}\cup\Psi_{2}}\psi((-5,5)\times B(0,5)). We iterate this step for all colors. Then the resulting surface Σ≔ΣN\Sigma\coloneqq\Sigma_{N} is in δ\delta-good position and satisfies max⁡τ≤ρ\max\tau\leq\rho, as desired.
∎
9.2. Stratification of thin products
Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) be a symplectic 44-manifold. Assume that MM is diffeomorphic to [0,1]×Y[0,1]\times Y for some closed 33-manifold YY. Let 0≤s−<s+≤10\leq s_{-}<s_{+}\leq 1 and assume that the difference σ≔s+−s−≪1\sigma\coloneqq s_{+}-s_{-}\ll 1 is small. In order to prove Lemma 8.1, we need to construct a special stratification 𝒳\mathcal{X} of [s−,s+]×Y[s_{-},s_{+}]\times Y whose top-dimensional strata are symplectic cuboids and perturbed symplectic frusta with cuts.
Let RR denote the Hamiltonian vector field induced by the Hamiltonian
H:M=[0,1]×Y→ℝH⁡(s,p)≔−s.H:M=[0,1]\times Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad H(s,p)\coloneqq-s.
Let JJ be a compatible almost complex structure on (M,ω)(M,\omega) such that J∂s=RJ\partial_{s}=R, where ss is the coordinate of the first factor of [0,1]×Y[0,1]\times Y. Let gg denote the Riemannian metric induced by ω\omega and JJ.
Let us fix a constant δ>0\delta>0 as in Lemma 9.1. If σ\sigma is sufficiently small, we may apply Lemma 9.1 with ρ≔σ/10\rho\coloneqq\sigma/10 to the 33-manifold {s−}×Y\left\{s_{-}\right\}\times Y equipped with the restrictions of RR and gg. Let Σ⊂{s−}×Y\Sigma\subset\left\{s_{-}\right\}\times Y be the resulting exhaustive transverse surface in δ\delta-good position. Moreover, let Σ+\Sigma^{+} be the surface obtained from Σ\Sigma by increasing the radii of all disk components from ρ\rho to (1+ε)​ρ(1+\varepsilon)\rho where ε≔ε⁡(δ)=δ2/100\varepsilon\coloneqq\varepsilon(\delta)=\delta^{2}/100 as in Subsection 9.1. We emphasize that δ\delta and ε\varepsilon are fixed universal constants. In particular, they do not change if we shrink σ\sigma.
Let 𝒮\mathcal{S} and 𝒴\mathcal{Y} denote the stratifications of Σ\Sigma and {s−}×Y\left\{s_{-}\right\}\times Y, respectively, induced by the pair of surfaces (Σ,Σ+)(\Sigma,\Sigma^{+}). Recall that the sets of top-dimensional strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} and 𝒴\mathcal{Y} are in bijective correspondence. The set of top-dimensional strata of the stratification 𝒳\mathcal{X} of [s−,s+]×Y[s_{-},s_{+}]\times Y we are about to construct will also be in bijective correspondence with these two sets.
Consider a point p∈Yp\in Y and a unitary trivialization
η:ℂ≅⟨∂s,R(s−,p)⟩⟂⊂T(σ−,p)M.\eta:\mathbb{C}\cong\langle\partial_{s},R_{(s_{-},p)}\rangle^{\perp}\subset T_{(\sigma_{-},p)}M.
Given such a tuple (p,η)(p,\eta), we define an embedding
ψ:[0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)→[s−,s+]×Y\psi:[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)\rightarrow[s_{-},s_{+}]\times Y
as follows: First, we define ψ⁡(0,0,z)≔(s−,expp⁡(σ​η​(z)))\psi(0,0,z)\coloneqq(s_{-},\exp_{p}(\sigma\eta(z))), where exp\exp denotes the exponential map of the restriction of the metric gg to {s−}×Y≅Y\left\{s_{-}\right\}\times Y\cong Y. Consider the hypersurface
W≔[s−,s+]×D⁡(p,2​σ)⊂[s−,s+]×Y,W\coloneqq[s_{-},s_{+}]\times D(p,2\sigma)\subset[s_{-},s_{+}]\times Y,
where D⁡(p,2​σ)⊂YD(p,2\sigma)\subset Y is the transverse geodesic disk defined via the same exponential map. The symplectic form ω\omega induces a characteristic foliation on WW. At the point (s−,p)(s_{-},p), this characteristic foliation is tangent to ∂s\partial_{s}. If σ\sigma is sufficiently small, the characteristic folation is close to being tangent to ∂s\partial_{s} everywhere on WW. In particular, the characteristic leaf starting at a point (s−,q)(s_{-},q) with q∈D⁡(p,σ)q\in D(p,\sigma) is a small perturbation of the line segment [s−,s+]×{q}[s_{-},s_{+}]\times\left\{q\right\}. Let s∈[0,1]s\in[0,1] and z∈B⁡(0,1)z\in B(0,1). We define ψ⁡(s,0,z)\psi(s,0,z) to be the unique intersection point of the characteristic leaf starting at the point ψ⁡(0,0,z)\psi(0,0,z) and the slice {s−+s​σ}×Y\left\{s_{-}+s\sigma\right\}\times Y. Finally, we define ψ⁡(s,t,z)≔φσ​t​(ψ⁡(s,0,z))\psi(s,t,z)\coloneqq\varphi^{\sigma t}(\psi(s,0,z)), where φt\varphi^{t} denotes the flow induced by RR.
Lemma 9.2.
Let Ω0\Omega_{0} denote the standard area form on B⁡(0,1)B(0,1) and let us define the area form Ω\Omega by
Ω≔σ−2ψ(0,0,⋅)∗ω.\Omega\coloneqq\sigma^{-2}\psi(0,0,\cdot)^{*}\omega.
Then we have
σ−2​ψ∗​ω=Ω+d​s∧d​tanddistC∞⁡(Ω,Ω0)=O⁡(σ)(σ→0).\sigma^{-2}\psi^{*}\omega=\Omega+ds\wedge dt\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{dist}_{C^{\infty}}(\Omega,\Omega_{0})=O(\sigma)\quad(\sigma\rightarrow 0).
Proof.
It is immediate from the construction of ψ\psi that the characteristic foliation on [0,1]×{0}×B⁡(0,1)[0,1]\times\left\{0\right\}\times B(0,1) induced by ψ∗​ω\psi^{*}\omega is tangent to the vector field ∂s\partial_{s}. This implies that
(9.1)
ψ∗ω|[0,1]×{0}×B⁡(0,1)=ψ(0,0,⋅)∗ω.\psi^{*}\omega|_{[0,1]\times\left\{0\right\}\times B(0,1)}=\psi(0,0,\cdot)^{*}\omega.
Similarly, it follows from the construction of ψ\psi that the characteristic foliation on {s}×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)\left\{s\right\}\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1) induced by ψ∗​ω\psi^{*}\omega is tangent to the vector field ∂t\partial_{t}. We conclude that
ψ∗ω|{s}×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)=ψ(0,0,⋅)∗ω.\psi^{*}\omega|_{\left\{s\right\}\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)}=\psi(0,0,\cdot)^{*}\omega.
This shows that we can write
σ−2​ψ∗​ω=Ω+d​s∧αs\sigma^{-2}\psi^{*}\omega=\Omega+ds\wedge\alpha_{s}
where (αs)s∈[0,1](\alpha_{s})_{s\in[0,1]} is a family of 11-forms on (−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)(-1,1)\times B(0,1). Note that (9.1) implies that the restriction of αs\alpha_{s} to {0}×B⁡(0,1)\left\{0\right\}\times B(0,1) vanishes. We have
0=d⁡(Ω+d​s∧αs)=d​s∧d​αs.0=d(\Omega+ds\wedge\alpha_{s})=ds\wedge d\alpha_{s}.
This implies that αs\alpha_{s} is a closed 11-form. We can therefore write αs=d​us\alpha_{s}=du_{s} for a function us:(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)→ℝu_{s}:(-1,1)\times B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}. The function usu_{s} is uniquely determined after imposing the normalization us​(0,0)=0u_{s}(0,0)=0. Note that since the restriction of αs\alpha_{s} to {0}×B⁡(0,1)\left\{0\right\}\times B(0,1) vanishes, we then have us​(0,z)=0u_{s}(0,z)=0 for all zz. Note that the Hamiltonian vector field of the Hamiltonian σ−1​ψ∗​H\sigma^{-1}\psi^{*}H with respect to the symplectic form σ−2​ψ∗​ω\sigma^{-2}\psi^{*}\omega is given by σ​ψ∗​R\sigma\psi^{*}R. Moreover, note that
(σ−1ψ∗H)(s,t,z)=−s−σ−1σ−andσψ∗R=∂t.(\sigma^{-1}\psi^{*}H)(s,t,z)=-s-\sigma^{-1}\sigma_{-}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma\psi^{*}R=\partial_{t}.
This implies that αs(∂t)=1\alpha_{s}(\partial_{t})=1. We conclude that us​(t,z)=tu_{s}(t,z)=t.
It remains to prove that distC∞⁡(Ω,Ω0)=O⁡(σ)\operatorname{dist}_{C^{\infty}}(\Omega,\Omega_{0})=O(\sigma) as σ\sigma tends to zero. Let us write Ωσ\Omega_{\sigma} to indicate the dependence of Ω\Omega on σ\sigma. For 0<r≤10<r\leq 1, define the map Sr:B⁡(0,1)→B⁡(0,1)S_{r}:B(0,1)\rightarrow B(0,1) by Sr​(z)≔r​zS_{r}(z)\coloneqq rz. A simple computation shows that
Ωr​σ=r−2​Sr∗​Ωσ.\Omega_{r\sigma}=r^{-2}S_{r}^{*}\Omega_{\sigma}.
Since Ωσ\Omega_{\sigma} agrees with Ω0\Omega_{0} at the center of B⁡(0,1)B(0,1), this implies that distC∞⁡(Ωr​σ,Ω0)=O⁡(r)\operatorname{dist}_{C^{\infty}}(\Omega_{r\sigma},\Omega_{0})=O(r) as rr tends to zero.
∎
Lemma 9.3.
Let ψ,ψ′:[0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)→[s−,s+]×Y\psi,\psi^{\prime}:[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)\rightarrow[s_{-},s_{+}]\times Y be the embeddings induced by two choices of points and unitary trivializations (p,η)(p,\eta) and (p′,η′)(p^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}), respectively. Assume that the intersection U≔im⁡(ψ)∩im⁡(ψ′)U\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\psi)\cap\operatorname{im}(\psi^{\prime}) is non-empty and consider the transition map
χ≔ψ′−1∘ψ:ψ−1​(U)→ψ′−1​(U).\chi\coloneqq\psi^{\prime-1}\circ\psi:\psi^{-1}(U)\rightarrow\psi^{\prime-1}(U).
Then there exist a translation f:ℝ→ℝf:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} and an orientation preserving affine isometry g:ℝ2→ℝ2g:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2} such that
distC∞⁡(χ,(idℝ×f×g)|ψ−1​(U))=O⁡(σ)(σ→0).\operatorname{dist}_{C^{\infty}}(\chi,(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times f\times g)|_{\psi^{-1}(U)})=O(\sigma)\quad(\sigma\rightarrow 0).
Proof.
Let g0g_{0} denote the Riemannian metric on [0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1) obtained by restricting the standard metric on ℝ4\mathbb{R}^{4}. Let us show that distC∞⁡(σ−2​ψ∗​g,g0)=O⁡(σ)\operatorname{dist}_{C^{\infty}}(\sigma^{-2}\psi^{*}g,g_{0})=O(\sigma) as σ\sigma tends to zero. For 0<r≤10<r\leq 1, let ψr\psi_{r} denote the embedding associated to (p,η)(p,\eta), but with the interval [s−,s+][s_{-},s_{+}] replaced by [s−,s−+r⁡(s+−s−)][s_{-},s_{-}+r(s_{+}-s_{-})]. Define a map
Sr:[0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)→[0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)Sr​(s,t,z)≔(r​s,r​t,r​z).S_{r}:[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)\rightarrow[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)\qquad S_{r}(s,t,z)\coloneqq(rs,rt,rz).
It is straightforward to check that ψr=ψ∘Sr\psi_{r}=\psi\circ S_{r} and hence that
(r​σ)−2​ψr∗​g=r−2​Sr∗​(σ−2​ψ∗​g).(r\sigma)^{-2}\psi_{r}^{*}g=r^{-2}S_{r}^{*}(\sigma^{-2}\psi^{*}g).
Note that σ−2​ψ∗​g\sigma^{-2}\psi^{*}g agrees with g0g_{0} at the point (0,0,0)(0,0,0). This implies that distC∞⁡((r​σ)−2​ψr∗​g,g0)=O⁡(r)\operatorname{dist}_{C^{\infty}}((r\sigma)^{-2}\psi_{r}^{*}g,g_{0})=O(r), as desired.
We observe that the transition map χ\chi is an isometry with respect to the metric σ−2​ψ∗​g\sigma^{-2}\psi^{*}g on ψ−1​(U)\psi^{-1}(U) and the metric σ−2​(ψ′)∗​g\sigma^{-2}(\psi^{\prime})^{*}g on (ψ′)−1​(U)(\psi^{\prime})^{-1}(U). Both of these metrics have C∞C^{\infty} distance O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) from g0g_{0} by the above. Moreover, by definition of ψ\psi and ψ′\psi^{\prime}, the map χ\chi preserves the coordinate of the first factor of [0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1) and we have χ∗∂t=∂t\chi_{*}\partial_{t}=\partial_{t}. Combining these observations, we see that χ\chi must have C∞C^{\infty} distance O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) from a map of the form idℝ×f×g\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times f\times g for a translation ff and an affine isometry gg.
∎
For each disk component D={s−}×D⁡(p,ρ)D=\left\{s_{-}\right\}\times D(p,\rho) of Σ\Sigma, pick an arbitrary unitary trivialization η:ℂ≅⟨∂s,R(s−,p)⟩⟂\eta:\mathbb{C}\cong\langle\partial_{s},R_{(s_{-},p)}\rangle^{\perp} and let ψD:[0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)→[σ−,σ+]×Y\psi_{D}:[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)\rightarrow[\sigma_{-},\sigma_{+}]\times Y be the embedding associated to (p,η)(p,\eta). Note that ψD−1​(D)={0}×{0}×B⁡(0,1/10)\psi_{D}^{-1}(D)=\left\{0\right\}\times\left\{0\right\}\times B(0,1/10). Moreover, it follows from Lemma 9.3 that, for every disk component D′D^{\prime} of Σ\Sigma which intersects the image of ψD\psi_{D}, there exist a point (t,z)∈ℝ×ℝ2(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2} and a perturbation EE of the disk {0}×{t}×B⁡(z,1/10)\left\{0\right\}\times\left\{t\right\}\times B(z,1/10) of C∞C^{\infty} size O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) such that ψD−1​(D′)=E∩([0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1))\psi_{D}^{-1}(D^{\prime})=E\cap([0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)). Let us assume that σ\sigma is sufficiently small such that the C∞C^{\infty} size of the perturbation is much smaller than ε\varepsilon.
Let us define the auxiliary hypersurface WD≔ψD​([0,1]×{0}×B⁡(0,1))W_{D}\coloneqq\psi_{D}([0,1]\times\left\{0\right\}\times B(0,1)). Again, it follows from Lemma 9.3 that, for each disk component D′D^{\prime} of Σ\Sigma such that WD′W_{D^{\prime}} intersects the image of ψD\psi_{D}, there exist (t,z)∈ℝ×ℝ2(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2} and a perturbation VV of [0,1]×{t}×B⁡(z,1)[0,1]\times\left\{t\right\}\times B(z,1) of C∞C^{\infty} size O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) such that ψD−1​(WD′)=V∩([0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1))\psi_{D}^{-1}(W_{D^{\prime}})=V\cap([0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)). We assume that σ\sigma is sufficiently small such that the C∞C^{\infty} size of the perturbation is much smaller than ε\varepsilon. In the following, we perturb the hypersurfaces WDW_{D} while preserving this property.
Consider a top-dimensional stratum CC of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S1). Hence the disks DiD_{i} are contained in the image of ψD\psi_{D}. Let pr23:[0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)→{0}×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)\operatorname{pr}_{23}:[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1)\rightarrow\left\{0\right\}\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1) denote the projection onto the second and third factor. For i∈{1,2,3}i\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}, let us now perturb WDiW_{D_{i}} such that
ψD−1​(WDi)∩Nε​(pr23−1​ψD−1​(C¯𝒴))\psi_{D}^{-1}(W_{D_{i}})\cap N_{\varepsilon}(\operatorname{pr}_{23}^{-1}\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{Y}}))
is contained in [0,1]×{ti}×B⁡(0,1)[0,1]\times\left\{t_{i}\right\}\times B(0,1). Here NεN_{\varepsilon} denotes the ε\varepsilon-neighbourhood as measured with respect to the standard metric on [0,1]×(−1,1)×B⁡(0,1)[0,1]\times(-1,1)\times B(0,1). We can arrange this perturbation to be supported inside N2​ε​(pr23−1​ψD−1​(C¯𝒴))N_{2\varepsilon}(\operatorname{pr}_{23}^{-1}\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{Y}})). Moreover, we can make sure that the C∞C^{\infty} size of this perturbation measured in the coordinate chart ψD\psi_{D} is O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) and in particular much smaller than ε\varepsilon. Let us repeat this step for all top-dimensional strata CC of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S1). Note that for two distinct such strata CC and C′C^{\prime}, the modifications take place in disjoint regions because C𝒴C_{\mathcal{Y}} and C𝒴′C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{Y}} are sufficiently far apart. We abbreviate the perturbed hypersurfaces by the same symbol WDW_{D}. Let us also perturb (Σ,Σ+)(\Sigma,\Sigma^{+}) by flowing the disk components (D,D+)(D,D^{+}) along the characteristic foliation on {s−}×Y\left\{s_{-}\right\}\times Y such that they are contained in WDW_{D}. The modified surface pair and its induced stratifications are still denoted by (Σ,Σ+)(\Sigma,\Sigma^{+}), 𝒮\mathcal{S}, and 𝒴\mathcal{Y}.
For each disk component DD of Σ\Sigma, let VDV_{D} denote the union of all characteristic leaves on WDW_{D} which intersect DD. Let VΣV_{\Sigma} be the union of all VDV_{D} as DD ranges over the disk components of Σ\Sigma. We define a diffeomorphism α:[0,σ]×Σ→VΣ\alpha:[0,\sigma]\times\Sigma\rightarrow V_{\Sigma} by declaring α⁡(s,p)\alpha(s,p) to be the unique intersection point of the characteristic leaf starting at pp with the slice {s−+s}×Y\left\{s_{-}+s\right\}\times Y. Moreover, we define a map τ:VΣ→ℝ>0\tau:V_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0} by
τ⁡(q)≔inf{t>0∣φt​(q)∈VΣ}\tau(q)\coloneqq\inf\left\{t>0\mid\varphi^{t}(q)\in V_{\Sigma}\right\}
We are now in a position to describe the top-dimensional strata of 𝒳\mathcal{X}. Let CC be a top-dimensional stratum of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S1). We define the corresponding stratum C𝒳C_{\mathcal{X}} of 𝒳\mathcal{X} to be
C𝒳≔{φt(p)∣p∈α([0,σ]×C),0<t<τ(p)}.C_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq\left\{\varphi^{t}(p)\mid p\in\alpha([0,\sigma]\times C),\kern 5.0pt0<t<\tau(p)\right\}.
We claim that C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} is a symplectic cuboid. Indeed, let DD denote the disk component of Σ\Sigma containing CC. Then it follows from our construction of the hypersurfaces WDW_{D} that
ψD−1​(C¯𝒳)=[0,1]×[0,T]×ψD−1​(C¯)\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}})=[0,1]\times[0,T]\times\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C})
for some T>0T>0. Since σ−2​ψD∗​ω=Ω+d​s∧d​t\sigma^{-2}\psi_{D}^{*}\omega=\Omega+ds\wedge dt for some area form Ω\Omega on B⁡(0,1)B(0,1) by Lemma 9.2, we see that C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} is a symplectic cuboid. It is conformally symplectomorphic to the cuboid Q⁡(T,A)Q(T,A) where AA denotes the Ω\Omega-area of ψD−1​(C¯)\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}). Note that we can bound TT and AA from above and below purely in terms of the universal constant δ\delta. This shows that we can find a universal compact set 𝒬\mathcal{Q} of symplectic cuboids such that every stratum C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} associated to a stratum CC of type (S1) is conformally symplectomorphic to an element of 𝒬\mathcal{Q}.
Next, consider a top-dimensional stratum CC of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S2). Again, let DD denote the disk component of Σ\Sigma containing CC and let AA be the Ω\Omega-area of ψD−1​(C)\psi_{D}^{-1}(C). Choose an area preserving parametrization β:[0,σ​A]×[0,σ]→C¯\beta:[0,\sigma A]\times[0,\sigma]\rightarrow\overline{C} such that the boundary edges of CC bordering strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S3) are given by β⁡({0}×[0,σ])\beta(\left\{0\right\}\times[0,\sigma]) and β⁡({σ​A}×[0,σ])\beta(\left\{\sigma A\right\}\times[0,\sigma]). The boundary edges β⁡([0,σ​A]×{0})\beta([0,\sigma A]\times\left\{0\right\}) and β⁡([0,σ​A]×{σ})\beta([0,\sigma A]\times\left\{\sigma\right\}) either border strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S1) or are contained in the boundary of DD.
Define a function f:[0,σ​A]×[0,σ]→[0,σ]f:[0,\sigma A]\times[0,\sigma]\rightarrow[0,\sigma] by the formula
f⁡(x,y)≔{3​x/Aif 0≤x≤σ​A/3σif σ​A/3≤x≤2​σ​A/33​σ−3​x/Aif 2​σ​A/3≤x≤σ​A.f(x,y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}3x/A&\text{if $0\leq x\leq\sigma A/3$}\\
\sigma&\text{if $\sigma A/3\leq x\leq 2\sigma A/3$}\\
3\sigma-3x/A&\text{if $2\sigma A/3\leq x\leq\sigma A$}.\end{cases}
We define
C𝒳≔{φt(p)∣p∈α∘(idℝ×β)(intD(f)),0<t<τ(p)}.C_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq\left\{\varphi^{t}(p)\mid p\in\alpha\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\beta)(\operatorname{int}D(f)),\kern 5.0pt0<t<\tau(p)\right\}.
Here we recall that D⁡(f)⊂ℝ×[0,σ​A]×[0,σ]D(f)\subset\mathbb{R}\times[0,\sigma A]\times[0,\sigma] denotes the subgraph of ff truncated at zero. See Figure 9.4 for an illustration.
D
Figure 9.4. For some disk component DD of Σ\Sigma, the figure shows the intersection with VDV_{D} of all closures C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} of strata of 𝒳\mathcal{X} associated to strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of types (S1) and (S2).
We claim that C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} is a perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts. Indeed, it follows from the way we adjusted the hypersurfaces WDW_{D} near the already constructed strata C𝒳′C_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime} with C′C^{\prime} of type (S1) that, for y∈{0,σ}y\in\left\{0,\sigma\right\}, a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
{φt​(p)∣p∈α∘(idℝ×β)​(D⁡(f|[0,σ​A]×{y}))}\left\{\varphi^{t}(p)\mid p\in\alpha\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\beta)(D(f|_{[0,\sigma A]\times\left\{y\right\}}))\right\}
in C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} looks eactly like a neighbourhood of a cut region in a perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts; see (B) in Figure 7.3. Moreover, after undoing these two cuts one obtains a perturbed symplectic frustum. Note that C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} is conformally symplectomorphic to ψD−1​(C¯𝒳)\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}}) equipped with the symplectic form Ω+d​s∧d​t\Omega+ds\wedge dt. Since the hypersurfaces ψD−1​(WD′)\psi_{D}^{-1}(W_{D^{\prime}}) for other disk components D′D^{\prime} of Σ\Sigma are contained in perturbations of C∞C^{\infty} size O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) of hypersurfaces of the form [0,1]×{t}×B⁡(0,1)[0,1]\times\left\{t\right\}\times B(0,1), the perturbed symplectic frustum underlying ψD−1​(C¯𝒳)\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}}) has, up to symplectomorphism, C∞C^{\infty} distance O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) from an actual symplectic frustum F=F⁡(a,b,c)F=F(a,b,c). Again we can bound a,b,ca,b,c and also c−bc-b from above and below by universal constants only depending on δ\delta. This implies that FF is contained in some universal compact set ℱ\mathcal{F} of symplectic frusta.
For each disk component DD of Σ\Sigma, let D⊂D0⊂D+D\subset D^{0}\subset D^{+} be a disk whose boundary circle ∂D0\partial D^{0} lies halfway between the boundary circles ∂D\partial D and ∂D+\partial D^{+}. Moreover, let VD0V_{D^{0}} be the union of all characteristic leaves of WDW_{D} intersecting D0D^{0}. Let Σ0\Sigma^{0} denote the union of all disks D0D^{0}. Similarly, let VΣ0V_{\Sigma^{0}} denote the union of all VD0V_{D^{0}}. Define AA to be the union of VΣ0V_{\Sigma^{0}} with all C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} where CC ranges over strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of types (S1) and (S2). The remaining top-dimensional strata of 𝒳\mathcal{X} are given by the connected components of ((0,σ)×Y)∖A((0,\sigma)\times Y)\setminus A. These connected components are in bijective correspondence with strata of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S3).
We claim that for each stratum CC of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S3), the closure of the corresponding stratum C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} is symplectomorphic to a perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts, sitting inside [0,σ]×Y[0,\sigma]\times Y upside down; see Figure 9.4. Consider a stratum C′C^{\prime} of 𝒮\mathcal{S} of type (S1) such that (C¯𝒳′∩C¯𝒳)∖VΣ(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\cap\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}})\setminus V_{\Sigma} is non-empty. Then the intersection of C¯𝒳\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}} with a neighbourhood of C¯𝒳′\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime} looks like a neighbourhood of a cut region in a perturbed symplectic frustum with cuts; see (A) and (C) in Figure 7.3. Undoing all these cuts yields a perturbed symplectic frustum. As before, let DD be the disk component of Σ\Sigma containing CC and consider ψD−1​(C¯𝒳)\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}}) equipped with Ω+d​s∧d​t\Omega+ds\wedge dt. As above, the perturbed symplectic frustum obtained by undoing all cuts of ψD−1​(C¯𝒳)\psi_{D}^{-1}(\overline{C}_{\mathcal{X}}) has, up to symplectomorphism, C∞C^{\infty} distance O⁡(σ)O(\sigma) from an actual symplectic frustum FF, which can be taken to be contained in a universal compact set ℱ\mathcal{F} of symplectic frusta. This concludes the proof of Lemma 8.1. ∎
10. Bounding the subleading asymptotics
The goal of this section is to prove Theorems D, E, and F.
Proof of Theorem D.
The proof follows the same strategy as the original proof of the ECH Weyl law in [Hut11a]. We proceed in several steps.
Step 1: We claim that the error terms are bounded for balls, i.e. ek​(B⁡(a))=O⁡(1)e_{k}(B(a))=O(1). Indeed, by [Hut22a, Example 1.2], we have
lim infk→∞ek​(B⁡(a))=−32​aandlim supk→∞ek​(B⁡(a))=−12​a,\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}e_{k}(B(a))=-\frac{3}{2}a\qquad\text{and}\qquad\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}e_{k}(B(a))=-\frac{1}{2}a,
which clearly implies that ek​(B⁡(a))=O⁡(1)e_{k}(B(a))=O(1).
Step 2: We show that the error terms are bounded for all finite disjoint unions of balls. In fact, we show the following more general statement: Suppose that X1,…,XnX_{1},\dots,X_{n} is a finite collection of symplectic 44-manifolds such that ek​(Xi)=O⁡(1)e_{k}(X_{i})=O(1) for all ii. Let X≔⊔iXiX\coloneqq\sqcup_{i}X_{i} be the disjoint union. Then we also have ek​(X)=O​(1)e_{k}(X)=O(1).
Recall from [Hut22a, Equation 1.4] that the ECH capacities of the disjoint union XX are given by
(10.1)
ck​(X)=max∑iki=k⁡cki​(Xi).c_{k}(X)=\max_{\sum_{i}k_{i}=k}c_{k_{i}}(X_{i}).
Here the maximum is taken over all tuples of non-negative integers (k1,…,kn)(k_{1},\dots,k_{n}) whose sum is kk. Set V≔vol⁡(X)V\coloneqq\operatorname{vol}(X) and Vi≔vol⁡(Xi)V_{i}\coloneqq\operatorname{vol}(X_{i}). The desired statement follows immediately if we can verify that
(10.2)
max⁡∑i∑iki=k⁡Vi​ki=V​k+O⁡(1)(k→∞).\max_{\sum\limits_{i}k_{i}=k}\sum\limits_{i}\sqrt{V_{i}k_{i}}=\sqrt{Vk}+O(1)\qquad(k\rightarrow\infty).
Consider the function
f:ℝ≥0n→ℝf⁡(x1,…,xn)≔∑iVi​xi.f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\coloneqq\sum\limits_{i}\sqrt{V_{i}x_{i}}.
Let k∈ℤ>0k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}. It follows from a direct computation that the maximum of ff subject to the constraint ∑ixi≤k\sum\limits_{i}x_{i}\leq k is attained at the point k/V⋅(V1,…,Vn)k/V\cdot(V_{1},\dots,V_{n}). In particular, this implies that
(10.3)
∑iVi​⌊Vi​kV⌋≤max⁡∑i∑iki=k⁡Vi​ki≤∑iVi2​kV=V​k.\sum\limits_{i}\sqrt{V_{i}\left\lfloor\frac{V_{i}k}{V}\right\rfloor}\leq\max_{\sum\limits_{i}k_{i}=k}\sum\limits_{i}\sqrt{V_{i}k_{i}}\leq\sum\limits_{i}\sqrt{\frac{V_{i}^{2}k}{V}}=\sqrt{Vk}.
We compute
(10.4)
∑iVi​⌊Vi​kV⌋=∑iVi2​kV+O⁡(1)=V​k+O⁡(1).\sum\limits_{i}\sqrt{V_{i}\left\lfloor\frac{V_{i}k}{V}\right\rfloor}=\sum\limits_{i}\sqrt{\frac{V_{i}^{2}k}{V}}+O(1)=\sqrt{Vk}+O(1).
The desired identity (10.2) is immediate from (10.3) and (10.4).
Step 3: Let X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} be a compact domain with smooth boundary. We show that the sequence of error terms ek​(X)e_{k}(X) is bounded from below. By Theorem A, ball packing stability holds for XX. Pick a positive integer nn such that pn​(X)=1p_{n}(X)=1. Let a>0a>0 be the unique real number such that n​vol⁡(B⁡(a))=vol⁡(X)n\operatorname{vol}(B(a))=\operatorname{vol}(X). Then for every b<ab<a, the disjoint union of nn copies of B⁡(b)B(b) admits a symplectic embedding into int⁡(X)\operatorname{int}(X). By monotonicity of ECH capacities [Hut22a, Equation 1.2], we obtain the inequality
ck​(⨆i=1nB⁡(b))≤ck​(X)c_{k}(\bigsqcup\limits_{i=1}^{n}B(b))\leq c_{k}(X)
for all b<ab<a. Since the left hand side is continuous in bb, the inequality continues to hold for b=ab=a. Using that ⊔iB(a)\sqcup_{i}B(a) has the same volume as XX and bounded error terms by Step 2, we conclude that the error terms ek​(X)e_{k}(X) are bounded from below.
Step 4: We show that the sequence of error terms ek​(X)e_{k}(X) is bounded from above for every compact domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} with smooth boundary. Let B⁡(A)B(A) be a large ball containing XX in its interior. By Theorem A, ball packing stability holds for W≔B⁡(A)∖int⁡(X)W\coloneqq B(A)\setminus\operatorname{int}(X). Pick n≥1n\geq 1 such that pn​(W)=1p_{n}(W)=1. Let a>0a>0 be the real number such that n​vol⁡(B⁡(a))=vol⁡(W)n\operatorname{vol}(B(a))=\operatorname{vol}(W). Then for every b<ab<a, the disjoint union of nn copies of B⁡(b)B(b) embeds into int⁡(W)\operatorname{int}(W) and monotonicity implies the inequality
ck​(X⊔⨆i=1nB⁡(b))≤ck​(B⁡(A)).c_{k}(X\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits_{i=1}^{n}B(b))\leq c_{k}(B(A)).
Again, this inequality continues to hold for b=ab=a. The disjoint union formula (10.1) then implies that
(10.5)
ck(X)+cℓ(⊔iB(a))≤ck+ℓ(B(A))c_{k}(X)+c_{\ell}(\sqcup_{i}B(a))\leq c_{k+\ell}(B(A))
for all ℓ≥0\ell\geq 0. Set
V1≔vol(X),V2≔vol(⊔iB(a)),V≔vol(B(A))=V1+V2.V_{1}\coloneqq\operatorname{vol}(X),\quad V_{2}\coloneqq\operatorname{vol}(\sqcup_{i}B(a)),\quad V\coloneqq\operatorname{vol}(B(A))=V_{1}+V_{2}.
Since the error terms of B⁡(A)B(A) and of ⊔iB(a)\sqcup_{i}B(a) are bounded by Steps 1 and 2, an upper bound on the error terms of XX follows easily from inequality (10.5) if we can show that
infℓ≥0V⁡(k+ℓ)−V1​k−V2​ℓ\inf_{\ell\geq 0}\sqrt{V(k+\ell)}-\sqrt{V_{1}k}-\sqrt{V_{2}\ell}
admits a finite upper bound independent of kk. This can be checked via an elementary computation in the same spirit as the one given in Step 2. We omit the details.
∎
Proof of Theorem E.
We closely follow the strategy of the proof of [EH, Theorem 8.1] and adapt the notation from that paper.
We begin by observing that is suffices to show the inequality
(10.6)
cσi​(ϕ,γi,H+)−cσi​(ϕ,γi,H−)+∫γi(H+−H−)​𝑑t≥di​A−1​∫Yϕ(H+−H−)​𝑑t∧ωϕ+O⁡(1)c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{+})-c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{-})+\int_{\gamma_{i}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt\geq d_{i}A^{-1}\int_{Y_{\phi}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt\wedge\omega_{\phi}+O(1)
for all Hamiltonians H±∈C∞​(Yϕ)H_{\pm}\in C^{\infty}(Y_{\phi}). Indeed, switching H+H_{+} and H−H_{-} in inequality (10.6) and multiplying the resulting inequality by −1-1 yields the reverse inequality. Next, we observe that inequality (10.6) is invariant under shifting the Hamiltonians H±H_{\pm} by constants. More precisely, if C±∈ℝC_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R} are real numbers, then inequality (10.6) holds for the pair (H+,H−)(H_{+},H_{-}) if and only if it holds for the pair (H++C+,H−+C−)(H_{+}+C_{+},H_{-}+C_{-}). After replacing H+H_{+} by H++CH_{+}+C for a sufficiently large constant C≥0C\geq 0, we can assume that H+>H−H_{+}>H_{-}. Let MM denote the symplectic cobordism between the graphs of H+H_{+} and H−H_{-}. By Theorem A, ball packing stability holds for MM. Pick N≥1N\geq 1 such that pN​(M)=1p_{N}(M)=1. Let a>0a>0 be the real number such that N​vol⁡(B⁡(a))=vol⁡(M)N\operatorname{vol}(B(a))=\operatorname{vol}(M). Then for all b<ab<a, the disjoint union of NN copies of B⁡(b)B(b) admits a symplectic embedding into int⁡(M)\operatorname{int}(M). It follows from [EH, Lemma 5.2] that for all non-negative integers ki≥0k_{i}\geq 0 and for all b<ab<a we have
(10.7)
cσi​(ϕ,γi,H+)−cUki​σi​(ϕ,γi,H−)+∫γi(H+−H−)​𝑑t≥ckialt​(⨆j=1NB⁡(b)).c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{+})-c_{U^{k_{i}}\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{-})+\int_{\gamma_{i}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt\geq c_{k_{i}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(\bigsqcup\limits_{j=1}^{N}B(b)).
Here ckaltc_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}} denote the elementary alternatives to ECH capacities defined in [Hut22b], see Remark 1.2. It is a consequence of [Hut22b, Theorem 6] that the alternative capacities ckaltc_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}} agree with the usual ECH capacities ckc_{k} on balls and disjoint unions of balls. Note that inequality (10.7) continues to hold for b=ab=a by continuity.
Let m≥1m\geq 1 be an integer such that all classes σi\sigma_{i} are UU-cyclic of order mm. Such mm exists by [CPPZ]. We abbreviate
V≔vol(⊔jB(a))=vol(M)=∫Yϕ(H+−H−)dt∧ωϕ.V\coloneqq\operatorname{vol}(\sqcup_{j}B(a))=\operatorname{vol}(M)=\int_{Y_{\phi}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt\wedge\omega_{\phi}.
and set
ni≔⌊di2​Vm​A2​(di−g+1)⌋andki=m​ni​(di−g+1).n_{i}\coloneqq\left\lfloor\frac{d_{i}^{2}V}{mA^{2}(d_{i}-g+1)}\right\rfloor\qquad\text{and}\qquad k_{i}=mn_{i}(d_{i}-g+1).
By [EH, Proposition 4.2(a)], we have
cUki​σi​(ϕ,γi,H−)=cσi​(ϕ,γi,H−)−m​ni​A.c_{U^{k_{i}}\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{-})=c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{-})-mn_{i}A.
Using this, inequality (10.7) with b=ab=a can be rewritten as
(10.8)
cσi(ϕ,γi,H+)−cσi(ϕ,γi,H−)+∫γi(H+−H−)dt≥cki(⊔jB(a))−mniA.c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{+})-c_{\sigma_{i}}(\phi,\gamma_{i},H_{-})+\int_{\gamma_{i}}(H_{+}-H_{-})dt\geq c_{k_{i}}(\sqcup_{j}B(a))-mn_{i}A.
Using Theorem D, we compute
cki(⊔jB(a))\displaystyle c_{k_{i}}(\sqcup_{j}B(a))
=2​V​ki+O⁡(1)\displaystyle=2\sqrt{Vk_{i}}+O(1)
=2​V​m⁡(di−g+1)​⌊di2​Vm​A2​(di−g+1)⌋+O⁡(1)\displaystyle=2\sqrt{V}\sqrt{m(d_{i}-g+1)\left\lfloor\frac{d_{i}^{2}V}{mA^{2}(d_{i}-g+1)}\right\rfloor}+O(1)
=2​V​di2​VA2+O⁡(di)+O⁡(1)\displaystyle=2\sqrt{V}\sqrt{\frac{d_{i}^{2}V}{A^{2}}+O(d_{i})}+O(1)
=2​A−1​V​di​1+O⁡(di−1)+O⁡(1)\displaystyle=2A^{-1}Vd_{i}\sqrt{1+O(d_{i}^{-1})}+O(1)
=2​A−1​V​di+O⁡(1).\displaystyle=2A^{-1}Vd_{i}+O(1).
Moreover, we compute
m​ni​A\displaystyle mn_{i}A
=m​A​⌊di2​Vm​A2​(di−g+1)⌋\displaystyle=mA\left\lfloor\frac{d_{i}^{2}V}{mA^{2}(d_{i}-g+1)}\right\rfloor
=A−1​V​di​didi−g+1+O⁡(1)\displaystyle=A^{-1}Vd_{i}\frac{d_{i}}{d_{i}-g+1}+O(1)
=A−1​V​di+O⁡(1).\displaystyle=A^{-1}Vd_{i}+O(1).
Combining these two computations with inequality (10.8) yields the desired inequality (10.6).
∎
Proof of Theorem F.
We begin by observing that it suffices to establish the inequality
(10.9)
cL¯d​(H)≤A−1​∫[0,1]×ΣH​𝑑t∧ω+O⁡(d−1)c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)\leq A^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}Hdt\wedge\omega+O(d^{-1})
for every Hamiltonian H∈C∞​([0,1]×Σ)H\in C^{\infty}([0,1]\times\Sigma). Indeed, substituting H¯\overline{H} for HH in inequality (10.9) yields
(10.10)
cL¯d(H¯)≤A−1∫[0,1]×ΣH¯dt∧ω+O(d−1)=−A−1∫[0,1]×ΣHdt∧ω+O(d−1).c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\overline{H})\leq A^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}\overline{H}dt\wedge\omega+O(d^{-1})=-A^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}Hdt\wedge\omega+O(d^{-1}).
By the subadditivity property in [CHMSS22, Theorem 1.13], we have
(10.11)
0=cL¯d​(0)=cL¯d​(H​#​H¯)≤cL¯d​(H)+cL¯d​(H¯).0=c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(0)=c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H\#\overline{H})\leq c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)+c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\overline{H}).
Combining inequalities (10.10) and (10.11) yields the desired reverse inequality
cL¯d​(H)≥A−1​∫[0,1]×ΣH​𝑑t∧ω+O⁡(d−1).c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)\geq A^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}Hdt\wedge\omega+O(d^{-1}).
It remains to prove inequality (10.9). This involves the relationship between PFH and link spectral invariants established by Chen [Chea, Cheb]. He considers PFH spectral invariants cσ​(ϕ,γ,H)c_{\sigma}(\phi,\gamma,H) in the special case ϕ=idΣ\phi=\operatorname{id}_{\Sigma}. In this case, the mapping torus YϕY_{\phi} is simply given by the product 𝕋×Σ\mathbb{T}\times\Sigma. For every degree d>0d>0, Chen considers the special reference cycle γd≔d⋅(𝕋×{∗})\gamma_{d}\coloneqq d\cdot(\mathbb{T}\times\left\{*\right\}) for some point ∗∈Σ*\in\Sigma. He constructs a distinguished non-zero PFH class ed∈H​P​(idΣ,γd)e_{d}\in HP(\operatorname{id}_{\Sigma},\gamma_{d}) called the “PHF unit” and shows the inequality
(10.12)
d​cL¯d​(H)≤ced​(idΣ,γd,H)+∫γdH​𝑑t.dc_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)\leq c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{\Sigma},\gamma_{d},H)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}Hdt.
Here we follow the notational conventions for link and PFH spectral invariants from [CHMSS22] and [EH], which differ from Chen’s conventions. In particular, Chen absorbs the factor dd into the link spectral invariant cL¯d​(H)c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H). By Theorem E, we have
(10.13)
ced​(idΣ,γd,H)−ced​(idΣ,γd,0)+∫γdH​𝑑t=d​A−1​∫[0,1]×ΣH​𝑑t∧ω+O⁡(1).c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{\Sigma},\gamma_{d},H)-c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{\Sigma},\gamma_{d},0)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}Hdt=dA^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times\Sigma}Hdt\wedge\omega+O(1).
It can be seen from the description given in [Chea, Cheb] of the class ede_{d} in the PFH chain complex of φG1\varphi_{G}^{1} for a small Morse function GG that the spectral invariant ced​(idΣ,γd,0)c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{\Sigma},\gamma_{d},0) vanishes. The desired inequality (10.9) hence follows from (10.12) and (10.13).
∎
11. C2−εC^{2-\varepsilon} failure of packing stability
In this section we prove Theorem C, i.e. we construct a star-shaped domain X⊂ℝ4X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} which is C1C^{1} close to the unit ball, whose boundary is C1,αC^{1,\alpha} for all α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1) and smooth on the complement of a single point, and for which packing stability fails.
We begin by recalling a construction from [Edt24] which, given a 11-periodic Hamiltonian HH on the unit disk 𝔻⊂ℂ\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{C}, yields a domain X⁡(H)⊂ℂ2X(H)\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} such that the characteristic flow on the boundary of X⁡(H)X(H) lifts the Hamiltonian flow on 𝔻\mathbb{D} induced by HH. It will be convenient to follow the exposition in [ABE, §1]. Recall that 𝕋≔ℝ/ℤ\mathbb{T}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z} denotes the circle. Define the domain
U≔{(s,t,z)∈ℝ×𝕋×ℂ∣s>π⁡(|z|2−1)}⊂ℝ×𝕋×ℂU\coloneqq\left\{(s,t,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}\mid s>\pi(|z|^{2}-1)\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}
and consider the diffeomorphism
Φ:U→ℂ∗×ℂΦ⁡(s,t,z)≔e2​π​i​t​(1+sπ−|z|2,z).\Phi:U\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\qquad\Phi(s,t,z)\coloneqq e^{2\pi it}\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{s}{\pi}-|z|^{2}},z\right).
We equip UU with the symplectic form d​s∧d​t+ωds\wedge dt+\omega, where ω\omega denotes the standard area form on ℂ\mathbb{C}. Moreover, we equip ℂ∗×ℂ\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C} with the restriction of the standard symplectic form ω0\omega_{0} on ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}. A simple computation shows that Φ\Phi is a symplectomorphism with respect to these symplectic forms. Note that the image under Φ\Phi of a set of the form {(s,t,z)∈U∣s<s0}\left\{(s,t,z)\in U\mid s<s_{0}\right\} is an open ball with the complex line {z1=0}\left\{z_{1}=0\right\} removed. Let H:𝕋×𝔻→ℝH:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} be a compactly supported Hamiltonian such that
H⁡(t,z)>π⁡(|z|2−1)for all (t,z)∈𝕋×𝔻.H(t,z)>\pi(|z|^{2}-1)\qquad\text{for all $(t,z)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{D}$.}
We define
U(H)≔{(s,t,z)∈U∣z∈𝔻,s<H(t,z)}⊂UU(H)\coloneqq\left\{(s,t,z)\in U\mid z\in\mathbb{D},s<H(t,z)\right\}\subset U
and set
X⁡(H)≔clos⁡(Φ⁡(U⁡(H)))⊂ℂ2.X(H)\coloneqq\operatorname{clos}(\Phi(U(H)))\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}.
The volume of X⁡(H)X(H) is given by
(11.1)
vol⁡(X⁡(H))=vol⁡(B4​(π))+∫[0,1]×𝔻H​𝑑t∧ω.\operatorname{vol}(X(H))=\operatorname{vol}(B^{4}(\pi))+\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{D}}Hdt\wedge\omega.
If the Hamiltonian HH has regularity Ck,αC^{k,\alpha}, then the same is true for the boundary of X⁡(H)X(H). If HH is Ck,αC^{k,\alpha} small, then X⁡(H)X(H) is Ck,αC^{k,\alpha} close to the unit ball in ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}. The desired domain X⊂ℂ2X\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} for which packing stability fails will be of the form X⁡(H)X(H) for a carefully constructed C1C^{1} small Hamiltonian HH which is C1,αC^{1,\alpha} for all α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1).
We turn to the definition of HH. Fix a positive parameter λ>0\lambda>0. For n≥10n\geq 10, consider the annulus 𝔸n⊂𝔻\mathbb{A}_{n}\subset\mathbb{D} with the same center as 𝔻\mathbb{D}, inner radius Rn−≔2ln⁡(n)R_{n}^{-}\coloneqq\frac{2}{\ln(n)}, and outer radius Rn+≔2ln⁡(n−1)R_{n}^{+}\coloneqq\frac{2}{\ln(n-1)}. Note that the interiors of the annuli 𝔸n\mathbb{A}_{n} are pairwise disjoint and that the width Rn+−Rn−R_{n}^{+}-R_{n}^{-} of 𝔸n\mathbb{A}_{n} is strictly bigger than 2n​ln⁡(n)2\frac{2}{n\ln(n)^{2}}. For each nn, we choose nn pairwise disjoint disks Dn1,…,DnnD_{n}^{1},\dots,D_{n}^{n} of radius rn≔1n​ln⁡(n)2r_{n}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n\ln(n)^{2}} inside the interior of 𝔸n\mathbb{A}_{n}. In order to see that this is possible, note that the diameter 2​rn=2n​ln⁡(n)22r_{n}=\frac{2}{n\ln(n)^{2}} of each of the disks DniD_{n}^{i} is strictly smaller than the width of 𝔸n\mathbb{A}_{n} and the sum of the diameters of the nn disks DniD_{n}^{i}, which is given by 2​rn​n=2ln⁡(n)22r_{n}n=\frac{2}{\ln(n)^{2}}, is strictly smaller than the circumference of the inner boundary circle of 𝔸n\mathbb{A}_{n}, which is given by 2​π​Rn−=4​πln⁡(n)2\pi R_{n}^{-}=\frac{4\pi}{\ln(n)}. For r>0r>0, let 𝔻r⊂ℂ\mathbb{D}_{r}\subset\mathbb{C} denote the disk of radius rr centered at the origin. Fix a smooth radially symmetric function f:𝔻→ℝf:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} which is non-increasing in the radial direction, satisfies f⁡(0)=1f(0)=1, and is supported inside 𝔻110\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{10}}. For each nn, we set
fn:𝔻rn→ℝfn​(z)≔ln⁡(n)n2​f​(zrn).f_{n}:\mathbb{D}_{r_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad f_{n}(z)\coloneqq\frac{\ln(n)}{n^{2}}f(\frac{z}{r_{n}}).
For 1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n, let fnif_{n}^{i} be the function obtained by pushing fnf_{n} forward via the translation moving 𝔻rn\mathbb{D}_{r_{n}} to DniD_{n}^{i}. Fix a compactly supported smooth function g:(−12,12)→[0,∞)g:(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})\rightarrow[0,\infty) with integral ∫g⁡(t)​𝑑t=1\int g(t)dt=1 and set
gn​(t)≔ln⁡(n)​g​(ln⁡(n)​t).g_{n}(t)\coloneqq\ln(n)g(\ln(n)t).
Note that the support of gng_{n} is contained in (−12​ln⁡(n),12​ln⁡(n))(-\frac{1}{2\ln(n)},\frac{1}{2\ln(n)}) and that we have ∫gn​(t)​𝑑t=1\int g_{n}(t)dt=1. We regard each gng_{n} as a function gn:𝕋→[0,∞)g_{n}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow[0,\infty). Now define
Hk:𝕋×𝔻→ℝHk​(t,z)≔λ​∑n=10kgn​(t)​∑i=1nfni​(z)H_{k}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad H_{k}(t,z)\coloneqq\lambda\sum\limits_{n=10}^{k}g_{n}(t)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{n}^{i}(z)
and
H:𝕋×𝔻→ℝH⁡(t,z)≔limk→∞Hk​(t,z).H:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad H(t,z)\coloneqq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}H_{k}(t,z).
The limit is uniform, implying that HH is continuous. Each compact subset of 𝕋×𝔻\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{D} which does not contain the point (0,0)(0,0) intersects the support of gn​(t)​fni​(z)g_{n}(t)f_{n}^{i}(z) for only finitely many values of nn. Thus HH is smooth on the complement of the point (0,0)(0,0). The sequence of Hamiltonian isotopies (φHkt)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{H_{k}}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} converges in the C0C^{0} topology to an isotopy in Homeoc⁡(𝔻,ω)\operatorname{Homeo}_{c}(\mathbb{D},\omega), the space of compactly supported area preserving homeomorphisms of 𝔻\mathbb{D}. We abbreviate this isotopy by (φHt)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{H}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]}. By definition, this means that φH1\varphi_{H}^{1} is contained in the group of hameomorphisms Hameoc⁡(𝔻,ω)\operatorname{Hameo}_{c}(\mathbb{D},\omega). This group was introduced by Oh an Müller in [OM07], see also [CHMSS, Definition 2.2]. It will also be convenient to have an autonomous Hamiltonian H~\tilde{H} generating the same hameomorphism φH1\varphi_{H}^{1}. We set
H~k:𝔻→ℝH~k​(z)≔λ​∑n=10k∑i=1nfni​(z)\tilde{H}_{k}:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad\tilde{H}_{k}(z)\coloneqq\lambda\sum\limits_{n=10}^{k}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{n}^{i}(z)
and
H~:𝔻→ℝH~​(z)≔limk→∞H~k​(z).\tilde{H}:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad\tilde{H}(z)\coloneqq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{H}_{k}(z).
Then the Hamiltonian isotopies (φH~kt)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{\tilde{H}_{k}}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} converge in the C0C^{0} topology to an isotopy (φH~t)t∈[0,1](\varphi_{\tilde{H}}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]} in Homeoc⁡(𝔻,ω)\operatorname{Homeo}_{c}(\mathbb{D},\omega). Since φHk1=φH~k1\varphi_{H_{k}}^{1}=\varphi_{\tilde{H}_{k}}^{1} for all kk, we have φH1=φH~1\varphi_{H}^{1}=\varphi_{\tilde{H}}^{1}.
Lemma 11.1.
The Hamiltonians HH and H~\tilde{H} are contained in the Hölder space C1,αC^{1,\alpha} for every α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1), but not in C1,1C^{1,1}.
Proof.
For x≠y∈𝔻rnx\neq y\in\mathbb{D}_{r_{n}} and α∈(0,1]\alpha\in(0,1], we compute
|d​fn​(x)−d​fn​(y)||x−y|α\displaystyle\frac{|df_{n}(x)-df_{n}(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}
=ln⁡(n)n2⋅|1rn​d​f​(xrn)−1rn​d​f​(yrn)|rnα​|xrn−yrn|α\displaystyle=\frac{\ln(n)}{n^{2}}\cdot\frac{|\frac{1}{r_{n}}df(\frac{x}{r_{n}})-\frac{1}{r_{n}}df(\frac{y}{r_{n}})|}{r_{n}^{\alpha}|\frac{x}{r_{n}}-\frac{y}{r_{n}}|^{\alpha}}
=nα−1​ln⁡(n)3+2​α​|d​f​(xrn)−d​f​(yrn)||xrn−yrn|α.\displaystyle=n^{\alpha-1}\ln(n)^{3+2\alpha}\frac{|df(\frac{x}{r_{n}})-df(\frac{y}{r_{n}})|}{|\frac{x}{r_{n}}-\frac{y}{r_{n}}|^{\alpha}}.
This implies that
[d​fn]C0,α=nα−1​ln⁡(n)3+2​α​[d​f]C0,α,[df_{n}]_{C^{0,\alpha}}=n^{\alpha-1}\ln(n)^{3+2\alpha}[df]_{C^{0,\alpha}},
where [⋅]C0,α[\cdot]_{C^{0,\alpha}} denotes the C0,αC^{0,\alpha} seminorm. If α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1), then nα−1​ln⁡(n)3+2​αn^{\alpha-1}\ln(n)^{3+2\alpha} converges to zero as nn tends to infinity. Using this, we see that H~\tilde{H} is C1,αC^{1,\alpha} for α<1\alpha<1. If α=1\alpha=1, then nα−1​ln⁡(n)3+2​αn^{\alpha-1}\ln(n)^{3+2\alpha} diverges, which implies that H~\tilde{H} is not C1,1C^{1,1}.
The proof that HH is in C1,αC^{1,\alpha} for α<1\alpha<1 but not for α=1\alpha=1 is similar. A short computation shows that [d​gn]C0,α=ln⁡(n)2+α​[d​g]C0,α[dg_{n}]_{C^{0,\alpha}}=\ln(n)^{2+\alpha}[dg]_{C^{0,\alpha}}. Since logarithmic terms are dominated by negative powers of nn, we see that [d⁡(gn​fn)]C0,α[d(g_{n}f_{n})]_{C^{0,\alpha}} converges to zero for α<1\alpha<1 and diverges for α=1\alpha=1.
∎
Lemma 11.1 implies that the domain X⁡(H)X(H) is C1,αC^{1,\alpha} for all α<1\alpha<1, but not C1,1C^{1,1}. Since HH is smooth on the complement of a single point, the same is true for the domain X⁡(H)X(H). By choosing the parameter λ>0\lambda>0 small, we can make sure that X⁡(H)X(H) is arbitrarily C1C^{1} close to the unit ball in ℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}. Our goal is to show that the error terms in the Weyl law for the alternative ECH capacities (see Remark 1.2) of X⁡(H)X(H) diverge to −∞-\infty. More precisely, let us write
ckalt​(X⁡(H))=2​vol⁡(X⁡(H))​k+ekalt​(X⁡(H)).c_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H))=2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X(H))k}+e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)).
We will show:
Proposition 11.2.
We have limk→∞ekalt​(X⁡(H))=−∞\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H))=-\infty.
Remark 11.3.
It should also be the case that the error terms in the Weyl law for the usual ECH capacities of X⁡(H)X(H) diverge to −∞-\infty. The reason we formulate Proposition 11.2 for the alternative ECH capacities instead of the original ones is that we rely on certain inequalities between PFH spectral invariants and capacities from [EH]. These inequalities are stated for the alternative ECH capacities, but also expected to hold for the usual ones.
Remark 11.4.
The domain X⁡(H)X(H) is the limit of smooth domains X⁡(Hk)X(H_{k}). A direct computation shows that the Ruelle invariants Ru⁡(X⁡(Hk))\operatorname{Ru}(X(H_{k})) of these domains diverge to +∞+\infty. Therefore, Proposition 11.2 is expected in view of Hutchings’ conjecture, see equation (1.3).
Theorem C is an easy consequence of Theorem D, Remark 1.2, and Proposition 11.2. Indeed, let W⊂ℝ4W\subset\mathbb{R}^{4} be a compact domain with smooth boundary and assume by contradiction that pW​(X​(H))=1p^{W}(X(H))=1. After rescaling, we can assume that WW has the same volume as X⁡(H)X(H). The identity pW​(X​(H))=1p^{W}(X(H))=1 then implies that r​WrW admits a symplectic embedding into X⁡(H)X(H) for all r∈(0,1)r\in(0,1). By monotonicity of capacities, we obtain ckalt​(W)≤ckalt​(X⁡(H))c_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(W)\leq c_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)). Since vol⁡(W)=vol⁡(X⁡(H))\operatorname{vol}(W)=\operatorname{vol}(X(H)), we deduce that ekalt​(W)≤ekalt​(X⁡(H))e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(W)\leq e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)) for all kk. But ekalt​(W)=O⁡(1)e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(W)=O(1) by Theorem D and Remark 1.2, contradicting Proposition 11.2.
It remains to prove Proposition 11.2. We obtain the necessary estimates on the error terms ekalt​(X⁡(H))e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)) in a slightly roundabout way which involves both link spectral invariants and PFH spectral invariants. The reason for this detour is that while we do not know how to compute the (alternative) ECH capacities of X⁡(H)X(H), the link spectral invariants of the Hamiltonian HH are easy to compute for appropriate choices of links. PFH is used as an intermediary between link spectral invariants and alternative ECH capacities. We make use of the work of Chen [Chea, Cheb], which relates link spectral invariants and PFH spectral invariants. We also rely on methods from [EH] to relate PFH spectral invariants and alternative ECH capacities.
We begin by estimating the link spectral invariants of HH. Let (S2,ω)(S^{2},\omega) be the sphere of area A≔πA\coloneqq\pi obtained from 𝔻\mathbb{D} by collapsing the boundary circle to a point. Any compactly supported Hamiltonian GG on 𝔻\mathbb{D} descends to a Hamiltonian on S2S^{2}. In the following, we will use the same letter GG for this Hamiltonian. Recall from [CHMSS22] that a Lagrangian link L¯⊂S2\underline{L}\subset S^{2} is the disjoint union of finitely many smoothly embedded circles. We call a link L¯\underline{L} monotone if all components of S2∖L¯S^{2}\setminus\underline{L} have equal area. This area must be equal to Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}, where dd denotes the number of components of L¯\underline{L}. Given a monotone link L¯⊂S2\underline{L}\subset S^{2}, the paper [CHMSS22] introduces a spectral invariant
cL¯:C∞​([0,1]×S2)→ℝc_{\underline{L}}:C^{\infty}([0,1]\times S^{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
called link spectral invariant. The main properties of link spectral invariants are summarized in [CHMSS22, Theorem 1.1 & 1.13]. By the Hofer Lipschitz property, each cL¯c_{\underline{L}} is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the C0C^{0} norm on C∞​([0,1]×S2)C^{\infty}([0,1]\times S^{2}). This implies that cL¯c_{\underline{L}} admits a continuous extension to C0​([0,1]×S2)C^{0}([0,1]\times S^{2}). In particular, cL¯​(H)c_{\underline{L}}(H) is defined for every link L¯\underline{L}.
Lemma 11.5.
For each d≥1d\geq 1, let L¯d\underline{L}_{d} be a monotone dd-component link in (S2,ω)(S^{2},\omega). Then
(11.2)
d​cL¯d​(H)−d​A−1​∫[0,1]×S2H​𝑑t∧ω→−∞(d→+∞).dc_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)-dA^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times S^{2}}Hdt\wedge\omega\rightarrow-\infty\qquad(d\rightarrow+\infty).
Remark 11.6.
The factor A−1A^{-1} is not present in the Weyl law [CHMSS22, Theorem 1.1] because there the surface is normalized to have area 11.
Proof.
We claim that
(11.3)
|cL¯d​(H)−cL¯d′​(H)|≤2​Ad|c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)-c_{\underline{L}_{d}^{\prime}}(H)|\leq\frac{2A}{d}
for any two monotone dd-component links L¯d\underline{L}_{d} and L¯d′\underline{L}_{d}^{\prime}. This inequality implies that if (11.2) holds for one sequence of monotone links, then it holds for every other sequence of monotone links as well. This means that once we know inequality (11.3), we can reduce ourselves to verifying (11.2) for one single carefully constructed sequence of links. We prove inequality (11.3). Let L¯d\underline{L}_{d} be a monotone dd-component link. If G∈Cc∞​([0,1]×𝔻)G\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,1]\times\mathbb{D}) is a smooth Hamiltonian compactly supported in the interior of the disk, then cL¯d​(G)c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(G) only depends on the time-11 map φG1\varphi_{G}^{1} by the homotopy invariance property in [CHMSS22, Theorem 1.13]. The resulting map
fL¯d:Hamc⁡(𝔻)→ℝφG1↦cL¯d​(G)f_{\underline{L}_{d}}:\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(\mathbb{D})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad\varphi_{G}^{1}\mapsto c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(G)
is uniformly continuous with respect to the C0C^{0} topology on Hamc⁡(𝔻)\operatorname{Ham}_{c}(\mathbb{D}) and continuously extends to a map fL¯d:Homeoc⁡(𝔻)→ℝf_{\underline{L}_{d}}:\operatorname{Homeo}_{c}(\mathbb{D})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}, see [CHMSS22, Proposition 3.4]. We have
(11.4)
fL¯d​(φH1)=limk→∞fL¯d​(φHk1)=limk→∞cL¯d​(Hk)=cL¯d​(H).f_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\varphi_{H}^{1})=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}f_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\varphi_{H_{k}}^{1})=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H_{k})=c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H).
The map fL¯df_{\underline{L}_{d}} is a quasimorphism with defect at most Ad\frac{A}{d}, see [CHMSS22, Proof of Proposition 7.9]. It is proved in [CHMSS22, Theorem 7.7] that the homogenization
fd:Homeoc⁡(𝔻)→ℝfd​(φ)≔limn→∞fL¯d​(φn)nf_{d}:\operatorname{Homeo}_{c}(\mathbb{D})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad f_{d}(\varphi)\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{f_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\varphi^{n})}{n}
is independent of the monotone dd-component link L¯d\underline{L}_{d}. A simple computation using the fact that fL¯df_{\underline{L}_{d}} has defect at most Ad\frac{A}{d} shows that |fd−fL¯d|≤Ad|f_{d}-f_{\underline{L}_{d}}|\leq\frac{A}{d}. We deduce that |fL¯d−fL¯d′|≤2​Ad|f_{\underline{L}_{d}}-f_{\underline{L}_{d}^{\prime}}|\leq\frac{2A}{d} for any other monotone dd-component link L¯d′\underline{L}_{d}^{\prime}. In combination with (11.4), this implies inequality (11.3).
Note that since φH1=φH~1\varphi_{H}^{1}=\varphi_{\tilde{H}}^{1}, we have
cL¯d​(H)=fL¯d​(φH1)=fL¯d​(φH~1)=cL¯d​(H~).c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(H)=f_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\varphi_{H}^{1})=f_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\varphi_{\tilde{H}}^{1})=c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\tilde{H}).
Moreover,
∫[0,1]×S2H​𝑑t∧ω=∫[0,1]×S2H~​𝑑t∧ω.\int_{[0,1]\times S^{2}}Hdt\wedge\omega=\int_{[0,1]\times S^{2}}\tilde{H}dt\wedge\omega.
When checking (11.2), we may therefore replace HH by the autonomous Hamiltonian H~\tilde{H}.
It remains to construct a special sequence of monotone links L¯d\underline{L}_{d} for which we can show (11.2) to hold. Monotonicity means that each component of S2∖L¯dS^{2}\setminus\underline{L}_{d} must have area Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}. Let N=N⁡(d)>0N=N(d)>0 be the maximal positive integer such that the area of DNiD_{N}^{i} is at least Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}. For each of the disks DniD_{n}^{i} with n≤Nn\leq N, we place ⌊area⁡(Dni)/Ad+1⌋\lfloor\operatorname{area}(D_{n}^{i})/\frac{A}{d+1}\rfloor components of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} inside DniD_{n}^{i} such that they form a nested sequence of circles centered at the center of DniD_{n}^{i}. We adjust the radii of these circles such that the disk bounded by the innermost circle and the annuli bounded by two consecutive circles have area Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}. Note that since ff was chosen to be supported inside 𝔻110\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{10}}, the outermost circle component of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} in DniD_{n}^{i} is disjoint from the support of H~\tilde{H}.
We show that we can insert the remaining components of the link L¯d\underline{L}_{d} to be disjoint from the support of H~\tilde{H}. Let Σ\Sigma denote the surface obtained from S2S^{2} be removing, for every disk DniD_{n}^{i} with n≤Nn\leq N, the disk bounded by the outermost component of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} inside DniD_{n}^{i}. We place the remaining components of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} inside Σ\Sigma such that each of them bounds a topological disk DD of area Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}. As a first step, we arrange the components such that for each of the resulting disks DD, the total area of all disks DniD_{n}^{i} with n>Nn>N intersecting DD is at most 16​Ad+1\frac{16A}{d+1}. This is not particularly subtle. For example, it is enough to make sure that each of the topological disks DD is contained in a metric disk of area 4​Ad+1\frac{4A}{d+1}, where we use the standard metric on 𝔻\mathbb{D}. We can also make sure that each component of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} only intersects finitely many of the disks DniD_{n}^{i}.
Our second step is to move the link L¯d\underline{L}_{d} via a compactly supported Hamiltonian diffeomorphism of Σ\Sigma to make it disjoint from the support of H~\tilde{H}. For each disk DniD_{n}^{i}, let D~ni\tilde{D}_{n}^{i} be the disk with the same center as DniD_{n}^{i} and with radius rn10\frac{r_{n}}{10}. Note that H~\tilde{H} is supported in the closure of the union of all D~ni\tilde{D}_{n}^{i}. Moreover, note that for each component LL of L¯d\underline{L}_{d}, the total area of all disks D~ni\tilde{D}_{n}^{i} with n>Nn>N intersecting the disk bounded by LL is strictly less than Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}. We can therefore find a compactly supported Hamiltonian diffeomorphism ψ\psi of Σ\Sigma which moves every disk D~ni\tilde{D}_{n}^{i} with n>Nn>N intersecting L¯d\underline{L}_{d} into the interior of the disk bounded by some component LL of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} it intersects. Moreover, we can choose ψ\psi such that it does not move any disk D~ni\tilde{D}_{n}^{i} which does not intersect L¯d\underline{L}_{d}. After replacing L¯d\underline{L}_{d} by ψ−1​(L¯d)\psi^{-1}(\underline{L}_{d}), we can assume that L¯d\underline{L}_{d} is disjoint from the support of H~\tilde{H}.
We observe that each component of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} is contained in a level set of H~\tilde{H}. One can therefore easily compute cL¯d​(H~)c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\tilde{H}) via the Lagrangian control property in [CHMSS22, Theorem 1.13]. For each component LL of L¯d\underline{L}_{d}, let H~|L∈ℝ\tilde{H}|_{L}\in\mathbb{R} be the value H~\tilde{H} takes on LL. Then
(11.5)
cL¯d​(H~)=1d​∑L⊂L¯dH~|L.c_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\tilde{H})=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{L\subset\underline{L}_{d}}\tilde{H}|_{L}.
Let L¯d+\underline{L}_{d}^{+} be the collection of all components of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} which are contained in a disk DniD_{n}^{i} for n≤Nn\leq N. By construction of L¯d\underline{L}_{d}, only components L⊂L¯d+L\subset\underline{L}_{d}^{+} contribute to the sum (11.5).
Consider a disk DniD_{n}^{i} with n≤Nn\leq N. Let L1,…,LkL_{1},\dots,L_{k} denote the components of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} inside DniD_{n}^{i}. Suppose that they are ordered such that LiL_{i} is inside LjL_{j} for i<ji<j. We claim that
(11.6)
Ad+1​∑i=1kH~|Li≤∫DniH~​ω.\frac{A}{d+1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{H}|_{L_{i}}\leq\int_{D_{n}^{i}}\tilde{H}\omega.
Indeed, let GG be the function on DniD_{n}^{i} whose value on the disk bounded by L1L_{1} is equal to H~|L1\tilde{H}|_{L_{1}}, whose value on the annulus between LiL_{i} and Li+1L_{i+1} is equal to H~|Li+1\tilde{H}|_{L_{i+1}}, and which vanishes outside of LkL_{k}. Since the function ff was chosen to be non-increasing in the radial direction, we have G≤H~G\leq\tilde{H}. Moreover, the left hand side of (11.6) is simply the integral of GG over DniD_{n}^{i}. Thus identity (11.6) is an immediate consequence.
Let Dd+D_{d}^{+} denote the union of all disks DniD_{n}^{i} with n≤Nn\leq N and Dd−D_{d}^{-} the union of all disks DniD_{n}^{i} with n>Nn>N. Then we can estimate using (11.6):
d​cL¯d​(H~)−d​A−1​∫𝔻H~​ω\displaystyle dc_{\underline{L}_{d}}(\tilde{H})-dA^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{D}}\tilde{H}\omega
=∑L⊂L¯d+H~|L−d​A−1​∫Dd+H~​ω−d​A−1​∫Dd−H~​ω\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{L\subset\underline{L}_{d}^{+}}\tilde{H}|_{L}-dA^{-1}\int_{D_{d}^{+}}\tilde{H}\omega-dA^{-1}\int_{D_{d}^{-}}\tilde{H}\omega
≤(d+1)​A−1​∫Dd+H~​ω−d​A−1​∫Dd+H~​ω−d​A−1​∫Dd−H~​ω\displaystyle\leq(d+1)A^{-1}\int_{D_{d}^{+}}\tilde{H}\omega-dA^{-1}\int_{D_{d}^{+}}\tilde{H}\omega-dA^{-1}\int_{D_{d}^{-}}\tilde{H}\omega
≤A−1​∫𝔻H~​ω−d​A−1​∫Dd−H~​ω.\displaystyle\leq A^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{D}}\tilde{H}\omega-dA^{-1}\int_{D_{d}^{-}}\tilde{H}\omega.
Therefore, it suffices to show that d​∫Dd−H~​ωd\int_{D_{d}^{-}}\tilde{H}\omega diverges to +∞+\infty as dd tends to infinity. By construction of H~\tilde{H} we have
d​∫Dd−H~​ω\displaystyle d\int_{D_{d}^{-}}\tilde{H}\omega
=d​∑n>N⁡(d)n​∫𝔻rnfn​ω\displaystyle=d\sum\limits_{n>N(d)}n\int_{\mathbb{D}_{r_{n}}}f_{n}\omega
=d​∑n>N⁡(d)n​ln⁡(n)​rn2n2​∫𝔻f​ω\displaystyle=d\sum\limits_{n>N(d)}\frac{n\ln(n)r_{n}^{2}}{n^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}f\omega
=d​∑n>N⁡(d)1n3​ln⁡(n)3​∫𝔻f​ω.\displaystyle=d\sum\limits_{n>N(d)}\frac{1}{n^{3}\ln(n)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}f\omega.
We estimate
d​∑n>N⁡(d)1n3​ln⁡(n)3\displaystyle d\sum\limits_{n>N(d)}\frac{1}{n^{3}\ln(n)^{3}}
≥d​∫N⁡(d)+1∞1x3​ln⁡(x)3​𝑑x\displaystyle\geq d\int_{N(d)+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{x^{3}\ln(x)^{3}}dx
=d​∫ln⁡(N⁡(d)+1)∞u−3​e−2​u​𝑑u\displaystyle=d\int_{\ln(N(d)+1)}^{\infty}u^{-3}e^{-2u}du
=d​(N⁡(d)+1)−2​∫0∞1(v+ln⁡(N⁡(d)+1))3​e−2​v​𝑑v\displaystyle=d(N(d)+1)^{-2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(v+\ln(N(d)+1))^{3}}e^{-2v}dv
=d​(N⁡(d)+1)−2​ln⁡(N⁡(d)+1)−3​∫0∞1(1+vln⁡(N⁡(d)+1))3​e−2​v​𝑑v\displaystyle=d(N(d)+1)^{-2}\ln(N(d)+1)^{-3}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+\frac{v}{\ln(N(d)+1)})^{3}}e^{-2v}dv
≥C​d​N​(d)−2​ln⁡(N⁡(d))−3\displaystyle\geq CdN(d)^{-2}\ln(N(d))^{-3}
for some constant C>0C>0 which can be chosen uniformly among all sufficiently large dd. Recall that N⁡(d)N(d) is the maximal integer nn such that the area of DniD_{n}^{i} is at least Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}. This means that
π​N​(d)−2​ln⁡(N⁡(d))−4≥Ad+1.\pi N(d)^{-2}\ln(N(d))^{-4}\geq\frac{A}{d+1}.
We can therefore further estimate
d​N​(d)−2​ln⁡(N⁡(d))−3≥C​ln⁡(N⁡(d)).dN(d)^{-2}\ln(N(d))^{-3}\geq C\ln(N(d)).
Since N⁡(d)N(d) tends to infinity as dd goes to infinity, we conclude that d​∫Dd−H~​ωd\int_{D_{d}^{-}}\tilde{H}\omega diverges to +∞+\infty as dd goes to infinity.
∎
Our next step is to use Chen’s work [Chea, Cheb] relating PFH and link spectral invariants to obtain information on the subleading asymptotics of the PFH spectral invariants of HH. Recall from [EH] that for every area preserving map ϕ∈Diff⁡(S2,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{Diff}(S^{2},\omega), every reference cycle γ⊂Yϕ\gamma\subset Y_{\phi} in the mapping torus YϕY_{\phi} of ϕ\phi, and every non-zero PFH class σ∈H​P​(ϕ,γ)\sigma\in HP(\phi,\gamma), we have a spectral invariant
C∞​(Yϕ)→ℝG↦cσ​(ϕ,γ,G).C^{\infty}(Y_{\phi})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad G\mapsto c_{\sigma}(\phi,\gamma,G).
Similarly to the case of link spectral invariants, this map is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the C0C^{0} norm on C∞​(Yϕ)C^{\infty}(Y_{\phi}) and continuously extends to C0​(Yϕ)C^{0}(Y_{\phi}). Chen considers the special case ϕ=idS2\phi=\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}}. In this case, the mapping torus YϕY_{\phi} is simply given by 𝕋×S2\mathbb{T}\times S^{2}. For every d≥1d\geq 1, Chen considers the special reference cycle γd≔d⋅(𝕋×{∗})\gamma_{d}\coloneqq d\cdot(\mathbb{T}\times\left\{*\right\}), i.e. dd copies of the circle 𝕋×{∗}\mathbb{T}\times\left\{*\right\} for some point ∗∈S2*\in S^{2}. For each d≥1d\geq 1, he constructs a distinguished non-zero class ed∈H​P​(idS2,γd)e_{d}\in HP(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d}) called the “PFH unit”. Let L¯d⊂S2\underline{L}_{d}\subset S^{2} be a monotone dd-component link. Suppose that every component of L¯d\underline{L}_{d} bounds a disk in S2S^{2} of area Ad+1\frac{A}{d+1}. For such links L¯d\underline{L}_{d}, Chen shows that
(11.7)
ced​(idS2,γd,G)+∫γdG​𝑑t−A≤d​cL¯d​(G)≤ced​(idS2,γd,G)+∫γdG​𝑑tc_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},G)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}Gdt-A\leq dc_{\underline{L}_{d}}(G)\leq c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},G)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}Gdt
for every 11-periodic Hamiltonian GG on S2S^{2}.
Remark 11.7.
In (11.7) we follow the notations and conventions for PFH and link spectral invariants introduced in [EH] and [CHMSS22]. These differ slightly from the ones used by Chen. Most notably, Chen’s definition of link spectral invariants differs from the one used in [CHMSS22] by a factor dd. For this reason, the factor dd in front of the link spectral invariant in (11.7) does not show up in Chen’s work. Moreover, Chen normalizes surfaces to have area 11. Thus the summand −A-A becomes −1-1 in Chen’s work.
Lemma 11.8.
We have
ced​(idS2,γd,H)+∫γdH−d​A−1​∫[0,1]×S2H​𝑑t∧ω→−∞(d→∞).c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},H)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}H-dA^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times S^{2}}Hdt\wedge\omega\rightarrow-\infty\quad(d\rightarrow\infty).
Proof.
This is an immediate consequence of Lemma 11.5 and Chen’s inequalities (11.7).
∎
Consider ℝ×𝕋×S2\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times S^{2} equipped with the symplectic form d​s∧d​t+ωds\wedge dt+\omega. Let M≔D⁡(H+A)⊂ℝ×𝕋×S2M\coloneqq D(H+A)\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times S^{2} be the symplectic cobordism between the graph of H+AH+A and the graph of the Hamiltonian which is constant equal to 00. It follows from (11.1) that the volume of MM agrees with the volume of the disjoint union of X⁡(H)X(H) and B4​(A)B^{4}(A).
Lemma 11.9.
Every compact subset of the disjoint union of the interiors of X⁡(H)X(H) and B4​(A)B^{4}(A) admits a symplectic embedding into the interior of MM.
Remark 11.10.
Using the notation introduced in Theorem C, this statement is equivalent to the assertion that pX​(H)⊔B4​(A)​(M)=1p^{X(H)\sqcup B^{4}(A)}(M)=1.
Proof.
Note that the domain U⁡(H)U(H) showing up in the definition of X⁡(H)X(H) is contained in ℝ×𝕋×int⁡(𝔻)\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{D}) and can therefore be naturally regarded as a subset of ℝ×𝕋×S2\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times S^{2}. It is straightforward to check that translating U⁡(H)U(H) by AA along the ℝ\mathbb{R} factor of ℝ×𝕋×S2\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times S^{2} yields a domain contained in int⁡(M)\operatorname{int}(M). In other words, U⁡(H)U(H) symplectically embeds into int⁡(M)\operatorname{int}(M). The complement of the image of this symplectic embedding contains the set
W≔{(s,t,z)∈ℝ×𝕋×int(𝔻)∣z∈int(𝔻),0<s<A|z|2}.W\coloneqq\left\{(s,t,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\times\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{D})\mid z\in\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{D}),0<s<A|z|^{2}\right\}.
We claim that the interior of the ball B⁡(A)B(A) admits a symplectic embedding into WW. Indeed, let TT denote the triangle in the first quadrant of ℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2} with vertices (0,0)(0,0), (A,0)(A,0), and (0,A)(0,A). Let
Xint⁡(T)≔{(z1,z2)∈ℂ2∣π⁡(|z1|2,|z2|2)∈int⁡(T)}X_{\operatorname{int}(T)}\coloneqq\left\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid\pi(|z_{1}|^{2},|z_{2}|^{2})\in\operatorname{int}(T)\right\}
denote the toric domain associated to the interior of TT. It is well-known that the interior of B⁡(A)B(A) symplectically embeds into Xint⁡(T)X_{\operatorname{int}(T)}, see e.g. [Tra95]. The claim follows in view of the symplectomorphism
W→Xint⁡(T)(s,t,r​e2​π​i​θ)↦(sπ​e2​π​i​t,1−r2​e−2​π​i​θ).W\rightarrow X_{\operatorname{int}(T)}\qquad(s,t,re^{2\pi i\theta})\mapsto\left(\sqrt{\frac{s}{\pi}}e^{2\pi it},\sqrt{1-r^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\theta}\right).
It remains to show that every compact subset KK of the interior of X⁡(H)X(H) admits a symplectic embedding into U⁡(H)U(H). Let L⊂ℂ2L\subset\mathbb{C}^{2} denote the complex axis {z1=0}\left\{z_{1}=0\right\}. By construction, U⁡(H)U(H) is symplectomorphic to int⁡(X⁡(H))∖L\operatorname{int}(X(H))\setminus L. This means that if we can find a compactly supported symplectomorphism φ\varphi of int⁡(X⁡(H))\operatorname{int}(X(H)) which disjoins LL from KK, then KK symplectically embeds into U⁡(H)U(H). One can construct φ\varphi as follows: Consider the 33-dimensional half space Q≔ℝ≥0×ℂ⊂ℂ2Q\coloneqq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}. The boundary of QQ is precisely LL and the intersection of QQ with X⁡(H)X(H) is of the form {(g⁡(z),z)∣z∈𝔻}\left\{(g(z),z)\mid z\in\mathbb{D}\right\} for a suitable function g:𝔻→ℝ≥0g:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}. The leaves of the characteristic foliation on QQ induced by the symplectic form are rays of the form ℝ≥0×{∗}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\left\{*\right\}. Now consider an isotopy of LL supported inside int⁡(X⁡(H))\operatorname{int}(X(H)) which pushes LL along the characteristic rays on QQ towards the boundary of X⁡(H)X(H). Any such isotopy can be realized by a Hamiltonian flow compactly supported in the interior of X⁡(H)X(H) and it is clearly possible to disjoin LL from KK via such an isotopy. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎
We are finally in a position to prove Proposition 11.2, i.e. that the error terms ekalt​(X⁡(H))e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)) diverge to −∞-\infty as kk tends to ∞\infty.
Proof of Proposition 11.2.
It follows from Lemma 11.9 and [EH, Lemma 5.2] that
(11.8)
ced​(idS2,γd,H+A)−cUk​ed​(idS2,γd,0)+∫γd(H+A)​𝑑t≥ckalt​(X⁡(H)⊔B⁡(A))c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},H+A)-c_{U^{k}e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},0)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}(H+A)dt\geq c_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)\sqcup B(A))
for all kk. Set V≔vol⁡(M)=∫[0,1]×S2(H+A)​𝑑t∧ωV\coloneqq\operatorname{vol}(M)=\int_{[0,1]\times S^{2}}(H+A)dt\wedge\omega. Moreover, abbreviate
nd≔⌊d2​VA2​(d+1)⌋andkd≔nd​(d+1).n_{d}\coloneqq\left\lfloor\frac{d^{2}V}{A^{2}(d+1)}\right\rfloor\qquad\text{and}\qquad k_{d}\coloneqq n_{d}(d+1).
Since our underlying surface is the sphere S2S^{2}, every PFH class σ\sigma is UU-cyclic of order 11. Combining inequality (11.8) with [EH, Proposition 4.2(a)], we see that
ced​(idS2,γd,H+A)−ced​(idS2,γd,0)+∫γd(H+A)​𝑑t≥ckdalt​(X⁡(H)⊔B⁡(A))−nd​A.c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},H+A)-c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},0)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}(H+A)dt\geq c_{k_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)\sqcup B(A))-n_{d}A.
Set
id≔⌊vol⁡(X⁡(H))​kdV⌋andjd≔kd−id.i_{d}\coloneqq\left\lfloor\frac{\operatorname{vol}(X(H))k_{d}}{V}\right\rfloor\qquad\text{and}\qquad j_{d}\coloneqq k_{d}-i_{d}.
By the disjoint union property for the capacities ckaltc_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}, we have
ckdalt​(X⁡(H)⊔B⁡(A))≥cidalt​(X⁡(H))+cjdalt​(B⁡(A))=2​vol⁡(X⁡(H))​id+2​vol⁡(B⁡(A))​jd+eidalt​(X⁡(H))+O⁡(1)c_{k_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)\sqcup B(A))\geq c_{i_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H))+c_{j_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(B(A))=2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X(H))i_{d}}+2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(B(A))j_{d}}+e_{i_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H))+O(1)
An elementary computation shows that
2​vol⁡(X⁡(H))​id+2​vol⁡(B⁡(A))​jd−nd​A=d​A−1​V+O⁡(1).2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X(H))i_{d}}+2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(B(A))j_{d}}-n_{d}A=dA^{-1}V+O(1).
We deduce that
(11.9)
ced​(idS2,γd,H+A)−ced​(idS2,γd,0)+∫γd(H+A)​𝑑t−d​A−1​V≥eidalt​(X⁡(H))+O⁡(1).c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},H+A)-c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},0)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}(H+A)dt-dA^{-1}V\geq e_{i_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H))+O(1).
Using [EH, Remark 4.4] and Lemma 11.8, we see that
ced​(idS2,γd,H+A)+∫γd(H+A)​𝑑t−d​A−1​V=ced(idS2,γd,H)+∫γdHdt−dA−1∫[0,1]×S2Hdt∧ω⟶−∞(d→∞).c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},H+A)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}(H+A)dt-dA^{-1}V\\
=c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},H)+\int_{\gamma_{d}}Hdt-dA^{-1}\int_{[0,1]\times S^{2}}Hdt\wedge\omega\quad\longrightarrow\quad-\infty\qquad(d\rightarrow\infty).
Moreover, as already observed in the proof of Theorem F, the spectral invariant ced​(idS2,γd,0)c_{e_{d}}(\operatorname{id}_{S^{2}},\gamma_{d},0) vanishes. Combining these observations with inequality (11.9), we see that eidalt​(X⁡(H))e_{i_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)) tends to −∞-\infty as dd goes to infinity. From this one can further deduce that the entire sequence of error terms ekalt​(X⁡(H))e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)) diverges to −∞-\infty. Indeed, since the sequence ckalt​(X⁡(H))c_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)) is non-decreasing in kk, we have
ekalt​(X⁡(H))−eℓalt​(X⁡(H))≤2​vol⁡(X⁡(H))​(k−ℓ)for all k≥ℓ.e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H))-e_{\ell}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H))\leq 2\sqrt{\operatorname{vol}(X(H))}(\sqrt{k}-\sqrt{\ell})\qquad\text{for all $k\geq\ell$}.
Since idi_{d} grows quadratically in dd, this estimate is enough to control all error terms ekalt​(X⁡(H))e_{k}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)) just in terms of the subsequence eidalt​(X⁡(H))e_{i_{d}}^{\operatorname{alt}}(X(H)).
∎
